\pi_ {1} \colon \lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, y) \dot {\to} \lambda (x, y). x
$$

$$
\pi_ {2}: \lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, y) \dot {\to} \lambda (x, y). y
$$

if there is no ambiguity, we may write them by listing their components as follows:

$$
\pi_ {1 _ {a, b}} \colon \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b) \to a
$$

$$
\pi_ {2 a, b} \colon \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b) \to b
$$

we might even omit subscripts from  $\pi_{1a,b}$  and  $\pi_{2a,b}$ . the exponential functor 'exp' is associated with one natural transformation which gives evaluation morphisms.

$$
\operatorname {e v}: \lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (\exp (x, y), x) \stackrel {{\cdot}} {{\to}} \lambda (x, y). y
$$

note that the variance of these two functors is  $\top +$ . therefore,  $\delta$  is

$$
\delta_ {\lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, y), \lambda (x, y). x} = \left\{\pi_ {1} \right\}
$$

$$
\delta_ {\lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, y), \lambda (x, y). y} = \left\{\pi_ {2} \right\}
$$

$$
\delta_ {\lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (\exp (x, y), x), \lambda (x, y). y} = \{\mathrm {e v} \}
$$

functors  $(\gamma)$  1  $\operatorname{prod}(+, + )$ $\exp(-, + )$

natural transformations  $(\delta)$ $\pi_1\colon \operatorname {prod}(a,b)\to a$

$$
\pi_ {2} \colon \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b) \to b
$$

$$
\operatorname {e v}: \operatorname {p r o d} (\exp (a, b), a) \rightarrow b
$$

factorizers  $(\psi)$

$$
f \colon c \to a \qquad g \colon c \to b
$$

$$
\operatorname {p a i r} (f, g) \colon c \to \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b)
$$

$$
\begin{array}{c} h \colon \operatorname {p r o d} (c, a) \to b \\ \hline \operatorname {c u r r y} (h) \colon c \to \exp (a, b) \end{array}
$$

figure 2.1: csl signature for cartesian closed categories

for any other combinations of closed functorial expression  $k$  and  $l$ ,  $\delta_{k,l}$  is empty.

finally, there are three factorizers for the three functors: '!' for '1', 'pair' for 'prod' and 'curry' for 'exp'.

$$
\overline {{: : \lambda (x) . x \rightarrow \lambda (x) . 1}}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \begin{array}{c} f \colon \lambda (x, y, z). z \to \lambda (x, y, z). x \qquad g \colon \lambda (x, y, z). z \to \lambda (x, y, z). y \\ \hline \operatorname {p a i r} (f, g) \colon \lambda (x, y, z). z \to \lambda (x, y, z). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, y) \end{array} \\ \begin{array}{c} h \colon \lambda (x, y, z). \operatorname {p r o d} (z, x) \to \lambda (x, y, z). y \\ \hline \operatorname {c u r r y} (h) \colon \lambda (x, y, z). z \to \lambda (x, y, z). \exp (x, y) \end{array} \\ \end{array}
$$

if there is no ambiguity, we might write these rules down as in figure 2.1, where we summarize the definition of the csl signature for cartesian closed categories. we will omit the tedious formal definition of  $\psi$  as an indexed set.

as we mentioned earlier, a csl signature is one sorted, but because it handles higher objects, it is no less powerful than a many sorted equational signature. let us demonstrate this. an equational signature is given by a pair  $\langle s, \sigma \rangle$  where  $s$  is a set (of sort names) and  $\sigma$  is an  $s^* \times s$ -indexed set (of operator names). we will translate it to a corresponding csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ . since functors play a role of sorts, we declare a constant functor for each sort in  $s$ .

$$
\gamma_ {()} = s,
$$

and we have a binary product functor to deal with sequences of sorts, and the

terminal object for constants.

$$
\gamma_ {+ +} = \{\mathrm {p r o d} \} \qquad \mathrm {a n d} \qquad \gamma_ {()} = \{1 \}
$$

operations will be translated to natural transformations: for each operation  $o \in \sigma_{s_1 \ldots s_n, s}$ , we have a natural transformation of the same name.

$$
o \colon \operatorname {p r o d} (s _ {1}, \operatorname {p r o d} (\dots , \operatorname {p r o d} (s _ {n - 1}, s _ {n}))) \to s
$$

that is,

$$
\delta_ {\lambda (), \mathrm {p r o d} (s _ {1}, \mathrm {p r o d} (\dots , \mathrm {p r o d} (s _ {n - 1}, s _ {n}))), \lambda (), s} = \sigma_ {s _ {1} \dots s _ {n}, s}
$$

we also have two projections for 'prod' in  $\delta$  and  $\psi$  has only one factorizer 'pair' for pairing. it is easy to see that the csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  corresponds to the equational signature  $\langle s, \sigma \rangle$ .

proposition 2.2.3: a many-sorted equational signature can be represented by a csl signature.

it is interesting to know that we represented a many-sorted equational signature by a category with products because an algebraic theory can exactly be given as a category with products [lawvere 63].

let us make csl signatures form a category by extending a pre-csl signature morphism in a natural way. intuitively, a signature morphism (not only in csl but in general) does some renamings of symbols and/or some mergings of symbols.

definition 2.2.4: a csl signature morphism  $\sigma$  from a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  to a csl signature  $\langle \gamma', \delta', \psi' \rangle$  is a triple  $\langle \sigma', \sigma'', \sigma''' \rangle$  consisting of

1. a  $\mathbf{var}^*$ -indexed function  $\sigma_{v_1\ldots v_n}' : \gamma_{v_1\ldots v_n} \to \gamma_{v_1\ldots v_n}'$  for mapping functor names,  
2. a  $\lambda \times \lambda$ -indexed function (where  $\lambda = \mathbf{cfe}(\gamma)$ )  $\sigma_{k,l}^{\prime \prime}\colon \delta_{k,l}\to \delta_{\sigma^{\prime}k,\sigma^{\prime}l}^{\prime}$  for mapping natural transformation names, and  
3. a  $(\lambda \times \lambda)^{*}\times (\lambda \times \lambda)$  -indexed function

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {\langle \langle k _ {1}, l _ {1} \rangle \dots \langle k _ {m}, l _ {m} \rangle , \langle k, l \rangle} ^ {\prime \prime \prime}: \\ \psi_ {\langle \langle k _ {1}, l _ {1} \rangle \dots \langle k _ {m}, l _ {m} \rangle , \langle k, l \rangle \rangle} \rightarrow \psi_ {\langle \langle \sigma^ {\prime} k _ {1}, \sigma^ {\prime} l _ {1} \rangle \dots \langle \sigma^ {\prime} k _ {m}, \sigma^ {\prime} l _ {m} \rangle , \langle \sigma^ {\prime} k, \sigma^ {\prime} l \rangle \rangle} ^ {\prime} \\ \end{array}
$$

for mapping factorizer names.

we often write  $\sigma f$  for  $\sigma_{v_1\dots v_n}'(f)$ ,  $\sigma \alpha$  for  $\sigma_{k,l}''(\alpha)$  and  $\sigma \psi$  for  $\sigma_{\langle \langle k_1,l_1\rangle \dots ,\langle k,l\rangle \rangle}^{\prime \prime \prime \prime}(\psi)$ .

definition 2.2.5: the category of csl signatures csig has csl signatures as its objects and csl signature morphisms as its morphisms. the identity morphism on a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  consists of the corresponding identity functions for the components and the composition of csl morphisms is given by combining the component-wise compositions as indexed functions. this clearly forms a category.

# 2.3 structures of categorical specification language

in this section, we will define csl structures. a csl signature specifies symbols for functors, natural transformations and factorizers, so intuitively, a csl structure is a category associated with these functors, natural transformations and factorizers.

definition 2.3.1: given a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ , a csl structure  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$  is a small category  $\mathcal{c}$  together with an assignment  $\xi$

1.  $\xi$  assigns each functor name of variety  $v_{1}\ldots v_{n}$  to a functor  $\mathcal{c}^{v_1}\times \dots \times \mathcal{c}^{v_n}\to$ $\mathcal{c}$ . as we have seen in section 2.1,  $\xi$  can be extended to the assignment of closed functorial expressions to functors.  
2.  $\xi$  assigns each natural transformation name  $\alpha \in \delta_{k,l}$ , where  $k$  and  $l$  are closed functorial expressions of  $n$  variables, to a set of  $\mathcal{c}$  morphisms

$$
\xi \alpha_ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}} \colon \xi k (a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}) \rightarrow \xi l (a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n})
$$

for arbitrary  $\mathcal{c}$  objects  $a_{1},\ldots ,a_{n}$ .

3. each factorizer symbol  $\psi \in \psi_{\langle \langle k_1,l_1\rangle \ldots ,\langle k,l\rangle \rangle}$ , where  $k_{1},l_{1},\ldots ,k_{m},l_{m},$ $k,l$  are closed functorial expressions of  $n$  variables, is assigned to a set of set functions

$$
\begin{array}{l} \xi \psi_ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}}: \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} \left(\xi k _ {i} \left(a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}\right), \xi l _ {i} \left(a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}\right)\right) \\ \rightarrow \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (\xi k (a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}), \xi l (a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n})) \\ \end{array}
$$

for arbitrary  $\mathcal{c}$  objects  $a_{1},\ldots ,a_{n}$ .

note that we do not assign a natural transformation symbol to a natural transformation, but it is mapped to a set of morphisms and whether they form a natural transformation or not is left to be stated by equations.

$$
\xi_ {v _ {1} \dots v _ {n}}: \gamma_ {v _ {1} \dots v _ {n}} \to \mathbf {f u n c t} \left(\mathcal {c} ^ {v _ {1}} \times \dots \times \mathcal {c} ^ {v _ {n}}, \mathcal {c}\right).
$$

$$
\xi_ {k, l} \colon \delta_ {k, l} \to \mathbf {n a t} (\xi k | ^ {\top \dots \top}, \xi l | ^ {\top \dots \top}).
$$

example 2.3.2: let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  be the csl signature for cartesian closed categories defined in example 2.2.2. then, any cartesian closed category is a csl structure of this signature by obvious assignment of the symbols to the functors, natural transformations and factorizers. however, the converse is not true. we can have a category which has three functors, three natural-transformation-look-alikes and three factorizer-look-alikes. csl structures do not require mapping natural transformation symbols to natural transformations but only to a set of morphisms, nor do they require mapping factorizer symbols to factorizers. the factorizer-look-alikes may not give unique morphisms or may not commute some diagrams.

csl structure morphisms are defined simply as a kind of homomorphisms. they keep the structure nicely.

definition 2.3.3: given a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ , a csl structure morphism from a csl structure  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$  to another  $\langle \mathcal{d}, \zeta \rangle$  is a covariant functor  $t: \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{d}$  such that

1. for any  $f$  in  $\gamma_{v_1\dots v_n}$

$$
t \circ \xi f = \zeta f \circ \left(t ^ {v _ {1}} \times \dots \times t ^ {v _ {n}}\right)
$$

holds,

$$
\begin{array}{c} \mathcal {c} ^ {v _ {1}} \times \dots \times \mathcal {c} ^ {v _ {n}} \xrightarrow {\xi f} \mathcal {c} \\ t ^ {v _ {1}} \times \dots \times t ^ {v _ {n}} \bigg | \quad \bigcirc \\ \mathcal {d} ^ {v _ {1}} \times \dots \times \mathcal {d} ^ {v _ {n}} \xrightarrow [ \zeta f ]{} \mathcal {d} \end{array}
$$

2. for any  $\alpha \in \delta_{k,l}$  and for any  $\mathcal{c}$  objects  $a_1,\ldots ,a_n$

$$
t (\xi \alpha_ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}}) = \zeta \alpha_ {t (a _ {1}), \dots , t (a _ {n})},
$$

and

3. for any  $\psi \in \psi_{\langle k_1,l_1\rangle \ldots \langle k_m,l_m\rangle ,\langle k,l\rangle$  , for any  $\mathcal{c}$  objects  $a_{1},\dots ,a_{n}$  and for any  $\mathcal{c}$  morphisms  $f_{i}\colon \xi k_{i}(a_{1},\ldots ,a_{n})\to \xi l_{i}(a_{1},\ldots ,a_{n})$ $(i = 1,\dots ,m)$

$$
t (\xi \psi_ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}} (f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m})) = \zeta \psi_ {t (a _ {1}), \dots , t (a _ {n})} (t (f _ {1}), \dots , t (f _ {m})). [ ]
$$

hence, the category of csl structures is:

definition 2.3.4: for a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ , the category of csl structures,  $\mathbf{cmod}(\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle)$ , has csl structures as objects and csl structure morphisms as morphisms; the identity morphism on  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$  is the identity functor  $\mathbf{i}_{\mathcal{c}}$  and the composition of morphisms is the composition of their underlying functors.

# 2.4 functorial calculus (revisit)

in section 2.1 we saw functorial expressions denote functors. in this section we will see an expression involving natural transformation symbols and factorizer symbols denote a set of morphisms. for example, under the signature of cartesian closed categories given in example 2.2.2, what should the following expression denote?

$$
\pi_ {1} \circ \operatorname {p r o d} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\pi_ {2}), \mathbf {i})
$$

we even have a problem for expressions like  $\pi_1\circ \pi_2$  because  $\pi_{1}$  and  $\pi_{2}$  are not projections of the same product:  $\pi_{2}$  is of  $\mathrm{prod}(a,\mathrm{prod}(b,c))$  and  $\pi_{1}$  is of  $\mathrm{prod}(b,c)$ . actually, natural transformations are polymorphic like ml functions are.7 as we defined in definition 2.3.1,  $\pi_{1}$  denotes a set of morphisms.

$$
\xi \pi_ {1 _ {a, b}} \colon \operatorname {x p r o d} (a, b) \to a
$$

we have to figure out for each occurrence of  $\pi_1$  what  $a$  and  $b$  are.

definition 2.4.1: for a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ , we have a set  $\mathbf{exp}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  of csl expressions defined by the following bnf.

$$
e: := \mathbf {i} | e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} | \alpha | \psi (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) | f (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {n}) | f
$$

where  $\alpha \in \delta$ ,  $\psi \in \psi$  and  $f$  is a variable for morphisms. we also have a set  $\mathbf{aexp}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  of csl annotated expressions defined by the following bnf.

$$
\begin{array}{c} e: := \mathbf {i} [ k ] \mid e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} \mid \alpha [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] \mid \psi [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) \\ f (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {n}) \mid f \end{array}
$$

where  $k_{1},\ldots ,k_{n}$  are closed functorial expressions over  $\gamma$  .

it is trivial to see that for each annotated expression there is a corresponding expression (i.e. forgetting all the annotations, [...]), which we call skeleton of the annotated expression. we are going to type-check an expression first and, then, we determine its denotation. annotated expressions are used to remember

the type-checking information inside expressions. we will give the typing rules for annotated expressions and show that every expression has the most general annotated expression and we take the type of this annotated expression as the type of the expression.

first, we define the notion of unification.

definition 2.4.2: a closed functorial expression  $k$  of  $n$  variables is said to be more general than a closed functorial expression  $k'$  of  $m$  variables if there are closed functorial expressions  $k_1, \ldots, k_n$  of  $m$  variables such that

$$
k [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] \equiv k ^ {\prime}
$$

where  $\equiv$  is the equivalence relation ignoring variable renaming. (trivially,  $k\equiv k^{\prime}$  implies  $\xi k = \xi k^{\prime}.$ )

definition 2.4.3: closed functorial expressions  $k$  and  $l$  are said to be unifiable when there is a closed functorial expression  $k'$  such that  $k'$  is less general than  $k$  as well as  $l$ .

proposition 2.4.4: if closed functorial expressions  $k$  and  $l$  are unifiable, then there is a most general unification, that is, there exist  $k_{1},\ldots ,k_{n}$  and  $l_{1},\ldots ,l_{m}$  such that  $k[k_1,\dots,k_n]\equiv l[l_1,\dots,l_m]$  and for any  $k^{\prime}$  which is less general than  $k$  and  $k^{\prime}$  there are  $k_1^\prime ,\ldots ,k_l^\prime$  such that  $k^{\prime}\equiv k[k_1,\ldots ,k_n][k_1^{\prime},\ldots ,k_l^{\prime}]$ . proof: same as ordinary unification of terms.

let us now define the type of annotated expressions.

definition 2.4.5: let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  be a csl signature. an annotated expression  $e$  has a type  $\rho \vdash e\colon k \to l$  when it can be derived from the following rules, where  $\rho$  is a given assignment of each morphism variable to its type and  $k$  and  $l$  are closed functorial expressions.

1. for the identity,  $\rho \vdash \mathbf{i}[k]\colon k\to k$  
2. for the composition,

$$
\frac {\rho \vdash e _ {1} : k ^ {\prime} \to l \qquad \rho \vdash e _ {2} : k \to k ^ {\prime}}{\rho \vdash e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} : k \to l}.
$$

3. for a natural transformation  $\alpha \in \delta_{k,l}$ , where  $k$  and  $l$  are of  $n$  variables,

$$
\rho \vdash \alpha [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n} ] \colon k [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n} ] \rightarrow l [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n} ]
$$

4. for a factorizer  $\psi \in \psi_{\langle \langle k_1,l_1\rangle \ldots \langle k_m,l_m\rangle ,\langle k,l\rangle \rangle}$ , where  $k_{i},l_{i},k,l$  are of  $n$  variables,

$$
\frac {\rho \vdash e _ {i} \colon k _ {i} [ k _ {1} ^ {\prime} , \ldots , k _ {n} ^ {\prime} ] \rightarrow l _ {i} [ k _ {1} ^ {\prime} , \ldots , k _ {n} ^ {\prime} ]}{\rho \vdash \psi [ k _ {1} ^ {\prime} , \ldots , k _ {n} ^ {\prime} ] (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {m}) \colon k [ k _ {1} ^ {\prime} , \ldots , k _ {n} ^ {\prime} ] \rightarrow l [ k _ {1} ^ {\prime} , \ldots , k _ {n} ^ {\prime} ]}
$$

5. for a functor  $f\in \gamma_{v_1\dots v_n}$

$$
\frac {\rho \vdash e _ {i} : k _ {i} \xrightarrow {v _ {i}} l _ {i}}{\rho \vdash f (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {n}) : f [ k _ {1} , \ldots , k _ {n} ] \to f [ l _ {1} , \ldots , l _ {n} ]}
$$

where  $e_i \colon k_i \xrightarrow{v_i} l_i$  is  $e_i \colon k_i \to l_i$  if  $v_i$  is + or  $\bot$ ,  $e_i \colon l_i \to k_i$  if  $v_i$  is - and  $\mathbf{i} \colon k_i \to k_i$  if  $v_i$  is  $\top$ .

6. for a morphism variable  $f$ ,  $\rho \vdash f \colon \rho(f)$ .

definition 2.4.6: we say an annotated expression  $e$  is more general than  $e'$  if there exist closed functorial expressions  $k_{1},\ldots ,k_{n}$  such that  $e^{\prime}\equiv e[k_{1},\ldots ,k_{n}]$ , where  $e[k_1,\dots,k_n]$  is  $e$  with all its annotations being composed with  $k_{1},\ldots ,k_{n}$ , e.g.  $\alpha [l_1,\ldots ,l_m][k_1,\ldots ,k_n]$  is  $\alpha [l_1[k_1,\ldots ,k_n],\ldots ,l_m[k_1,\ldots ,k_n]]$ .

proposition 2.4.7: if an annotated expression  $e$  has a type  $\rho \vdash e: k \to l$ ,  $e[k_1, \ldots, k_n]$  has the following type.

$$
\rho [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] \vdash e [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] \colon k [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] \to l [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ]
$$

where  $\rho [k_1,\ldots ,k_n](f)$  is  $k^{\prime}[k_{1},\dots ,k_{n}]\to l^{\prime}[k_{1},\dots ,k_{n}]$  when  $\rho (f)$  is  $k^{\prime}\rightarrow$ $l^{\prime}$

proof: it can easily proved from definition 2.4.5 by structural induction on  $e$ .

proposition 2.4.8: let  $e \in \mathrm{exp}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  be an expression of a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ . if there exists an annotated expression  $e'$  whose skeleton is  $e$  and if it has a type, then there exists a most general annotated expression which has a type and whose skeleton is  $e$ .

proof: it can be proved by structural induction on  $e$ . here, we present an algorithm of calculating a most general annotated expression.

1. if  $e$  is  $\mathbf{i}$ , the most general annotated expression is  $\mathbf{i}[i]$ , where  $i$  is the identity closed functorial expression  $\lambda(x).x$ .  
2. if  $e$  is  $e_1 \circ e_2$ , from induction hypothesis, we have the most general annotated expressions for  $e_1$  and  $e_2$ .

$$
\rho_ {1} \vdash e _ {1} ^ {\prime}: k \to k ^ {\prime} \qquad \rho_ {2} \vdash e _ {2} ^ {\prime}: l \to l ^ {\prime}
$$

we unify  $k$  with  $l'$  achieving  $k[k_1, \ldots, k_n] \equiv l'[l_1, \ldots, l_m]$ . the most general annotated expression for  $e$  and its type is

$$
\rho \vdash e _ {1} ^ {\prime} [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] \circ e _ {2} ^ {\prime} [ l _ {1}, \ldots , l _ {m} ] \colon l [ l _ {1}, \ldots , l _ {m} ] \to k ^ {\prime} [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ]
$$

where  $\rho$  is the result of combining  $\rho_{1}[k_{1},\ldots ,k_{n}]$  and  $\rho_{2}[l_{1},\ldots ,l_{m}]$ .

3. if  $e$  is a natural transformation  $\alpha \in \delta_{k,l}$ , the most general annotated expression and its type is

$$
\rho \vdash \alpha [ \pi_ {1} ^ {n}, \dots , \pi_ {n} ^ {n} ]: k \to l,
$$

where  $\pi_i^n$  is the closed functorial expression  $\lambda(x_1, \ldots, x_n).x_i$ .

4. if  $e$  is  $\psi(e_1, \ldots, e_m)$  for a factorizer  $\psi \in \psi_{\langle \langle k_1, l_1 \rangle \ldots, \langle k, l \rangle \rangle}$ , from induction hypothesis, we have annotated expressions  $e_i'$  such that

$$
\rho_ {i} \vdash e _ {i} ^ {\prime}: k _ {i} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow l _ {i} ^ {\prime}.
$$

we unify  $k_{i}^{\prime}$  with  $k_{i}$  and  $l_{i}^{\prime}$  with  $l_{i}$ . if the unification is successful, we have the most general annotated expression for  $e$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \rho \vdash \psi [ j _ {1}, \dots , j _ {n} ] (e _ {1} ^ {\prime} [ j _ {1 1} ^ {\prime}, \dots ], \dots , e _ {m} ^ {\prime} [ j _ {1 m} ^ {\prime}, \dots ]) \colon \\ k [ j _ {1}, \ldots , j _ {n} ] \rightarrow l [ j _ {1}, \ldots , j _ {n} ] \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $j_{1},\ldots ,j_{n},j_{11}^{\prime},\ldots$  are the substitution for  $k_{i}$  and  $l_{i}$  obtained from the unification and  $\rho$  is the result of combining  $\rho_{i}[j_{1i}^{\prime},\dots ]$

5. if  $e$  is  $f(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ , let the most general annotated expressions for  $e_i$  be

$$
\rho_ {i} \vdash e _ {i} ^ {\prime}: k _ {i} \xrightarrow {v _ {i}} l _ {i}.
$$

then, the most general annotated expression for  $e$  and its type is

$$
\rho \vdash f (e _ {1} ^ {\prime}, \ldots , e _ {n} ^ {\prime}) \colon f [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] \to f [ l _ {1}, \ldots , l _ {n} ].
$$

6. if  $e$  is a morphism variable  $f$ , its most general annotated expression is itself and has the following type.

$$
\rho \vdash f \colon \lambda (x, y). x \rightarrow \lambda (x, y). y \quad \mathbb {i}
$$

from this proposition,

definition 2.4.9: we define the type of an expression  $e \in \mathbf{exp}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  to be the type of the most general annotated expression  $e'$  whose skeleton is  $e$ .

let us finally define the denotation of expressions in  $\mathbf{exp}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  when a csl structure  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$  is given. since each expression  $e$  is associated uniquely to the most general annotated expression  $e'$  by proposition 2.4.8, we can define the denotation of  $e$  to be that of  $e'$  and define the denotation of annotated expressions.

definition 2.4.10: let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  be a csl signature and  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$  be a csl structure. for an annotated expression  $e \in \mathbf{aexp}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  of type  $\rho \vdash e: k \to l$  (where  $k$  and  $l$  are of  $l$  variables), we define its denotation,  $\xi e$ , to be a set of morphisms

$$
\left(\xi e\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}}: \xi k \left(a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}\right)\rightarrow \xi l \left(a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}\right)
$$

for any  $\mathcal{c}$  objects  $a_{1},\ldots ,a_{l}$  and for any morphism variable assignment  $\omega$  (where  $\omega (f,a_1,\dots ,a_l)$  gives a morphism of type  $\xi k^{\prime}(a_1,\ldots ,a_l)\to \xi l^{\prime}(a_1,\ldots ,a_l)$  when  $\rho (f)$  is  $k^{\prime}\rightarrow l^{\prime}$ ).

1. for the identity,

$$
\left(\xi \mathbf {i} [ k ]\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \mathbf {i} _ {\xi k [ a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l} ]}
$$

2. for compositions,

$$
\left(\xi e _ {1} \circ e _ {2}\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}} \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \left(\xi e _ {1}\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}} \circ \left(\xi e _ {2}\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}}
$$

3. for natural transformations,

$$
\left(\xi \alpha \left[ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n} \right]\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \xi \alpha_ {\xi k _ {1} \left(a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}\right), \dots , \xi k _ {n} \left(a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}\right)}
$$

4. for factorizers,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(\xi \psi \left[ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n} \right] \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right)\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \\ \psi_ {\xi k _ {1} (a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}), \dots , \xi k _ {n} (a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l})} \big ((\xi e _ {1}) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}}, \dots , (\xi e _ {m}) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}} \big) \\ \end{array}
$$

5. for functors,

$$
\left(\xi f \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}\right)\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \xi f \left(\left(\xi e _ {1}\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}}, \dots , \left(\xi e _ {n}\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}}\right)
$$

6. for morphism variables,

$$
\left(\xi f\right) _ {a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {n}} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \omega \left(f, a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {l}\right)
$$

proof of well-definedness: we have to show, for example, for a natural transformation  $\alpha \in \delta_{k,l}$ ,  $(\xi \alpha [k_1,\ldots ,k_n])_{a_1,\ldots ,a_l}$  is a morphism from

$$
\xi k [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] (a _ {1}, \ldots , a _ {l}) \qquad \mathrm {t o} \qquad \xi l [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] (a _ {1}, \ldots , a _ {l}).
$$

this holds because from definition 2.3.1  $\xi \alpha_{\xi k_1(a_1,\dots,a_l),\dots,\xi k_n(a_1,\dots,a_l)}$  is a morphism from  $\xi k(\xi k_1(a_1,\dots,a_l),\dots))$  to  $\xi l(\xi k_1(a_1,\dots,a_l),\dots)$  and from proposition 2.1.18 it is from  $\xi k[k_1,\dots,k_n](a_1,\dots,a_l)$  to  $\xi l[k_1,\dots,k_n](a_1,\dots,a_l)$ .

example 2.4.11: let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  be a csl signature for cartesian closed categories presented in example 2.2.2 and  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$  be a csl structure of this signature where  $\mathcal{c}$  is a cartesian closed category and  $\xi$  is the standard assignment (i.e. the product symbol to the product functor and so on). let us find out the denotation of  $\operatorname{ev} \circ \operatorname{pair}(f, \pi_2)$ . first, we have to find out the corresponding most general annotated expression and its type by the algorithm used to prove proposition 2.4.8.

1.  $\operatorname{ev} \circ \operatorname{pair}(f, \pi_2)$  is given by composing  $\operatorname{ev}$  and  $\operatorname{pair}(f, \pi_2)$ , so we need to calculate the most general annotated expressions for these two sub-expressions first.

(a) ev is a natural transformation, and its most general annotated expression is

$$
\rho_ {1} \vdash \operatorname {e v} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {2}, \pi_ {2} ^ {2} ]: \operatorname {p r o d} [ \exp , \pi_ {1} ^ {2} ] \rightarrow \pi_ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

(b)  $\operatorname{pair}(f, \pi_2)$  is given by applying the factorizer pair to  $f$  and  $\pi_2$ .

i.  $f$  is a morphism variable, its most general annotated expression is

$$
\rho_ {2} \vdash f \colon \pi_ {1} ^ {2} \to \pi_ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

ii.  $\pi_2$  is a natural transformation, and its most general annotated expression is

$$
\rho_ {3} \vdash \pi_ {2} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {2}, \pi_ {1} ^ {2} ]: \mathrm {p r o d} \rightarrow \pi_ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

pair has the type

$$
\langle \langle \pi_ {3} ^ {3}, \pi_ {1} ^ {3} \rangle \langle \pi_ {3} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} \rangle , \langle \pi_ {3} ^ {3}, \operatorname {p r o d} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ] \rangle \rangle
$$

by unification, we get the most general annotated expression for  $\operatorname{pair}(f, \pi_2)$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \rho_ {4} \vdash \operatorname {p a i r} [ \pi_ {3} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3}, \operatorname {p r o d} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ] ] (f, \pi_ {2} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ]) \colon \\ \operatorname {p r o d} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ] \to \operatorname {p r o d} [ \pi_ {3} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ] \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\rho_4$  maps  $f$  to  $\mathrm{prod}[\pi_1^3,\pi_2^3 ]\to \pi_3^3$

unifying  $\mathrm{prod}[\pi_3^3,\pi_2^3 ]$  and  $\mathrm{prod}[\exp ,\pi_1^2 ]$  , we get the most general annotated expression for  $\operatorname {ev}\circ \operatorname {pair}(f,\pi_2)$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \rho_ {5} \vdash \mathrm {e v} [ \pi_ {2} ^ {3}, \pi_ {3} ^ {3} ] \circ \mathrm {p a i r} [ \exp [ \pi_ {2} ^ {3}, \pi_ {3} ^ {3} ], \pi_ {2} ^ {3}, \mathrm {p r o d} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ] ] (f, \pi_ {2} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ]) \\ : \mathrm {p r o d} [ \pi_ {1} ^ {3}, \pi_ {2} ^ {3} ] \to \pi_ {3} ^ {3} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\rho_{5}$  maps  $f$  to  $\mathrm{prod}[\pi_1^3,\pi_2^3 ]\to \exp [\pi_2^3,\pi_3^3 ]$

from definition 2.4.10, the denotation of this annotated expression is a set of morphisms for objects  $a$ ,  $b$  and  $c$  and a morphism variable assignment

$$
\omega (f, a, b, c) \colon \xi \mathrm {p r o d} (a, b) \to \xi \exp (b, c).
$$

1.  $(\xi \mathrm{ev}[\pi_2^3,\pi_3^3 ])_{a,b,c} = \xi \mathrm{ev}_{b,c}$  
2.  $(\xi f)_{a,b,c} = \omega (f,a,b,c)$  
3.  $(\xi \pi_{2}[\pi_{1}^{3},\pi_{2}^{3}])_{a,b,c} = \xi \pi_{2a,b}$  
4.  $(\xi \mathrm{pair}[\dots ](f,\pi_2[\dots ]))_{a,b,c} =$ $\xi \mathrm{pair}_{\xi \exp (b,c),b,\xi \mathrm{prod}(a,b)}(\omega (f,a,b,c),\xi \pi_{2a,b})$  
5.  $(\xi \mathrm{ev}[\ldots ]\circ \mathrm{pair}[\ldots ](f,\pi_2[\ldots ]))_{a,b,c} =$ $\xi \mathrm{ev}_{b,c}\circ \xi \mathrm{pair}_{\xi \exp (b,c),b,\xi \mathrm{prod}(a,b)}(\omega (f,a,b,c),\xi \pi_{2a,b})$

therefore, the denotation of  $\operatorname{ev} \circ \operatorname{pair}(f, \pi_2)$  is

$$
\xi \mathrm {e v} _ {b, c} \circ \xi \mathrm {p a i r} _ {\xi \exp (b, c), b, \xi \mathrm {p r o d} (a, b)} (\omega (f, a, b, c), \xi \pi_ {2 a, b}) \quad \mathbb {i}
$$

# 2.5 sentences and satisfaction relation of categorical specification language

in this section, we will finish defining the specification language csl at last. first, we define what a csl sentence is.

definition 2.5.1: a csl conditional equation is a sequence of csl expression pairs and a csl expression pair. we usually write it as

$$
e _ {1} = e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \wedge \dots \wedge e _ {n} = e _ {n} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow e = e ^ {\prime},
$$

or simply  $e = e'$  if the preceding sequence is empty. to be typed, it needs to share the same morphism variable environment,  $e_i$  and  $e_i'$  have to have the same type and  $e$  and  $e'$  have to have the same type. we may write the types as follows:

$$
\rho \vdash e _ {1} = e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \colon k _ {1} \to l _ {1} \land \ldots \land e _ {n} = e _ {n} ^ {\prime} \colon k _ {n} \to l _ {n} \rightarrow e = e ^ {\prime} \colon k \to l
$$

we write  $\mathbf{c}\mathbf{e}\mathbf{q}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  for the set of all the csl conditional equations which can be typed.

the csl conditional equations are the csl sentences. we now have to define the satisfaction relation for csl. we have separately defined what csl structures are and what csl conditional equations are. the satisfaction relation connects these two together so that we can say a csl conditional equation holds or not in a particular csl structure.

definition 2.5.2: let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  be a csl signature. a csl structure  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$  satisfies a csl conditional equation

$$
e _ {1} = e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \wedge \ldots \wedge e _ {n} = e _ {n} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow e = e ^ {\prime}
$$

having a type

$$
\rho \vdash e _ {1} = e _ {1} ^ {\prime}: k _ {1} \to l _ {1} \land \dots \land e _ {n} = e _ {n} ^ {\prime}: k _ {n} \to l _ {n} \rightarrow e = e ^ {\prime}: k \to l,
$$

if and only if for any objects  $a_{1},\ldots ,a_{l}$  and any morphism variable assignment  $\omega$  we have either

1. a csl equation  $e_i = e_i'$  such that  $(\xi e_i)_{a_1, \dots, a_l} \neq (\xi e_i')_{a_1, \dots, a_l}$ , or  
2.  $(\xi e)_{a_1,\dots ,a_l} = (\xi e')_{a_1,\dots ,a_l}$

we will write

$$
\langle \mathcal {c}, \xi \rangle \models e _ {1} = e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \wedge \dots \wedge e _ {n} = e _ {n} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow e = e ^ {\prime}
$$

when  $\langle \mathcal{c},\xi \rangle$  satisfies this csl conditional equation.

we have defined the specification language, csl: csl signatures, csl structures, csl conditional equations and csl satisfaction relation. we could have defined it as an institution (see [goguen and burstall 83]) by defining cmod as a contravariant functor and showing csl satisfaction condition.

finally, let us finish presenting the csl theory (i.e. a pair of csl signature and csl conditional equations) of cartesian closed categories.

example 2.5.3: we have presented the signature for cartesian closed categories in example 2.2.2 (or figure 2.1), so all we have to do is to list the csl conditional equations. (note that they are not conditional for this example.)

$1.f = !$  
2.1 = i  
3.  $\operatorname{pair}(\pi_1, \pi_2) = \mathbf{i}$  
$4.\pi_{1}\circ \mathrm{pair}(f,g) = f$  
$5.\pi_{2}\circ \mathrm{pair}(f,g) = g$  
6.  $\operatorname {pair}(f,g)\circ h = \operatorname {pair}(f\circ h,g\circ h)$  
7.  $\operatorname{prod}(f, g) = \operatorname{pair}(f \circ \pi_1, g \circ \pi_2)$  
8.curry(ev) = i  
9.ev  $\circ$  curry  $(\mathrm{prod}(f,\mathbf{i})) = f$  
10.  $\mathrm{curry}(f)\circ g = \mathrm{curry}(f\circ \mathrm{prod}(g,\mathbf{i}))$  
11.  $\exp (f,g) = \mathrm{curry}(g\circ \mathrm{eval}\circ \mathrm{prod}(f,\mathbf{i}))$

the naturality of  $\pi_1$ ,  $\pi_2$  and  $\mathrm{ev}$  can be derived from these equations. for example, the naturality of  $\pi_1$  is shown by

$$
\pi_ {1} \circ \mathrm {p r o d} (f, g) = \pi_ {1} \circ \mathrm {p a i r} (f \circ \pi_ {1}, g \circ \pi_ {2}) = f \circ \pi_ {1}.
$$

# 2.6 free categories

one of the major advantages of algebraic specification methods using equations or conditional equations over other specification methods is that any theory has an initial model (i.e. the initial object in the category of models which satisfy the theory). this also holds for csl, and in this section, we will construct an initial structure for a csl theory. remember that a csl structure is a pair of a category and an interpretation. the category of an initial csl structure corresponds to a so-called free category.

given a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  and a set  $\theta$  of csl conditional equations, we are going to define a special category  $\mathcal{c}$  and an interpretation  $\xi$ . for simplicity, we assume that  $\gamma$  does not contain any free-variant functors. (we can always get such a signature by discarding free-variant arguments. this does not affect its semantics at all.)

definition 2.6.1: we say that a closed functorial expression is ground if it has no variables, that is, its basic form is  $\lambda().e$ . we take ground closed functorial expressions as the objects of  $\mathcal{c}$ .

the definition of morphisms of  $\mathcal{c}$  is a little bit more complicated, so we define them step by step.

definition 2.6.2: a ground annotated expression is an annotated expression such that

1. all the annotation,  $[k_1,\ldots ,k_n]$  consists of ground closed functorial expressions, and  
2. it does not contain any morphism variables.  $\mathbb{i}$

proposition 2.6.3: if a ground annotated expression  $e$  has a type  $\rho \vdash e \colon k \to l$ , and both  $k$  and  $l$  are ground functorial expressions.

proof: we can easily prove it from definition 2.4.5 by structural induction.  $\square$

the following will give us the basis of the morphisms in  $\mathcal{c}$ .

definition 2.6.4: for ground closed functorial expressions  $k$  and  $l$ , we define

$$
\mathbf {g e x p} (k, l) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left\{e \mid e \text {i s g r o u n d a n d} \emptyset \vdash e: k \rightarrow l \right\}
$$

to make  $\mathbf{gexp}$  proper morphisms, we introduce a family of equivalence relations  $\equiv$  indexed by a pair of ground functorial expressions. each  $\equiv_{k,l}$  is an equivalence relation on  $\mathbf{gexp}(k,l)$ .

definition 2.6.5: we define  $\equiv$  to be the smallest relation satisfying the following conditions. (in the following, to simplify the presentation, we omit indexes of  $\equiv$  if there is no ambiguity.)

1.  $\equiv$  is an equivalence relation, that is, reflexive, symmetric and transitive.  
2. if  $e_1 \equiv_{k,l} e_1'$  and  $e_2 \equiv_{k',k} e_2'$ , then  $e_1 \circ e_2 \equiv_{k',l} e_1' \circ e_2'$ .  
3. if  $e \in \mathbf{gexp}(k, l)$ , then  $\mathbf{i}[l] \circ e \equiv e$  and  $e \circ \mathbf{i}[k] \equiv e$ .  
4. for a functor symbol  $f \in \gamma_{v_1 \ldots v_n}$ , if  $e_1 \equiv e_1'$ , ... and  $e_n \equiv e_n'$ , then  $f(e_1, \ldots, e_n) \equiv f(e_1', \ldots, e_n')$ .  
5. for a factorizer symbol  $\psi \in \psi_{\langle \langle k_1', l_1' \rangle \ldots \langle k_l', l_l' \rangle, \langle k', l' \rangle}$  and ground functorial expressions  $k_1, \ldots, k_n$ , if  $e_1 \equiv e_1'$ , ... and  $e_l \equiv e_l'$ , then

$$
\psi [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {l}) \equiv \psi [ k _ {1}, \ldots , k _ {n} ] (e _ {1} ^ {\prime}, \ldots , e _ {l} ^ {\prime}).
$$

6. finally, for a conditional csl equation  $e_1 = e_1' \wedge \ldots \wedge e_n = e_n' \rightarrow e = e' \in \theta$  whose type is

$$
\rho \vdash e _ {1} = e _ {1} ^ {\prime}: k _ {1} ^ {\prime} \to l _ {1} ^ {\prime} \land \dots \land e _ {n} = e _ {n} ^ {\prime}: k _ {n} ^ {\prime} \to l _ {n} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow e = e ^ {\prime}: k ^ {\prime} \to l ^ {\prime}
$$

with  $\rho(f_i)$  is  $k_i'' \to l_i''$ , ground functorial expressions  $k_1, \ldots, k_m$ , and ground annotated expressions

$$
e _ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} \in \mathbf {g e x p} \left(k _ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {m} ], l _ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {m} ]\right),
$$

if for all  $j = 1,\ldots ,n$

$$
\begin{array}{l} (e _ {j} [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {m} ]) [ e _ {1} ^ {\prime \prime}, \dots , e _ {l} ^ {\prime \prime} / f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {l} ] \equiv \\ (e _ {j} ^ {\prime} [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {m} ]) [ e _ {1} ^ {\prime \prime}, \dots , e _ {l} ^ {\prime \prime} / f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {l} ], \\ \end{array}
$$

then

$$
\begin{array}{l} (e [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {m} ]) [ e _ {1} ^ {\prime \prime}, \dots , e _ {l} ^ {\prime \prime} / f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {l} ] \equiv \\ (e ^ {\prime} [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {m} ]) [ e _ {1} ^ {\prime \prime}, \dots , e _ {l} ^ {\prime \prime} / f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {l} ].   ] \\ \end{array}
$$

we can now define the morphisms of  $\mathcal{c}$ .

definition 2.6.1 (continued): the  $\mathcal{c}$  morphisms from  $k$  to  $l$  are the equivalence classes of  $\mathbf{gexp}(k,l)$  by  $\equiv_{k,l}$ , or simply,

$$
\operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (k, l) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \mathbf {g e x p} (k, l) / \equiv_ {k, l}.
$$

we write  $\langle e\rangle$  for the equivalence class to which  $e$  belongs.

proposition 2.6.6:  $\mathcal{c}$  is a category.

proof: the identity morphism of a  $\mathcal{c}$  object  $k$  is given by  $\langle \mathbf{i}[k]\rangle$ . the composition of  $\langle e\rangle \colon k\to l$  and  $\langle e^{\prime}\rangle \colon k^{\prime}\to k$  is defined by

$$
\langle e \rangle \circ \langle e ^ {\prime} \rangle \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \langle e \circ e ^ {\prime} \rangle .
$$

the composition is trivially associative, and the satisfiability of the absorption rules of the identities is guaranteed by the third condition of the equivalence relation  $\equiv$  defined in definition 2.6.5.

let us define an interpretation  $\xi$  so that  $\langle \mathcal{c},\xi \rangle$  is a csl structure of the csl signature  $\langle \gamma ,\delta ,\psi \rangle$

definition 2.6.7: the definition of  $\xi$  is divided into three.

1. for a functor symbol  $f \in \gamma_{v_1 \ldots v_n}$ ,  $\xi f$  is a functor  $\mathcal{c}^{v_1 \ldots v_n} \to \mathcal{c}$  defined by

$$
\xi f \left(k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n}\right) \stackrel {{\text {d e f}}} {{=}} f \left[ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n}\right), \quad \text {a n d}
$$

$$
\xi f \left(\left\langle e _ {1} \right\rangle , \dots , \left\langle e _ {n} \right\rangle\right) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left\langle f \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}\right) \right\rangle .
$$

2. for a natural transformation symbol  $\alpha \in \delta_{k,l}$ ,  $\xi \alpha$  is

$$
\xi \alpha_ {k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n}} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \langle \alpha [ k _ {1}, \dots , k _ {n} ] \rangle .
$$

3. finally, for a factorizer symbol  $\psi \in \psi_{\langle \langle k_1,l_1\rangle \ldots \langle k_n,l_n\rangle ,\langle k,l\rangle \rangle}$ ,  $\xi \psi$  is

$$
\xi \psi_ {k _ {1} ^ {\prime}, \dots , k _ {n} ^ {\prime}} (\langle e _ {1} \rangle , \dots , \langle e _ {n} \rangle) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \langle \psi [ k _ {1} ^ {\prime}, \dots , k _ {n} ^ {\prime} ] (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}) \rangle . \llbracket
$$

proposition 2.6.8:  $\langle \mathcal{c},\xi \rangle$  is a csl structure of  $\langle \gamma ,\delta ,\psi \rangle$  . moreover, it is a theory structure of the csl theory given by the set  $\theta$  of csl conditional equations. proof: the condition 6 in definition 2.6.5 makes it satisfy the conditional equations.

we have constructed a special csl structure  $\langle \mathcal{c},\xi \rangle$ , and we will next show that it is the initial object in the full-subcategory of  $\mathbf{cmod}(\langle \gamma ,\delta ,\psi \rangle)$  of all the structures satisfying  $\theta$ .

theorem 2.6.9: for any csl structure satisfying  $\theta$ , there is a unique csl structure morphism from  $\langle \mathcal{c}, \xi \rangle$ .

proof: let  $\langle \mathcal{d},\zeta \rangle$  be an arbitrary csl structure satisfying  $\theta$ . using the denotation of annotated expressions on this structure (see definition 2.4.10), we define a functor  $t$  from  $\mathcal{c}$  to  $\mathcal{d}$  as follows:

$$
t (k) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \zeta k \quad \mathrm {a n d} \quad t (\langle e \rangle) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \zeta e.
$$

note that, since  $k$  is a ground functorial expression  $\zeta k$  is an  $\mathcal{d}$  object, and that, since  $e$  is a ground annotated expression,  $\zeta e$  is a  $\mathcal{d}$  morphism (it does not need objects  $a_{1},\ldots ,a_{m}$  or a morphism variable assignment  $\omega$ ). it is easy to see that  $t$  is a (covariant) functor (note that we have to show the well-definedness first). it is also not so difficult to show that  $t$  is a csl structure morphism and that it is the unique one from  $\langle \mathcal{c},\xi \rangle$  to  $\langle \mathcal{d},\zeta \rangle$  (simply extending the result on algebraic specifications).

the advantage of working in an initial csl structure is that, if we show that a conditional equation holds in the initial one, then we automatically know that it holds in any csl structure. in chapter 4, we will define the symbolic computation in categories and it can be regarded as the computation in free categories.

# chapter 3

# categorical data types

in chapter 2, we introduced categorical data types from a point of view of a specification language defining categories. although the specification language csl is a rigorous language, it is rather tedious and categoritians may never define categories in that way. in this chapter, we will give another presentation of categorical data types, which will be simpler and more intuitive. this is also the way categorical data types originated. note that we are not discarding csl completely and that the semantics of categorical data types will be given in terms of csl.

section 3.1 is an extended introduction to categorical data types. we will investigate some conventional data types and introduce a new uniform categorical way of defining data types. in section 3.2, we will make this new way into the cdt declaration mechanism. section 3.3 will give various examples of cdt declarations. in section 3.4, cdt declarations will be connected to csl theories, and finally in section 3.5 we will give a construction of cdt data types.

# 3.1 what are categorical data types?

the need for pairs of two (or more) items of data often arises when we write programs. it is often the case that a function or procedure takes more than one argument and this means that a pair (or a record) of data needs to be passed to the function or procedure. in another situation, we may want to declare a new data type whose element is a pair of elements of other data types. in pascal, we can define such data types using its record ... end construct. for example, intchar whose element is a pair of an integer and a character can be declared as:

```txt
type intchar  $=$  record first:integer; second: char end;
```

in ml, this can be done by

```txt
type intchar = int * string;
```

(where since ml does not have a type for representing characters, we have to use 'string' type whose element is a sequence of characters). these two languages have the means of constructing data types of pairs from already-existing data types. let us call the constructors product type constructors. most of the current programming languages have product type constructors in one way or another as their built-in primitives because they are so essential that we can even say that no programming language is complete without them.

in order to understand the nature of product type constructors, let us suppose a programming language which does not have them as primitives and that we have to define them in terms of others. this might mean that we need in the language some kind of one level higher operations which can define not types but type constructors. let us refer to an algebraic specification language clear, and see its ability to define a product type constructor.

```txt
constant triv = theory
    sort element
    endth
procedure prod(a:triv,b:triv) = theory
    data sort prod
    ops pair: element of a,element of b -> prod
        pi1 : prod -> element of a
        pi2 : prod -> element of b
    eqns all a:element of a,b:element of b,
        pi1(pair(a,b)) = a
        all a:element of a,b:element of b,
        pi2(pair(a,b)) = b
    endth
```

this defines an algebra  $p$  of three sorts: two 'element' sorts (let us call them 'a-element' and 'b-element' to distinguish them) and a 'prod' sort. the underlying set  $|p|_{\mathrm{prod}}$  is the (set) product of two sets  $|p|_{\mathrm{a-element}}$  and  $|p|_{\mathrm{b-element}}$ . the declaration also defines three operations (or functions), 'pair', 'pi1' and 'pi2', satisfying the two equations listed. note that the following equation can be proved using induction on 'prod' sort (we have the induction principle on this sort because of the initial data constraint).

$$
\text {a l l} x: \text {p r o d}, \text {p a i r} (\mathrm {p i 1} (x), \mathrm {p i 2} (x)) = x
$$

an algebraic specification language like clear is powerful enough to allow us to define all kinds of type constructors (including ordinary data types as constant type constructors) in a uniform way without having any particular primitives. however, because of its use of equations, we cannot adopt its declaration mechanism in ordinary programming languages.<sup>2</sup> we might be puzzled that we need equations even to define such very basic data types as products.

let us find whether there is any other way of defining product type constructors by examining the foundation, namely mathematics. modern mathematics uses set theory extensively because of its power. set theory does not have a product type constructor as its primitive construct either, so it is defined by means of other constructs. for sets  $a$  and  $b$ , their (set) product is defined as:

$$
a \times b \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left\{\left(x, y\right) \mid x \in a, y \in b \right\}
$$

where  $(x,y)$  is really an abbreviation of  $\{\{x\} \{x y\}\}$ . although this looks very simple, it actually needs some work to show from the axioms of set theory that this is actually a set. set theory uses the power of first order logic so heavily that it is much harder to put the set theory formalism into a programming language than to put the algebraic specification formalism. as an example, let  $\phi (x)$  be a first order formula. then, from the comprehension axiom (or the replacement axiom), we have the set

$$
\left\{x \in a \mid \phi (x) \right\}
$$

where  $a$  is a set.  $\phi(x)$  can be anything expressible by first order logic (using quantifiers and negations), and this is too much powerful to investigate the basic property of data types. it disfigures the beauty behind this powerful definition mechanism and we cannot see through it easily.

set theory has achieved a firm position as the foundation of mathematics, but there are some alternatives. category theory is one of them. it has been proved that category theory has a remarkable ability to disclose true nature of mathematical objects. for example, a product constructor (or categorically a functor

$\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c}$  is beautifully characterized as the right adjoint of the diagonal functor  $\mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}$ . expanding the definition of adjunctions, this means that we have the following natural isomorphism:

$$
\operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (c, a) \times \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (c, b) \simeq \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (c, \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b)) \quad (*)
$$

(natural in  $a, b$  and  $c$ ). we use 'prod' for the product functor to follow the notation we use later. the isomorphic function from the left-hand side to the right-hand side is the factorizer of this adjunction and we write 'pair' for it. we can rewrite this adjoint situation as the following rule:

$$
\frac {c \xrightarrow {f} a \qquad c \xrightarrow {g} b}{c \xrightarrow {\operatorname {p a i r} (f , g)} \operatorname {p r o d} (a , b)} \tag {**}
$$

given two morphisms  $f \colon c \to a$  and  $g \colon c \to b$ , pair  $(f, g)$  give a morphism of  $c \to \operatorname{prod}(a, b)$ . this correspondence is one-to-one and is natural in  $a, b$  and  $c$ .

comparing with the product type constructor 'prod' defined by clear, we have the same 'pair' though the previous one takes two elements as the arguments and this one takes two morphisms instead, and now we can find the things corresponding to two projections 'pi1' and 'pi2' as well. replacing  $c$  by  $\operatorname{prod}(a, b)$  in  $(\ast)$ , we get:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (\operatorname {p r o d} (a, b), a) \times \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (\operatorname {p r o d} (a, b), b) \simeq \\ \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (\operatorname {p r o d} (a, b), \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b)). \\ \end{array}
$$

we have a very special morphism in  $\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{c}}(\operatorname{prod}(a, b), \operatorname{prod}(a, b))$ , namely the identity. because of the isomorphism, there exist unique morphisms of  $\operatorname{prod}(a, b) \to a$  and  $\operatorname{prod}(a, b) \to b$  which are mapped to the identity by 'pair', and these are the projections. we name them 'pi1' and 'pi2' as well. because of the very way they are defined, it is trivial that

$$
\operatorname {p a i r} (\mathrm {p i} 1, \mathrm {p i} 2) = \mathbf {i}. \qquad (+)
$$

furthermore, from the naturality in  $c$  of  $(\ast)$ , we have the rule

$$
\frac {c \xrightarrow {\operatorname {p a i r} (f , g)} \operatorname {p r o d} (a , b) \xrightarrow {\mathrm {p i 1}} a \qquad c \xrightarrow {\operatorname {p a i r} (f , g)} \operatorname {p r o d} (a , b) \xrightarrow {\mathrm {p i 2}} b}{c \xrightarrow {\operatorname {p a i r} (f , g)} \operatorname {p r o d} (a , b) \xrightarrow {\operatorname {p a i r} (\mathrm {p i 1} , \mathrm {p i 2})} \operatorname {p r o d} (a , b)},
$$

and, if we express it by equations and use  $(+)$ ,

$$
\operatorname {p a i r} (\mathrm {p i} 1 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (f, g), \mathrm {p i} 2 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (f, g)) = \operatorname {p a i r} (\mathrm {p i} 1, \mathrm {p i} 2) \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (f, g) = \operatorname {p a i r} (f, g).
$$

since 'pair' is isomorphic, we can conclude that the following equations hold:

$$
\operatorname {p i} 1 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (f, g) = f \quad \text {a n d} \quad \operatorname {p i} 2 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (f, g) = g.
$$

these are exactly the ones which we listed when defining 'prod' in clear. note that this time they are derived equations. by saying that 'prod' is the right adjoint to the diagonal functor, we get these equations automatically. this shows how neat the categorical definition is.

another advantage of categorical definition is that we can form the dual definition easily. we defined the product functor as the right adjoint of the diagonal functor. then, it is natural to ask what is the left adjoint of the diagonal functor. it is the coproduct functor  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c}$ . in the category of sets, the coproduct of two sets  $a$  and  $b$  is their disjoint sum

$$
a + b \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \left\{0 \right\} \times a \bigcup \left\{1 \right\} \times b.
$$

it is not easy to see in set theory that this is the dual of  $a \times b$ . in pascal, we can define coproducts by means of variant record. in ml, we used to have a built-in coproduct type constructor  $+$ , but the new standard ml does not. instead,  $+$  can be defined by the following 'datatype' declaration.

$$
\text {d a t a t y p e} ^ {\prime} \mathrm {a} + ^ {\prime} \mathrm {b} = \text {i n 1 o f} ^ {\prime} \mathrm {a} | \text {i n 2 o f} ^ {\prime} \mathrm {b};
$$

we cannot define the product type constructor by a 'datatype' declaration in ml, but we can define its dual. ml looks non-symmetric from this. in clear, we can define a coproduct type constructor as follows:

```txt
procedure coprod(a:triv,b:triv) = theory data sort coprod ops in1: element of a -> coprod in2: element of b -> coprod endth
```

again, this cannot be seen as the dual of 'prod' we defined earlier; here we do not use equations; we have only two operations whereas we had three. this shows that clear is not symmetric either.

now, in category theory, the coproduct functor  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c}$  which we call 'coprod' is defined by the dual of  $(\ast)$ , by just changing the direction of arrows.

$$
\operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (a, c) \times \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (b, c) \simeq \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (\operatorname {c o p r o d} (a, b), c)
$$

we name the isomorphic function going from the left-hand side to the right-hand side 'case' (we could call it 'copair' to emphasize the duality to 'pair', but, since

it plays a role of 'case' statements of ml or c (or pascal), we call it 'case'. writing the adjunction as a rule,

$$
\begin{array}{c c} a \xrightarrow {f} c & b \xrightarrow {g} c \\ \hline \operatorname {c o p r o d} (a, b) \xrightarrow {\operatorname {c a s e} (f , g)} c \end{array} .
$$

two injections in1:  $a \to \operatorname{coprod}(a, b)$  and in2:  $b \to \operatorname{coprod}(a, b)$  are defined as the morphisms which 'case' maps to the identity of coprod(a,b). as before, we can derive some equations easily.

from what we have looked at, it seems a good idea to design a category theory based (programming or specification) language which has the ability to define functors by means of adjunctions. since it is convenient to introduce names for unit natural transformations and factorizers at the same time, we regard an adjunction as a triple of a functor, a unit natural transformation and a factorizer (see, for example, [mac lane 71] for many equivalent ways of defining adjunctions). therefore, a category theory based language may have the following two forms of declaring new functors:

let  $\langle f,\alpha ,\psi \rangle$  be right adjoint of  $g$  let  $\langle f,\alpha ,\psi \rangle$  be left adjoint of  $g$

where  $g$  is a functor we already have,  $f$  is the new functor we are defining,  $\alpha$  is the associated unit natural transformation and  $\psi$  is the associated factorizer. one problem is that we need to have some primitive functors with which we start. we definitely need diagonal functors for we want to define product and coproduct functors. in order to define the natural number object (which is a constant functor), we need a pretty complicated functor  $g$ . the problem is how to represent such  $g$ .

let us investigate how other languages and theories define the data type of natural numbers. in set theory, it has the axiom of infinity which says the existence of natural numbers. this may look rather artificial. in pascal, there is no intuitively easy way to define it. in ml, though it is a built-in data type for efficiency, we could define it as:

$$
\text {d a t a t y p e} = \text {z e r o} \mid \text {s u c c} \text {o f} \text {n a t};
$$

note the recursiveness in this definition. essentially, we need some kind of recursiveness to define a data type of natural numbers. in clear, one can define it as

```txt
constant nat  $=$  theory data sort nat
```

```txt
opsns zero: nat succ: nat -> nat  
endth
```

this is very much similar to the one in ml, though we often think that the clear definition is based on the initial algebra semantics whereas the ml definition is based on domain theory. in domain theory, a data type of natural numbers can be defined as the solution of the following domain equation

$$
n \cong 1 + n. \qquad (*)
$$

the initial solution of  $(\ast)$  can be calculated as a colimit of a sequence of domains, but we do not go into its detail here. as a connection to the initial algebra semantics, the initial solution can be characterized as the initial  $t$ -algebra, where  $t$  is a functor  $t(x) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} 1 + x$  in this case. in general, given a category and an endo-functor  $t$ , we can form a category of  $t$ -algebras.

definition 3.1.1: for a category  $\mathcal{c}$  and an endo-functor  $t\colon \mathcal{c}\to \mathcal{c}$ , the category of  $t$ -algebras is defined

1. its objects are pairs  $\langle a, f \rangle$  where  $a$  is a  $\mathcal{c}$  object and  $f$  is a  $\mathcal{c}$  morphism  $t(a) \to a$ , and  
2. its morphisms  $h \colon \langle a, f \rangle \to \langle b, g \rangle$  are  $\mathcal{c}$  morphisms  $h \colon a \to b$  which make the following diagram commute.

![](images/d2d96c43ebbe92418dab7392a904b774f000259602ba3b88b6c268639378c659.jpg)

note that this is a weaker version of the category of  $t$ -algebras defined in many category theory books (e.g. [mac lane 71]) where  $t$  needs to be a monad.

we can dualize definition 3.1.1 to define  $t$ -coalgebras. however, in the theory of categorical data types ('cdt theory' for short), we combine the two definitions together.

definition 3.1.2: let  $\mathcal{c}$  and  $\mathcal{d}$  be categories and both  $f$  and  $g$  be functors from  $\mathcal{c}$  to  $\mathcal{d}$ . we define an  $f, g$ -dialgebra<sup>4</sup> as

1. its objects are pairs  $\langle a, f \rangle$  where  $a$  is a  $\mathcal{c}$  object and  $f$  is a  $\mathcal{d}$  morphism of  $f(a) \to g(a)$ , and  
2. its morphisms  $h \colon \langle a, f \rangle \to \langle b, g \rangle$  are  $\mathcal{c}$  morphisms  $h \colon a \to b$  such that the following diagram commutes.

![](images/d9d3d4284f9443f743c512f8146baf90754e5c7a0b6ac3cfd1620bec71720aec.jpg)

in the case where  $f$  or  $g$  is contravariant, we have to modify the direction of some arrows.

it is easy to show that it is a category; let us write  $\mathbf{dalg}(f,g)$  for it. note that  $\mathbf{dalg}(t,\mathbf{i})$  is the category of  $t$ -algebras and  $\mathbf{dalg}(\mathbf{i},t)$  is the category of  $t$ -coalgebras.

this is a very simple extension of definition 3.1.1, yet its symmetry and dividing the source category from the target one give us greater freedom. with  $t$ -algebras, we need to use the coproduct functor to define the domain of natural numbers, but by  $f, g$ -dialgebra we do not. let  $\mathcal{c}$  be any category and  $\mathcal{d}$  be its product  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}$ . we define the functors  $f$  and  $g$  as

$$
f (a) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \langle 1, a \rangle \quad \text {a n d} \quad g (a) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \langle a, a \rangle .
$$

let  $\langle \mathrm{nat},\langle \mathrm{zero,succ}\rangle \rangle$  be the initial  $f,g$  -dialgebra. from the definition, nat' is a  $\mathcal{c}$  object, zero' is a  $\mathcal{c}$  morphism of  $1\to$  nat and 'succ' is a  $\mathcal{c}$  morphism of nat  $\rightarrow$  nat. the initiality means that for any  $\mathbf{dalg}(f,g)$  object  $\langle a,\langle f,g\rangle \rangle$  there exists a unique  $\mathbf{dalg}(f,g)$  morphism  $h\colon \langle \mathrm{nat},\langle \mathrm{zero,succ}\rangle \rangle \to \langle a,\langle f,g\rangle \rangle .$  if we spell out the definition, this means that for any  $\mathcal{c}$  object  $a$  and any  $\mathcal{c}$  morphisms  $f\colon 1\to a$  and  $g\colon a\to a$  there exists a unique  $\mathcal{c}$  morphism  $h\colon \mathrm{nat}\to a$  which makes the following diagram commute.

![](images/553675d34b0c7ca2b638024af78d8fd43b875f0ec8ffbbc76cf99fc5a56f0ee2.jpg)

this is exactly the definition of 'nat' being a natural number object in category theory.

to get further generality of  $f, g$ -dialgebras, we parametrize  $f$  and  $g$ .

definition 3.1.3: let  $\mathcal{c}$ ,  $\mathcal{d}$  and  $\mathcal{e}$  be categories, and let  $f\colon \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{e}$  and  $g\colon \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d}^{-} \to \mathcal{e}$  be functors. we define  $\langle \operatorname{left}[f,g](a), \eta_a \rangle$  for a  $\mathcal{d}$  object  $a$  to be the initial object in the category  $\mathbf{dalg}(f(\cdot, a), g(\cdot, a))$ . dually, we define  $\langle \operatorname{right}[f,g](a), \epsilon_a \rangle$  to be the final object. we may write  $\operatorname{right}(a)$  for  $\operatorname{right}[f,g](a)$  and  $\operatorname{left}(a)$  for  $\operatorname{left}[f,g](a)$  if the context makes  $f$  and  $g$  clear.

proposition 3.1.4: if  $\operatorname{left}[f, g](a)$  exists for every object  $a \in |\mathcal{d}|$ ,  $\operatorname{left}[f, g]$  denotes a functor  $\mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{c}$  (i.e. we can extend it to  $\mathcal{d}$  morphisms). dually, if  $\operatorname{right}[f, g](a)$  exists for every  $a \in |\mathcal{d}|$ ,  $\operatorname{right}[f, g]$  denotes a functor  $\mathcal{d}^{-} \to \mathcal{c}$ . proof: we first need to define what  $\operatorname{left}[f, g](f)$  is for a  $\mathcal{d}$  morphism  $f \colon a \to b$ . we define it as the morphism  $h \colon \operatorname{left}(a) \to \operatorname{left}(b)$  which fills in the following diagram.

![](images/6c3367005fda388d540a42e50b5800929d54c3120e4032b15ad663564dfe9cf6.jpg)

![](images/71b313fefe6ecd9abc21bdc7c6c940292f70b2e198e52ccba5b7b9424817f670.jpg)

the unique existence of the morphism is provided because  $\langle \mathbf{left}(a),\eta_a\rangle$  is the initial object of  $\mathbf{dalg}(f(\cdot ,a),g(\cdot ,a))$ . in other words,  $\mathbf{left}(f)$  is the unique morphism which satisfies

$$
g (\mathbf {l e f t} (f), f) \circ \eta_ {b} \circ f (\mathbf {l e f t} (f), f) = \eta_ {a}.
$$

let us check that left is in fact a functor. trivially,

$$
g (\mathbf {i}, \mathbf {i}) \circ \eta_ {a} \circ f (\mathbf {i}, \mathbf {i}) = \eta_ {a}.
$$

therefore,  $\mathbf{left}(\mathbf{i}_a) = \mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{left}(a)}$ . for morphisms  $f\colon a\to b$  and  $g\colon b\to c$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} g (\mathbf {l e f t} (g) \circ \mathbf {l e f t} (f), g \circ f) \circ \eta_ {c} \circ f (\mathbf {l e f t} (g) \circ \mathbf {l e f t} (f), g \circ f) \\ = g (\mathbf {l e f t} (f), f) \circ g (\mathbf {l e f t} (g), g) \circ \eta_ {c} \circ f (\mathbf {l e f t} (g), g) \circ f (\mathbf {l e f t} (f), f) \\ = g (\mathbf {l e f t} (f), f) \circ \eta_ {b} \circ f (\mathbf {l e f t} (f), f) \\ = \eta_ {a} \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore,  $\mathbf{left}(g)\circ \mathbf{left}(f) = \mathbf{left}(g\circ f)$

![](images/391975b984a1ce386d68b6076fc9460680f0b4d8b5a9cafabe7c1f347e810887.jpg)

dually, we can prove that right is a functor.  $\square$

'left' and 'right' may suggest a connection with left and right adjoint functors. in fact,

proposition 3.1.5: for a functor  $f \colon \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{d}$ , its left adjoint functor can be denoted by

$$
\operatorname {l e f t} [ \lambda (x, y). y, \lambda (x, y). f (x) ]
$$

and, dually, its right adjoint functor can be denoted by

$$
\operatorname {r i g h t} [ \lambda (x, y). f (x), \lambda (x, y). y ].
$$

proof: let us only check the left adjoint case. we see  $\lambda(x, y).y$  as a functor  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{d}$  and  $\lambda(x, y).f(x)$  as a functor  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d}^{-} \to \mathcal{d}$ . from definition 3.1.3,  $\mathbf{left}$  is a functor  $\mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{c}$ . if we spell out the condition of  $\langle \mathbf{left}(a), \eta_a \rangle$  being the initial algebra, it means that for any  $\mathcal{c}$  object  $b$  and a  $\mathcal{c}$  morphism  $f\colon a \to f(b)$  there exists a unique  $\mathcal{c}$  morphism  $h\colon \mathbf{left}(a) \to b$  such that the following diagram

commutes.

![](images/422d3257e055f4c09a9a213d2f60382ffe66df99dca692094541ed9ce03b50a1.jpg)

this is exactly the condition of left being the left adjoint functor of  $f$ . dually, we can prove that right[λ(x,y).f(x), λ(x,y).y] is the right adjoint.

hence, definition 3.1.2 of  $f, g$ -dialgebras covers both  $t$ -algebras and adjoints so that it enables us to define products, coproducts, natural number object, and so on in a uniform way.

# 3.2 data type declarations in categorical data types

in the previous section, we have looked at some ways of defining data types in some languages. in this section, we will introduce how to define data types in cdt.

if we were only interested in functors, only  $\mathbf{left}[f,g]$  and  $\mathbf{right}[f,g]$  defined in the previous section would be needed, but we do want morphisms (or natural transformations) and factorizers which will make up some kind of programs, the meaning of which we will examine in chapter 4 (e.g. how to execute them).

$\mathbf{left}[f,g]$  and  $\mathbf{right}[f,g]$  have been defined for functors  $f: \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{e}$  and  $g: \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d}^{-} \to \mathcal{e}$ , where  $\mathcal{c}$ ,  $\mathcal{d}$  and  $\mathcal{e}$  are some categories. remember the aim of cdt; we would like to define (or specify, or study) a category of data types. therefore,  $\mathcal{c}$ ,  $\mathcal{d}$  and  $\mathcal{e}$  should somehow be related to this category. the simplest we can think of is that they are the product categories of this category and all the functors are in the form of  $\mathcal{c}^s \to \mathcal{c}$ , where  $s$  is a sequence of variances (i.e.  $s \in \mathbf{var}^*$ ) and  $\mathcal{c}^{v_1 \dots v_n} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathcal{c}^{v_1} \times \dots \times \mathcal{c}^{v_n}$ .

in order to simplify the presentation, let us use the vector notation and write, for example,  $\vec{f}$  for a sequence of functors  $\langle f_1,\ldots ,f_n\rangle$  where all of them have the same type  $\mathcal{c}^s\to \mathcal{c}$ , that is,  $\vec{f}$  is a functor of  $\mathcal{c}^s\rightarrow \mathcal{c}^n$ .

from definition 3.1.3, for  $\vec{f}:\mathcal{c}\times \mathcal{c}^s\to \mathcal{c}^n$  and  $\vec{g}:\mathcal{c}\times \mathcal{c}^{-\bullet s}\to \mathcal{c}^n$ ,  $\mathbf{left}[f,g]$  is a functor  $\mathcal{c}^s\rightarrow \mathcal{c}$  and  $\mathbf{right}[f,g]$  is a functor  $\mathcal{c}^{-\bullet s}\rightarrow \mathcal{c}$ , where  $u\bullet v_{1}\ldots v_{n}\stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=}$

$$
u \bullet v _ {1} \dots u \bullet v _ {n}.
$$

hence, we come to the definition of cdt declarations.

definition 3.2.1: in cdt theory, there are two forms of declaring new functors. one is to define a functor  $l \colon \mathcal{c}^s \to \mathcal{c}$  by

left object  $l(\vec{x})$  with  $\psi$  is

$$
\vec {\alpha}: \vec {f} (l, \vec {x}) \rightarrow \vec {g} (l, \vec {x}) \quad (*)
$$

end object

and the other is to define a functor  $r\colon \mathcal{c}^{-\bullet s}\to \mathcal{c}$  by

right object  $r(\vec{x})$  with  $\psi$  is

$$
\vec {\alpha}: \vec {f} (r, \vec {x}) \rightarrow \vec {g} (r, \vec {x})
$$

end object

where  $\vec{x}$  is a sequence  $\langle x_1, \ldots, x_n \rangle$  of variables,  $\psi$  is the associated factorizer,  $\vec{\alpha}$  is a sequence  $\langle \alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_m \rangle$  of the associated natural transformations, and  $\vec{f}$  and  $\vec{g}$  are sequences  $\langle f_1, \ldots, f_m \rangle$  and  $\langle g_1, \ldots, g_m \rangle$ , respectively, of functors which we have as primitives or we have already defined and whose type is  $f_i: \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}^s \to \mathcal{c}$  and  $g_i: \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}^{-\bullet s} \to \mathcal{c}$ , respectively. semantically,  $l$  is  $\mathbf{left}[\vec{f}, \vec{g}]$  and  $r$  is  $\mathbf{right}[\vec{f}, \vec{g}]$ . we may call  $l$  left functor or left object and  $r$  right functor or right object.

if we expand the definition of  $\mathbf{left}$ ,  $(\ast)$  defines for any  $\mathcal{c}$  objects  $\vec{a} = \langle a_1, \ldots, a_n \rangle$  an object  $l(\vec{a})$  and a morphism

$$
\vec {f} (l (\vec {a}), \vec {a}) \xrightarrow {\vec {\alpha} _ {\vec {a}}} \vec {g} (l (\vec {a}), \vec {a}),
$$

and for any object  $b$  and a sequence of morphisms  $\vec{f} \colon \vec{f}(b, \vec{a}) \to \vec{g}(b, \vec{a})$ ,  $\psi(\vec{f})$  denotes the unique morphism which makes the following diagram commute.

![](images/e489219ca7bd3a8074e691445dd5c5e0cf5b3e5dc41c984f1df488697b0594be.jpg)

in definition 3.2.1, it is not immediately clear what kind of  $\vec{f}$  and  $\vec{g}$  is allowed. we vaguely stated that they are primitive or have been defined already. in order

to clarify this point, we go back to csl and regard a cdt declaration as an extension of a given csl signature.

definition 3.2.2: let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  be a csl signature. a cdt declaration  $d \in \mathbf{decl}$  is given by the following bnf expression.

$$
\begin{array}{l} d := \left\{\text {l e f t} \mid \text {r i g h t} \right\} \text {o b j e c t} f \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \text {w i t h} \psi \text {i s} \\ \alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} \to e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \\ \begin{array}{c c c} \cdot & \cdot & \cdot \\ \cdot & \cdot & \cdot \end{array} \\ \alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime} \\ \mathrm {e n d o b j e c t} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $f$  is a new functor symbol,  $\psi$  is a new factorizer symbol,  $\alpha_{1},\ldots ,a_{m}$  are new natural transformation symbols, and  $e_{i}$  and  $e_i^\prime$ $(i = 1,\dots,m)$  are well-formed functorial expressions (under this signature  $\langle \gamma ,\delta ,\psi \rangle$  ) whose variables are  $x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n}$  and  $f$  (here we use  $f$  as a formal parameter like we use its function name inside a function body in pascal).

we need to put restriction on the variance of  $f$  in the functorial expressions such that for each  $i = 1, \ldots, m$

1. the variance of  $f$  in  $e_{i}$  should be either covariant or free,  
2. the variance of  $f$  in  $e_{i}^{\prime}$  should also be either covariant or free, and  
3. either the variance of  $f$  in  $e_{i}$  should be covariant or that in  $e_{i}'$  should be covariant.

we could have allowed  $f$  to be contravariant (as indeed the original definition of cdt did), but it turned out that the generality by contravariance was of very little use, so, because it simplifies the following argument, we restrict ourselves only to covariant functors. the third condition above is to make the extension consistent (each  $\alpha_{i}$  should somehow relate to the functor we are declaring).

let us calculate the variance of  $f$ . if the variance of  $\lambda(f, x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ .  $e_i$  is  $v_i s_i$  ( $v_i \in \mathbf{var}$  for the variance of  $f$  and  $s_i \in \mathbf{var}^*$  for the variance of  $x_1, \ldots, x_n$ ) and that of  $\lambda(f, x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ .  $e_i'$  is  $v_i' s_i'$ ,  $\lambda(f, x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ .  $e_i$  denotes a functor  $\mathcal{c}^{v_i} \times \mathcal{c}^{s_i} \to \mathcal{c}$  and  $\lambda(f, x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ .  $e_i'$  denotes a functor  $\mathcal{c}^{v_i'} \times \mathcal{c}^{s_i'} \to \mathcal{c}$ . the restriction above states that  $v_i \sqcup v_i' = +$ . from proposition 3.1.4, the variance of  $f$  in case that it is declared by a left cdt declaration should be

$$
\bigsqcup_ {i = 1} ^ {m} s _ {i} \sqcup - \bullet s _ {i} ^ {\prime}, \tag {*}
$$

and in case that it is declared by a right one, the variance should be

$$
\bigsqcup_ {i = 1} ^ {m} - \bullet s _ {i} \sqcup s _ {i} ^ {\prime}, \tag {**}
$$

where  $\mathbf{var}^*$  is a partially ordered set with the ordering given by  $u_1 \ldots u_n \sqsubseteq v_1 \ldots v_n$  if and only if  $u_1 \sqsubseteq v_1, \ldots$  and  $u_n \sqsubseteq v_n$ .

definition 3.2.2 (continued): a cdt declaration gives an extension of csl signature.

$$
\langle \gamma , \delta , \psi \rangle \hookrightarrow \langle \gamma \cup \{f \}, \delta \cup \{\alpha_ {1}, \dots , \alpha_ {m} \}, \psi \cup \{\psi \} \rangle
$$

where the variance of  $f$  is given by  $(\ast)$  or  $(\ast \ast)$ , the type of  $\alpha_{i}$  is

$$
\lambda (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}). e _ {i} [ f (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) / f ] \dot {\rightarrow} \lambda (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}). e _ {i} ^ {\prime} [ f (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) / f ],
$$

and the type of  $\psi$  is

$$
\begin{array}{c} f _ {i} \colon \lambda (x, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}). e _ {i} [ x / f ] \to \lambda (x, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}). e _ {i} ^ {\prime} [ x / f ] \quad (i = 1, \ldots , m) \\ \hline \psi (f _ {1}, \ldots , f _ {m}) \colon \lambda (x, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}). f (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \to \lambda (x, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}). x \end{array}
$$

by a left cdt declaration and

$$
\frac {f _ {i} \colon \lambda (x , x _ {1} , \ldots , x _ {n}) . e _ {i} [ x / f ] \to \lambda (x , x _ {1} , \ldots , x _ {n}) . e _ {i} ^ {\prime} [ x / f ] \quad (i = 1 , \ldots , m)}{\psi (f _ {1} , \ldots , f _ {m}) \colon \lambda (x , x _ {1} , \ldots , x _ {n}) . x \to \lambda (x , x _ {1} , \ldots , x _ {n}) . f (x _ {1} , \ldots , x _ {n})}
$$

by a right one.

we will see in section 3.4 a cdt declaration as a csl theory extension so that the semantics of a cdt declaration can be given by a csl structure.

# 3.3 examples of categorical data types

in this section, we will present several examples of categorical data types declared by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {l e f t o b j e c t} f \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \text {w i t h} \psi \text {i s} \\ \begin{array}{c} \alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} \to e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \\ \dots \end{array} \tag {*} \\ \alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime} \\ \mathrm {e n d o b j e c t} \\ \end{array}
$$

for left objects and by

$$
\mathrm {r i g h t o b j e c t} f (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \mathrm {w i t h} \psi \mathrm {i s}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} \to e _ {1} ^ {\prime}
$$

中

$$
\alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime}
$$

end object

$(\ast \ast)$

for right objects.

# 3.3.1 terminal and initial objects

let us start with an empty csl signature  $\langle \emptyset, \emptyset, \emptyset \rangle$ . the simplest case of (*) and  $(**)$  is when  $n = m = 0$ . if we consider the case when  $n = m = 0$  in  $(**)$ , we get the declaration of the terminal object.

right object 1 with!

end object

(we omitted the keyword 'is' to make the declaration look nicer.) from the definition, this defines an object '1' and for any object  $a$  there is a unique morphism '!' from  $a$  to '1'.

![](images/fc30923638c5255865b0713466c43b34fd11b7b9723b4ca3c31292fe5c8208aa.jpg)

therefore, it really is the terminal object.

dually, if we change the keyword 'right' to 'left' in the definition of the terminal object, we get the definition of the initial object.

left object 0 with !!

end object

the factorizer  $\mathbf{\omega}!!$  gives a unique morphism from  $0^{\prime}$  to any object  $a$ .

![](images/59fca69e2031523d1ec2b0276c88da059d5f9433103b882f37ed38b9095a81c5.jpg)

# 3.3.2 products and coproducts

next, we define products and coproducts. the binary product functor can be declared as the following right object.

right object  $\operatorname {prod}(x,y)$  with pair is pi1:prod  $\rightarrow x$  pi2:prod  $\rightarrow y$    
end object

from definition 3.2.2, this defines a functor symbol 'prod' whose variance is  $++$  (i.e. covariant in both arguments), two natural transformation symbols 'pi1' and 'pi2' whose types are

$$
\operatorname {p i l}: \lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, y) \rightarrow \lambda (x, y). x
$$

$$
\operatorname {p i 2}: \lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, y) \stackrel {{\cdot}} {{\to}} \lambda (x, y). y
$$

and a factorizer symbol 'pair' whose type is

$$
\frac {f \colon \lambda (z , x , y) . z \to \lambda (z , x , y) . x \qquad g \colon \lambda (z , x , y) . z \to \lambda (z , x , y) . y}{\operatorname {p a i r} (f , g) \colon \lambda (z , x , y) . z \to \lambda (z , x , y) . \operatorname {p r o d} (x , y)}.
$$

if we write this down in a more understandable way, it becomes the familiar definition of the binary product, that is 'prod' has two unit morphisms

$$
a \xleftarrow {\mathrm {p i 1}} \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b) \xrightarrow {\mathrm {p i 2}} b
$$

and  $\operatorname{pair}(f, g)$  gives the unique morphism for any morphisms  $f: c \to a$  and  $g: c \to b$  such that the following diagram commutes.

$$
a \xleftarrow { \begin{array}{c} \operatorname {p i 1} \\ \operatorname {p r o d} (a, b) \end{array} } b
$$

note that the cdt declaration of the binary product functor is very similar to the 'prod' theory in clear defined in 3.1. one of the differences is that in clear 'pair' is treated as a function in the same class as 'pi1' and 'pi2' but in cdt 'pair' is quite different from 'pi1' and 'pi2'. another one is that in clear

'prod' is declared as the initial algebra so it is close to cdt's left object but in cdt 'prod' is naturally a right object because the product functor is the right adjoint of the diagonal functor.

dually, we can define the binary coproduct functor as a left object.

left object coprod  $(x,y)$  with case is in1:  $x\to$  coprod in2:  $y\rightarrow$  coprod   
end object

again, this declaration looks very close to the one in clear (defined in section 3.1), but note that we have 'case' in cdt so that we can use it to write programs or to specify some properties.

just writing the situation as a diagram,

![](images/c33ec160215a1b768cd398ffab5ebc35ce3c33b0f408412a876fe3f801c352c8.jpg)

# 3.3.3 exponentials

one of the objections against algebraic specification methods is that it cannot handle function spaces. cdt's declaration mechanism looks very close to that of algebraic specification methods, but cdt is based on category theory not on many-sorted algebras, and in category theory function spaces can be defined as exponentials. for objects  $a$  and  $b$ , the exponential of  $b$  by  $a$  is written as  $\exp(a, b)^5$  satisfies the following natural isomorphism.

$$
