\operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (\operatorname {p r o d} (c, a), b) \simeq \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (c, \exp (a, b))
$$

in other words, the functor  $\exp(a, \cdot)$  is the right adjoint of  $\operatorname{prod}(\cdot, a)$ . we write 'curry' for the factorizer and 'eval' for the counit natural transformation. then, for any object  $c$  and any morphism  $f \colon \operatorname{prod}(c, a) \to b$ ,  $\operatorname{curry}(f)$  is the

unique morphism from  $\exp (a,b)$  to  $c$  such that the following diagram commutes.

![](images/a79c46108c98cabc5e89e9d14a083fe50b6821b600ea0754e46e4f622ae5e540.jpg)

the reason why the exponentials are function spaces is that their global elements are just morphisms.

$$
\operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (1, \exp (a, b)) \simeq \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (\operatorname {p r o d} (1, a), b) \simeq \operatorname {h o m} _ {\mathcal {c}} (a, b)
$$

let us write down the definition as a cdt declaration. assume a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  which contains the definition of the binary product functor as we defined in subsection 3.3.2. then, the exponential functor can be declared as follows.

right object  $\exp (x,y)$  with curry is eval:prod  $(\exp ,x)\to y$    
end object

this is so far the most complicated cdt declaration. in the previous examples, functorial expressions  $e_{i}$  and  $e_{i}^{\prime}$  in  $(\ast)$  and  $(\ast\ast)$  are all simply variables. from definition 3.2.2, the cdt declaration above defines a functor symbol 'exp' of type  $-+$ , a natural transformation 'eval' of type

$$
\lambda (x, y). \operatorname {p r o d} (\exp (x, y), x) \stackrel {\cdot} {\to} \lambda (x, y). y
$$

and a factorizer 'curry' of type

$$
\frac {f \colon \lambda (z , x , y) . \operatorname {p r o d} (z , x) \to \lambda (z , x , y) . y}{\operatorname {c u r r y} (f) \colon \lambda (z , x , y) . z \to \lambda (z , x , y) . \exp (x , y)}.
$$

these types are what we expect them to be from the exponential adjunction. let us once more convince ourselves that the semantics by  $f, g$ -dialgebras really defines the exponentials.  $\langle \exp(a, b), \operatorname{eval}_{a, b} \rangle$  is the final  $f, g$ -dialgebra where  $f(c) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \operatorname{prod}(c, a)$  and  $g(c) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} b$ . this means that, for any  $\langle c, f \rangle$  where  $c$  is an object and  $f$  is a morphism of  $f(c) \to g(c)$ ,  $\operatorname{curry}(f)$  is the unique morphism of

$$
\langle \exp (a, b), \operatorname {e v a l} _ {a, b} \rangle \rightarrow \langle c, f \rangle .
$$

from definition 3.1.2 of  $f, g$ -dialgebras,  $\mathrm{curry}(f)$  is the unique morphism  $c \to \exp(a, b)$  which makes the following diagram commute.

$$
\begin{array}{c} f (c) = \operatorname {p r o d} (c, a) \xrightarrow {f} b = g (c) \\ f (\operatorname {c u r r y} (f)) = \operatorname {p r o d} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (f), \mathbf {i}) \\ f (\exp (a, b)) = \operatorname {p r o d} (\exp (a, b), a) \xrightarrow [ \operatorname {e v a l} _ {a , b} ]{\|} b = g (\exp (a, b)) \end{array}
$$

this is exactly the condition of 'exp' being the exponential functor.

the declaration of the exponential functor in cdt very much looks like a declaration in a algebraic specification language (e.g. in clear), but, as is well-known, we cannot define function spaces as algebras. the essential difference lies in that 'exp' is a right object, in other words, defined by the terminal data constraint rather than the initial one which clear uses and in the availability of the factorizer 'curry'. if we define 'curry' as an ordinary function (or an ml function), its type is

$$
(c \times a \rightarrow b) \rightarrow (c \rightarrow (a \rightarrow b))
$$

and this could never be a type of algebraic functions (i.e. functions defined by algebraic specification methods).

# 3.3.4 natural number object

as we have already shown that the natural number object can be given by 'left', let us write it down as a cdt declaration. although we can define the natural number object if we have only the terminal object, it is often very convenient to assume that a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  contains not only the terminal object but also the product functor and the exponential functor. the declaration of the natural number object as a cdt is

left object nat with pr is

zero:  $1\to \mathrm{nat}$

succ: nat  $\rightarrow$  nat

end object

which defines a constant functor (i.e. an object) ‘nat’ with two morphisms ‘zero’ and ‘succ’.

![](images/c891355f6480601f46d97bd0f21a219aa4cf7e52242e7cdbb24f6d7572a14bd6.jpg)

in addition, the factorizer 'pr' (standing for primitive recursion) gives for any morphisms  $f \colon 1 \to a$  and  $g \colon a \to a$  a unique morphism  $\operatorname{pr}(f, g)$ : nat  $\to a$  such that the following diagram commutes.

![](images/5cf24abe90c7f30c159584b9a2066ca453a1a84793a64e0bbed534dbbcd92ad6.jpg)

as is well-known (e.g. [goldblatt 79] chapter 13), 'pr' provides us to define any primitive recursive function.

definition 3.3.1: for a category  $\mathcal{c}$  with the natural number object 'nat', the terminal object '1' and the binary product functor 'prod', a morphism  $f$  is primitive recursive (on natural numbers) if it can be generated after finitely many steps by means of the following rules:

1.  $f = \mathbf{i}_{\mathrm{nat}}$  : nat  $\rightarrow$  nat,  
2.  $f = \mathrm{zero:}1\to \mathrm{nat}$  
3.  $f = \mathrm{succ}$  :nat  $\rightarrow$  nat,  
4.  $f = \mathrm{pi}1$ : prod(nat, nat)  $\rightarrow$  nat,  
5.  $f = \mathrm{pi2}$  : prod(nat, nat)  $\rightarrow$  nat,  
6.  $f = g \circ \operatorname{pair}(h, k) \colon a \to \mathrm{nat}$  for primitive recursive morphisms

$g\colon \operatorname {prod}(\operatorname {nat},\operatorname {nat})\to \operatorname {nat}$ $h\colon a\to \mathrm{nat}$  and  $k\colon a\to \mathrm{nat}$

7.  $f = g\circ \operatorname {prod}(h,k)\colon \operatorname {prod}(a,b)\to \mathrm{nat}$  for primitive recursive morphisms  
$g\colon \operatorname {prod}(\operatorname {nat},\operatorname {nat})\to \operatorname {nat}$ $h\colon a\to \mathrm{nat}$  and  $k\colon b\to \mathrm{nat}$  ,and  
8.  $f\colon \operatorname {prod}(\operatorname {nat},a)\to \operatorname {nat}$  satisfying

(a)  $f\circ \mathrm{pair(zero}\circ !,\mathbf{i}) = g$  , and  
(b)  $f\circ \mathrm{pair}(\mathrm{suc}\circ \mathrm{pi1},\mathrm{pi2}) = h\circ \mathrm{pair}(f,\mathbf{i})$

for primitive recursive  $g \colon a \to \mathrm{nat}$  and  $h \colon \operatorname{prod}(\operatorname{nat}, \operatorname{prod}(\operatorname{nat}, a)) \to \operatorname{nat}$ .

this is a straight copy of the standard definition of primitive recursive functions on natural numbers.

proposition 3.3.2: if a cartesian closed category  $\mathcal{c}$  has the natural number object, it has all the primitive recursive morphisms.

proof: it is suffice to show that there exists a morphism  $f \colon \operatorname{prod}(\mathrm{nat}, a) \to \mathrm{nat}$  for any morphisms  $g \colon a \to \mathrm{nat}$  and  $h \colon \operatorname{prod}(\mathrm{nat}, \operatorname{prod}(\mathrm{nat}, a)) \to \mathrm{nat}$  such that

1.  $f\circ$  pair(zero  $\circ !,\mathbf{i}) = g$  , and  
2.  $f\circ \mathrm{pair}(\mathrm{suc}\circ \mathrm{pi1},\mathrm{pi2}) = h\circ \mathrm{pair}(f,\mathbf{i}).$

there is a morphism  $k \colon \mathrm{nat} \to \operatorname{prod}(\exp(a, \mathrm{nat}), \mathrm{nat})$  such that the following diagram commutes.

![](images/8aefbb2e84c04f200ebf2750e8df7006b86993e51ebe5fe4cfe1414dcd581109.jpg)

where  $g^{\prime}$  and  $h^\prime$  are

$$
g ^ {\prime} \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (g \circ \mathrm {p i} 2), \text {z e r o})
$$

$$
h ^ {\prime} \stackrel {{\text {d e f}}} {{=}} \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (h \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {p i} 2,
$$

$$
\left. \begin{array}{c} \text {p i l} \circ \text {p i l}), \end{array} \right.
$$

$$
\left. \left. \mathrm {p i} 2 \circ \mathrm {p i} 1\right)\right),
$$

$$
\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {p i} 2).
$$

therefore,  $k$  is  $\operatorname{pr}(g', h')$ . then,  $f \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \operatorname{eval} \circ \operatorname{prod}(\operatorname{pi1} \circ k, \mathbf{i})$ . we can easily show that this is what we wanted.

for example, the morphism 'add' corresponding to the addition function of natural numbers can be given as

$$
\mathrm {a d d} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \operatorname {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p r o d} (\operatorname {p r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2), \operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {e v a l})), \mathbf {i}).
$$

it corresponds to the following usual definition of 'add'.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {a d d} (0, y) = y \\ \operatorname {a d d} (x + 1, y) = \operatorname {a d d} (x, y) + 1 \\ \end{array}
$$

furthermore, we can easily prove categorically that 'add' satisfies familiar laws like commutativity (categorically add  $\circ$  pair(pi2, pi1) = add) and so on.

# 3.3.5 lists

we have defined the data type of natural numbers in the previous subsection. another algebraic data type which is often used in programming is the data type of lists. in cdt, the data type of lists is defined as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{c} \text {l e f t o b j e c t l i s t} (x) \text {w i t h p r l i s} \\ \quad \text {n i l : 1 \to l i s t} \\ \quad \text {c o n s : p r o d} (x, \text {l i s t}) \to \text {l i s t} \\ \text {e n d o b j e c t} \end{array}
$$

we needed to assume a csl signature having the terminal object and the product functor. the declaration above defines a one argument covariant functor 'list', two natural transformations

$$
\operatorname {n i l}: \lambda (x). 1 \dot {\to} \lambda (x). \operatorname {l i s t} (x) \quad \text {a n d} \quad \operatorname {c o n s}: \lambda (x). \operatorname {p r o d} (x, \operatorname {l i s t} (x)) \dot {\to} \lambda (x). \operatorname {l i s t} (x)
$$

and a factorizer 'prl' (standing for primitive recursion on list) whose type is

$$
\frac {f \colon \lambda (y , x) . 1 \to \lambda (y , x) . y \qquad g \colon \lambda (y , x) . \mathrm {p r o d} (x , y) \to \lambda (y , x) . y}{\operatorname {p r l} (f , g) \colon \lambda (y , x) . \mathrm {l i s t} (x) \to \lambda (y , x) . y}.
$$

as usual, we can express the situation as a diagram.

![](images/8726c06c5c37ba80f39297ce4570c372a4228694d1bd24427760ae69370f13ec.jpg)

a global element of  $\operatorname{list}(a)$  is normally constructed from 'nil' and 'cons'. for example,  $\operatorname{list}(\mathrm{nat})$  has

$$
\operatorname {c o n s} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}, \operatorname {c o n s} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}, \operatorname {n i l}))
$$

as a global element (in plain words, this element is the list of 1 and 2). 'nil' and 'cons' are usually called constructors of 'list'. we can see in general to define an algebraic cdt by listing its constructors. destructors are defined using factorizers. in the case of 'list', 'hd' (head) and 'tl' (tail) can be defined as follows.

$$
\mathrm {h d} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \operatorname {p r l} (\mathrm {i n} 2, \mathrm {i n} 1 \circ \mathrm {p i} 1)
$$

$$
\mathrm {t l} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \operatorname {c o p r o d} (\mathrm {p i} 2, \mathbf {i}) \circ \operatorname {p r l} (\mathrm {i n} 2, \mathrm {i n} 1 \circ \operatorname {p r o d} (\mathbf {i}, \operatorname {c a s e} (\operatorname {c o n s}, \mathrm {n i l})))
$$

note that we have to define 'hd' and 'tl' as total functions (in a sense). the type of 'hd' is  $\operatorname{list}(a) \to \operatorname{coprod}(a, 1)$  and is not  $\operatorname{list}(a) \to a$ . the type of 'tl' is also  $\operatorname{list}(a) \to \operatorname{coprod}(\operatorname{list}(a), 1)$ . the type '1' is for error (like  $\bot$  in a domain) and, for example,  $\operatorname{hd} \circ \operatorname{nil} = \operatorname{in2}$ .

as 'list' is a covariant functor, for a morphism  $f \colon a \to b$  list  $(f)$ : list  $(a) \to \operatorname{list}(b)$  transforms a list of  $a$  elements to a list of  $b$  elements by applying  $f$  to each element. for example, list(succ): list(nat) → list(nat) increments every element in a given list by one. in general, we have the following equations:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {l i s t} (f) = \operatorname {p r l} (\operatorname {n i l}, \operatorname {c o n s} \circ \operatorname {p r o d} (f, \mathbf {i})) \\ \operatorname {l i s t} (f) \circ \operatorname {n i l} = \operatorname {n i l} \\ \operatorname {l i s t} (f) \circ \operatorname {c o n s} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (x, l) = \operatorname {c o n s} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (f \circ x, \operatorname {l i s t} (f) \circ l) \\ \end{array}
$$

'list' corresponds to 'map' function in ml and 'mapcar' in lisp.

# 3.3.6 final co-algebras (infinite lists and co-natural number object)

the objects we defined in the preceding subsections are all familiar either in category theory or in programming languages. particularly, we have seen in subsection 3.3.4 and 3.3.5 the natural number object and the data type of lists, which are typical initial algebras. recently, several works have been done about final coalgebras, which are the dual of initial algebras (see [arbib and manes 80]). from their symmetry of cdt declarations, we can easily define final coalgebras in cdt as well as initial algebras.

let us dualize the declaration of the natural number object defined in subsec-

tion 3.3.4 by

left object nat with pr is zero:  $1\to \mathrm{nat}$  succ: nat  $\rightarrow$  nat   
end object

if we simply replace 'left' by 'right' and change the direction of arrows, we get

right object conat with copr is  
cozero: nat  $\rightarrow$  1  
cosucc: nat  $\rightarrow$  nat  
end object

unfortunately, this is not an exciting object. we can prove that 'conat' is isomorphic to the terminal object as follows: from the uniqueness of terminal objects up to isomorphism in any category, we simply need to show that '1' is the terminal  $f$ ,  $g$ -dialgebra for these particular  $f$  and  $g$ , that is, for any object  $a$  and morphisms  $f\colon a\to 1$  and  $g\colon a\to a$ , there exists a unique morphism  $h\colon a\to 1$  such that the following diagram commutes.

![](images/d9c4f35d6057e024005be8fc20f47ee53afc48ffb108411c156a1bbe26ed9590.jpg)

indeed, we have this unique morphism  $a \to 1$  because '1' is the terminal object and the above diagram trivially commutes.

although the exact dual of the natural number object is not an interesting thing, we can modify it to get a cdt data type of infinite lists.

right object inflist  $(x)$  with fold is head: inflist  $\rightarrow x$  tail: inflist  $\rightarrow$  inflist   
end object

the diagram of explaining 'inlist' is

![](images/edc9cf8e98d538cec4e3e2c175c969ac4bba0fc8c63628c067fc4a2e70a80c5f.jpg)

since the functor 'inlist' is not so familiar in category theory or in conventional programming languages, let us find out what it is in the category of sets. we expect it to be a set of infinite lists in some sense.

proposition 3.3.3: in the category of sets, for a set  $a$ , inflist(a) is the following set of  $\omega$ -infinite sequences of elements in  $a$ .

$$
\left\{\left(x _ {0}, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}, \dots\right) \mid x _ {i} \in a \right\}
$$

proof: we define 'head' and 'tail' as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {h e a d} \left(\left(x _ {0}, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}, \dots\right)\right) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} x _ {0} \\ \operatorname {t a i l} \left(\left(x _ {0}, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}, \dots\right)\right) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left(x _ {1}, x _ {2}, \dots , x _ {n + 1}, \dots\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

let  $\operatorname{fold}(f, g)(x)$  be a sequence  $(h_0(x), h_1(x), \ldots, h_n(x), \ldots)$  for functions  $f: b \to a$  and  $g: b \to b$ . the commutativity of the diagram above forces the following equations.

$$
\begin{array}{l} h _ {0} (x) = f (x) \\ \left(h _ {1} (x), h _ {2} (x), \dots , h _ {n + 1} (x), \dots\right) = \left(h _ {0} (x), h _ {1} (x), \dots , h _ {n} (x), \dots\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore,  $\operatorname{fold}(f,g)(x)$  is uniquely determined as

$$
\operatorname {f o l d} (f, g) (x) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} (f (x), f (g (x)), \dots , f (g ^ {n} (x)), \dots)
$$

hence, at least in the category of sets,  $\operatorname{inflist}(a)$  is really the data type of infinite lists of  $a$  elements.

more generally,

proposition 3.3.4: in a cartesian closed category  $\mathcal{c}$  with the natural number object,  $\operatorname{inflist}(a)$  is isomorphic to  $\exp(\operatorname{nat}, a)$ .

proof: let us define  $h \colon \exp(\operatorname{nat}, a) \to \operatorname{inflist}(a)$  to be the fill-in morphism of the following diagram.

![](images/06f5eb9a17e12ceec1cc4f39f4f9a4cb4a75892a6dff42af4d44973167f8cd5e.jpg)

where 'head' and 'tail' are

head' def eval  $\circ$  pair(i, zero o!).

$\mathrm{tail}' \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathrm{curry}(\mathrm{eval} \circ \mathrm{prod}(\mathbf{i}, \mathrm{succ}))$ .

we define  $h^\prime \colon \operatorname {inflist}(a)\to \exp (\operatorname {nat},a)$  to be

$$
h ^ {\prime} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {p r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {h e a d} \circ \operatorname {p i 2}), \exp (\operatorname {t a i l}, \mathbf {i})) \circ \operatorname {p i 2}, \operatorname {p i 1})).
$$

after some calculation, we can show that  $h \circ h' = \mathbf{i}$  and  $h' \circ h = \mathbf{i}$ , so  $\operatorname{inflist}(a)$  is isomorphic to  $\exp(\operatorname{nat}, a)$ .

this proposition tells us that

$$
\operatorname {i n f l i s t} (a) \cong a \times a \times \dots \times a \times \dots \cong \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {\infty} a.
$$

indeed, this is the dual of  $\sum_{i=1}^{\infty} a$ , the special case of which is the natural number object,  $\operatorname{nat} \cong \sum_{i=1}^{\infty} 1$ .

we started this subsection by considering the dual of the natural number object. it led us to the cdt data type of infinite lists. we still have a different question whether there is a final coalgebra which resembles a data type of natural numbers. the answer is yes. the following right object defines a cdt data type of natural numbers plus alpha.

right object conat with copr is

pred: conat  $\rightarrow$  coprod(1, conat)

end object

the situation can be written as a diagram

![](images/b7dff33d675c148cd1a12c3c2b1551c63f832eb46c4c4a3af2e3f4c291a0dee9.jpg)

the natural transformation 'pred' is the predecessor function and there is a morphism from 'nat' to 'conat' given by

$$
\operatorname {c o p r} (\operatorname {p r} (\text {i n 1}, \text {i n 2} \circ \text {c a s e (z e r o , s u c c)})))
$$

which we expect to be injective, but so far the author has been able neither to prove it nor to give a counter example. note that there is always a morphism from the left object to its corresponding right object. 'conat' has an interesting extra element, namely the infinity  $(\infty)$ . the ground element to denote it is

$$
\operatorname {i n f i n i t y} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \operatorname {c o p r} (\operatorname {i n 2})
$$

it is easy to prove that the predecessor of the infinity is itself (i.e. pred  $\circ$  infinity  $=$  in2  $\circ$  infinity).

in the category of sets, 'conat' is really the set of natural numbers and the infinity.

proposition 3.3.5: in set, 'conat' is 'nat  $\cup \{\infty\} '$ .

proof: the predecessor function is defined as usual. roughly speaking, for any function  $f \colon a \to \operatorname{coprod}(1, a)$ ,  $\operatorname{copr}(f)(x)$  is the number of applications of  $f$  to  $x$  to get the element of 1

$$
\operatorname {c o p r} (f) (x) = n \qquad \text {w h e r e} \quad f ^ {n} (x) \in 1,
$$

and, if it never results in the element of 1,  $\operatorname{copr}(f)(x) = \infty$ . we can easily show that this is the unique function which makes the 'conat' diagram commute.

therefore, in the category of sets, 'conat' is isomorphic to 'nat', but this is not the case for all the categories. there are some categories where 'conat' and 'nat' are not isomorphic.

proposition 3.3.6: in category trf of subsets of natural numbers as objects and total recursive functions as morphisms, there exists the natural number object but does not exist the co-natural number object.

proof: trf's terminal object, initial object, product functor and coproduct

functor are all the same as those of set. for example, injections 'in1' and 'in2' for coprod  $(a,b)$  are trivially total and recursive, and for any two total recursive functions  $f\colon a\to c$  and  $g\colon b\to c$  case  $(f,g)$  is also total recursive function. we can write it down as a kind of program.

$$
\begin{array}{c} \text {c a s e} (f, g) (x) \stackrel {{\text {d e f}}} {{=}} \text {i f} x \in a \text {t h e n} f (x) \\ \text {e l s e} g (x) \end{array}
$$

the natural number object 'nat' in trf is also the ordinary set of natural numbers. 'zero' and 'succ' are total recursive functions from the very definition of recursive functions and for  $f \colon 1 \to a$  and  $g \colon a \to a$  of total recursive functions,  $\operatorname{pr}(f, g)$  can be written as the following program.

$$
\begin{array}{c} \operatorname {p r} (f, g) (n) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \text {i f} n = 0 \text {t h e n} f () \\ \text {e l s e} g (\operatorname {p r} (f, g) (n - 1)) \end{array}
$$

which defines a total recursive function.

however, we cannot have the co-natural number object in trf. the program of  $\operatorname{copr}(f)$  for a total recursive function  $f\colon a\to \operatorname{coprod}(1,a)$  can only be

$$
\begin{array}{c} \operatorname {c o p r} (f) (x) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \text {i f} f (x) \in 1 \text {t h e n} 0 \\ \text {e l s e} \operatorname {c o p r} (f) (f (x)) + 1 \end{array}
$$

which is recursive but not total.  $\square$

there is also a category which has both 'nat' and 'conat' and in which they are non-isomorphic. we will show this in chapter 4.

from the point of view of finding fixed points of functors, 'nat' is the initial fixed point of  $f(x) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} 1 + x$  and 'conat' is the final fixed point of the same functor.

# 3.3.7 automata

the declarations of initial algebras and final coalgebras do not use the full power of the cdt declaration mechanism. their unit and counit natural transformations always have the form

$$
\alpha \colon e \to f
$$

for initial algebras and have the form

$$
\alpha \colon f \to e
$$

for final coalgebras, where  $e$  is any functorial expression but  $f$  is a variable (more specially, the variable which denotes the object we declare). therefore, all

we are doing is just listing constructors for initial algebras and listing destructors for final coalgebras. we will see what kinds of form define sensible functors in section 3.5. so far, the only exception was the exponentials. we will see another example in this subsection.

one of the interesting applications of category theory to computer science is to automata theory. [arbib and manes 75] presents the category  $\mathbf{dyn}(i)$  of  $i$ -dynamics whose object is

$$
q \times i \xrightarrow {\delta} q,
$$

where  $q$  is the set of states,  $i$  is the set of inputs and  $\delta$  is a dynamics which is a function determining the next state of the automaton according to the current state and input.

from this, we can construct a categorical data type of automata.

right object  $\mathrm{dyn}(i)$  with univ is next:  $\operatorname {prod}(\operatorname {dyn},i)\to \operatorname {dyn}$  end object

note that our  $\mathrm{dyn}(i)$  for an object  $i$  is just an object; it is not a category like  $\mathbf{dyn}(i)$  is. the diagram which explains this right object is

$$
\begin{array}{c} \operatorname {p r o d} (q, i) \xrightarrow {\delta} q \\ | \\ | \\ \operatorname {p r o d} (\operatorname {u n i v} (\delta), i) \downarrow \\ | \\ \downarrow \\ \operatorname {p r o d} (\operatorname {d y n} (i), i) \xrightarrow [ \text {n e x t} ]{\mathrm {d y n}} \end{array}
$$

for any dynamics  $\delta$ :  $\operatorname{prod}(q, i) \to q$  and an initial state  $q_0: 1 \to q$ , we get an automaton

$$
1 \xrightarrow {\mathrm {u n i v} (\delta) \circ q _ {0}} \mathrm {d y n} (i)
$$

as a global element of  $\mathrm{dyn}(i)$ . though we can put this automaton into the next state by applying 'next', we are never ever able to see its behaviour from the outside. moreover, because of this non-observability, we can easily prove that  $\mathrm{dyn}(i)$  is in fact isomorphic to the terminal object. in order to make 'dyn' a more sensible object, we need to add an output function. the new categorical

data type of automata is

right object  $\mathrm{dyn}'(i,o)$  with univ' is next:prod  $(\mathrm{dyn}',i)\to \mathrm{dyn}'$  output':  $\mathrm{dyn}'\rightarrow o$    
end object

for any dynamics  $\delta$ :  $\operatorname{prod}(q, i) \to q$ , any output function  $\beta\colon q \to o$  and an initial state  $q_0\colon 1 \to q$ , we have a global element in  $\mathrm{dyn}'(i, o)$

$$
1 \xrightarrow {\operatorname {u n i v} ^ {\prime} (\delta , \beta) \circ q _ {0}} \operatorname {d y n} ^ {\prime} (i, o).
$$

we can obtain its next state by applying 'next' and its output by 'output'. in addition, the following proposition holds.

proposition 3.3.7: in a cartesian closed category, the categorical data type of moore automata,  $\mathrm{dyn}'(i,o)$ , is isomorphic to  $\exp (\operatorname {list}(i),o)$ .

proof: by defining two morphisms between them and proving that they form an isomorphism.  $\square$

# 3.3.8 obscure categorical data types

we have defined more or less familiar data types as categorical data types in the preceding subsections. one might ask whether cdt can define any data types which are unable to be defined in other languages or methods. the data type of automata is such an example and we can invent similar examples more, but still they are familiar (or we are just trying to express our familiar data types in cdt). in fact, cdt allows us very obscure data types, some of which may not be conceptualized in the human brain (at least not in the author's brain).

from the prime requirement of cdt, it can define right and left adjoint functors of any existing functors, and in subsection 3.3.5, we defined 'list' as a covariant functor, so that we can declare its left and right adjoint functors in cdt as follows.

left objectladjlist(x)with  $\psi$  is  $\alpha \colon x\to \operatorname {list}(\operatorname {ladjlist})$    
end object

right object  $\mathrm{dyn}^{\prime}(i,o)$  with univ' is next': prod(dyn',i)  $\rightarrow$  prod(dyn',o) end object

which gave us the categorical data type of mealy automata. the current definition gives us the data type of moore automata.

right object radjlist(x) with  $\psi^\prime$  is

$$
\alpha^ {\prime}: \operatorname {l i s t} (\operatorname {r a d j l i s t}) \to x
$$

end object

some questions arise immediately after defining these data types: are they familiar data types, and are they in any way useful? the answers to the both questions are unfortunately negative. for the left adjoint,

proposition 3.3.8: in a cartesian closed category,  $\text{ladjlist}(a)'$  for any object  $a$  is isomorphic to the initial object.

proof: it is easy to show that the initial object makes the characteristic diagram of 'ladjlist' commute. note that in a cartesian closed category  $\text{list}(0)$  is isomorphic to the terminal object so that the unit morphism of 'ladjlist' is the unique morphism to the terminal object.

the right adjoint functor is more harmful than the left one.

proposition 3.3.9: a cartesian closed category with 'radjlist' degenerates (i.e. all the objects are isomorphic).

proof: we have the following morphism from the initial object.

$$
1 \xrightarrow {\mathrm {n i l}} \operatorname {l i s t} (\operatorname {r a d j l i s t} (0)) \xrightarrow {\alpha^ {\prime}} 0
$$

then, it is easy to show that the terminal object is isomorphic to the initial one. this further implies that any object in the category is isomorphic to the initial object.

$$
a \cong \operatorname {p r o d} (a, 1) \cong \operatorname {p r o d} (a, 0) \cong 0 \quad \mathbb {i}
$$

most of the left and right adjoint functors of conventional data types follow the same pattern as 'list', that is, they are either trivial or destructive, so they are useless.

hence, a natural question to ask ourselves is that what kind of categorical data types are useful. but what is the formal criteria of useful data types? we have not yet defined this. we will come back to this in chapter 4 and see it from a point of view of computability of categorical data types.

# 3.4 semantics of categorical data types

in definition 3.2.2, we associated a cdt declaration to a csl signature extension (an injective morphism in csig). in this section, we will see it as a csl theory extension and give the precise semantics of cdt declarations.

first, from our informal intention of cdt declarations we have to figure out the csl statements which characterize them. a cdt declaration

$$
\text {l e f t o b j e c t} l \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \text {w i t h} \psi \text {i s}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} \to e _ {1} ^ {\prime}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{c c c} \cdot & \cdot & \cdot \\ \cdot & \cdot & \cdot \end{array}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime}
$$

end object

is the syntactic form of defining the functor  $l = \mathbf{left}[\vec{f},\vec{g}]$ , where  $\vec{f}$  and  $\vec{g}$  are corresponding functors for  $e_1,\ldots ,e_m$  and  $e_1',\ldots ,e_m'$ , respectively.  $\langle l(\vec{a}),\vec{\alpha}\rangle$  is the initial object of  $\mathbf{dalg}(\vec{f},\vec{g})$  and  $\psi$  is its mediating morphism, that is, for any morphisms  $\vec{f}\colon \vec{f} (b,\vec{a})\to \vec{g} (b,\vec{a})$ ,  $\psi (\vec{f})$  gives a unique morphism from  $l(\vec{a})$  to  $b$  such that the following diagram commutes.

![](images/19aca78dc07b3aa78f47a4df34da394aa33186d03dabf5a1f8ca1ffa8ee9921f.jpg)

the commutativity of this diagram can be expressed as the following equation:

$$
\vec {g} (\psi (\vec {f}), \vec {a}) \circ \vec {\alpha} = \vec {f} \circ \vec {f} (\psi (\vec {f}), \vec {a}), \tag {*}
$$

and the uniqueness can be expressed as the following conditional equation:

$$
\vec {g} (h, \vec {a}) \circ \vec {\alpha} = \vec {f} \circ \vec {f} (h, \vec {a}) \rightarrow h = \psi (\vec {f}) \tag {**}
$$

the two equations say everything about  $l$ ,  $\vec{\alpha}$  and  $\psi$ . let us now translate them to csl statements in  $\langle \gamma \cup \{l\}, \delta \cup \{\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_m\}, \psi \cup \{\psi\} \rangle$  so as to give complete description of the cdt declaration above in csl. (*) corresponds to the following  $m$  csl equations:

$$
e _ {i} ^ {\prime} \left[ \psi \left(f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}\right) / l \right] \circ \alpha_ {i} = f _ {i} \circ e _ {i} \left[ \psi \left(f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}\right) / l \right] ^ {8} \quad \left(\mathrm {l e q} _ {i}\right)
$$

$(i = 1,\dots ,m)$  and  $(\ast \ast)$  corresponds to the following conditional csl equation.

$$
\begin{array}{l} e _ {1} ^ {\prime} [ g / l ] \circ \alpha_ {1} = f _ {1} \circ e _ {1} [ g / l ] \wedge \dots \\ \wedge e _ {m} ^ {\prime} [ g / l ] \circ \alpha_ {m} = f _ {m} \circ e _ {m} [ g / l ] \rightarrow g = \psi \left(f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}\right) \quad (\text {l c e q}) \\ \end{array}
$$

in addition, we should have a csl equation expressing functors by factorizers and natural transformations. we can extract such an equation from proposition 3.1.4.

$$
\begin{array}{l} l (h _ {1}, \dots , h _ {n}) = \\ \psi \left(e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \left[ h _ {i} / x _ {i} \right] \circ \alpha_ {1} \circ e _ {1} \left[ h _ {i} / x _ {i} \right], \dots , e _ {m} ^ {\prime} \left[ h _ {i} / x _ {i} \right] \circ \alpha_ {m} \circ e _ {m} \left[ h _ {i} / x _ {i} \right]\right) ^ {9} \quad (\mathrm {l f e q}) \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, the semantics of cdt declaration can be given as a csl theory extension as follows.

definition 3.4.1: given a csl theory  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi, \theta \rangle$ , a cdt declaration

$$
\text {l e f t o b j e c t} l \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \text {w i t h} \psi \text {i s}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} \to e _ {1} ^ {\prime}
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime}
$$

end object,

where  $e_{i}$  and  $e_{i}^{\prime}$ $(i = 1,\dots ,m)$  are csl functorial expressions over  $\langle \gamma ,\delta ,\psi \rangle$  whose variables are  $x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n}$  and  $l$ , is associated with a csl theory morphism

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {l} \colon \langle \gamma , \delta , \psi , \theta \rangle \to \langle \gamma \cup \{l \}, \delta \cup \{\alpha_ {1}, \dots , \alpha_ {m} \}, \psi \cup \{\psi \}, \\ \theta \cup \{\mathrm {l e q} _ {1}, \dots , \mathrm {l e q} _ {m}, \mathrm {l c e q}, \mathrm {l f e q} \} \rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

where the types of  $l$ ,  $\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}$  and  $\psi$  are as given in definition 3.2.2. dually, we can associate to a right object  $r$

$$
\text {r i g h t o b j e c t} r \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \text {w i t h} \psi \text {i s}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} \to e _ {1} ^ {\prime}
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime}
$$

end object,

a csl theory morphism  $\sigma_r$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {r} \colon \langle \gamma , \delta , \psi , \theta \rangle \to \langle \gamma \cup \{r \}, \delta \cup \{\alpha_ {1}, \dots , \alpha_ {m} \}, \psi \cup \{\psi \}, \\ \theta \cup \left\{\mathrm {r e q} _ {1}, \dots , \mathrm {r e q} _ {m}, \mathrm {r c e q}, \mathrm {r f e q} \right\} \rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathrm{req}_i$ , rceq and rfeq are

$$
\alpha_ {i} \circ e _ {i} [ \psi (f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}) / r ] = e _ {i} ^ {\prime} [ \psi (f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}) / r ] \circ f _ {i} \quad \left(\operatorname {r e q} _ {i}\right)
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {1} \circ e _ {1} [ g / r ] = e _ {1} ^ {\prime} [ g / r ] \circ f _ {1} \wedge \dots \\ \wedge \alpha_ {m} \circ e _ {m} [ g / r ] = e _ {m} ^ {\prime} [ g / r ] \circ f _ {m} \rightarrow g = \psi \left(f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}\right) \quad (\mathrm {r c e q}) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} r \left(h _ {1}, \dots , h _ {n}\right) = \\ \psi \left(e _ {1} ^ {\prime} \left[ h _ {1} / x _ {i} \right] \circ \alpha_ {1} \circ e _ {1} \left[ h _ {i} / x _ {i} \right], \dots , e _ {m} ^ {\prime} \left[ h _ {i} / x _ {i} \right] \circ \alpha_ {m} \circ e _ {m} \left[ h _ {i} / x _ {i} \right]\right) \quad (\text {r f e q}) \\ \end{array}
$$

1

example 3.4.2: let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi, \theta \rangle$  be the csl theory of cartesian closed categories (see examples 2.2.2 and 2.5.3). on top of this, as we have seen in section 3.3.4, we can define a natural number object by

left object nat with pr is zero:  $1\to$  nat succ: nat  $\rightarrow$  nat   
end object.

the csl statements characterizing this object are

$$
\operatorname {p r} (f, g) \circ \operatorname {z e r o} = f \quad \left(\mathrm {l e q} _ {\text {n a t}, 1}\right)
$$

$$
\operatorname {p r} (f, g) \circ \operatorname {s u c c} = g \circ \operatorname {p r} (f, g) \quad \left(\mathrm {l e q} _ {\text {n a t . 2}}\right)
$$

$$
h \circ \operatorname {z e r o} = f \wedge h \circ \operatorname {s u c c} = g \circ h \rightarrow h = \pr (f, g) \quad \left(\mathrm {l c e q} _ {\text {n a t}}\right)
$$

the csl extension  $\sigma_{\mathrm{nat}}$  associated with the declaration above is:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \gamma , \delta , \psi \rangle \xrightarrow {\sigma_ {\mathrm {n a t}}} \langle \gamma \cup \{\mathrm {n a t} \}, \delta \cup \{\mathrm {z e r o , s u c c} \}, \psi \cup \{\mathrm {p r} \}, \\ \theta \cup \{\mathrm {l e q} _ {\mathrm {n a t}, 1}, \mathrm {l e q} _ {\mathrm {n a t}, 2}, \mathrm {l c e q} _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \} \rangle \rrbracket \\ \end{array}
$$

thus, each cdt declaration can be associated with a csl theory extension. this can be thought as a semantics of cdt declarations. however, it is sometimes convenient to regard their semantics to be real categories.

definition 3.4.3: a sequence of cdt declarations  $d_{1},\ldots ,d_{l}$  defines a sequence of csl theory extensions starting from the empty csl theory.

$$
\langle \emptyset , \emptyset , \emptyset , \emptyset \rangle \xrightarrow {\sigma_ {d _ {1}}} \langle \gamma_ {1}, \delta_ {1}, \psi_ {1}, \theta_ {1} \rangle \xrightarrow {\sigma_ {d _ {2}}} \dots \xrightarrow {\sigma_ {d _ {l}}} \langle \gamma_ {l}, \delta_ {l}, \psi_ {l}, \theta_ {l} \rangle
$$

we define a csl structure of the cdt declaration sequence  $d_{1},\ldots ,d_{l}$  to be a category which is a csl theory structure of  $\langle \gamma_l,\delta_l,\psi_l,\theta_l\rangle$  , and the free structure of  $d_{1},\ldots ,d_{l}$  to be the free category of this csl theory (see section 2.6).

if we do not rely on any ways of defining functors other than cdt declarations and if we do not accept any pre-defined functors, it is inevitable to start with the empty csl theory. we have defined cartesian closed categories as a csl theory in examples 2.2.2 and 2.5.3, but we can do so in cdt starting from the empty theory by just declaring the terminal object (see subsection 3.3.1), products (see subsection 3.3.2) and exponentials (see subsection 3.3.3). the advantage of the latter is that we neither need to think about equations nor need to do tedious typing of functors, natural transformations or factorizers. these things come out automatically, so it is easy to define categories and there is less chance to make mistakes.

we have introduced cdt declarations from left and right, but we could not connect them formally. now, after having models of cdt as categories, we can do so.

proposition 3.4.4: let  $\langle \mathcal{c},\xi \rangle$  be a csl structure of a cdt declaration sequence  $d_{1},\ldots ,d_{l}$ . if  $d_{i}$  is

left object  $l(x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n})$  with  $\psi$  is

$$
\alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} \to e _ {1} ^ {\prime}
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime}
$$

end object

then  $\xi l = \mathbf{left}(\vec{f},\vec{g})$  where

$$
\vec {f} \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \langle \xi \lambda (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}). e _ {1}, \dots , \xi \lambda (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}). e _ {m} \rangle
$$

$$
\vec {g} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \langle \lambda (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}). e _ {1} ^ {\prime}, \dots , \lambda (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}). e _ {m} ^ {\prime} \rangle .
$$

the similar thing holds for right cdt declarations.

proof: trivial, because we set the csl statements so that this proposition holds.  $\square$

finally, in this section, let us summarize the objects we have defined in this chapter and their characteristic csl statements in figure 3.1.

# 3.5 existence of left and right

in section 3.1, we have introduced functors  $\mathbf{left}[f,g]$  and  $\mathbf{right}[f,g]$  with the condition which characterizes them, but we did not consider whether such functors exist or not. in this section, we study them mathematically and present a condition of the existence.

<table><tr><td>name</td><td>cdt declaration</td><td>csl statements</td></tr><tr><td>terminal</td><td>right object 1 with ! end object</td><td>f = !</td></tr><tr><td>initial</td><td>left object 0 with !! end object</td><td>f = !!</td></tr><tr><td>products</td><td>right object prod(x,y) with pair is pi1: prod → x pi2: prod → y end object</td><td>pi1 ○ pair(f,g) = f pi2 ○ pair(f,g) = g h = pair(pi1 ○ h, pi2 ○ h) prod(f,g) = pair(f○pi1,g○pi2)</td></tr><tr><td>coproducts</td><td>left object coprod(x,y) with case is in1: x → coprod in2: y → coprod end object</td><td>case(f,g) ○ in1 = f case(f,g) ○ in2 = g h = case(h○in1,h○in2) coprod(f,g) = case(in1○f,in2○g)</td></tr><tr><td>exponentials</td><td>right object exp(x,y) with curry is eval: prod(exp,x) → y end object</td><td>eval ○ prod(curry(f),i) = f h = curry(eval ○ prod(h,i)) exp(f,g) = curry(g○eval○prod(i,f))</td></tr><tr><td>nno</td><td>left object nat with pr is zero: 1 → nat succ: nat → nat end object</td><td>pr(f,g) ○ zero = f pr(f,g) ○ succ = g ○ pr(f,g) h○succ = g○h ⇒ h = pr(h○zero,g)</td></tr><tr><td>lists</td><td>left object list(x) with prl is nil: 1 → list cons: prod(x, list) → list end object</td><td>prl(f,g) ○ nil = f prl(f,g) ○ cons = prod(i, prl(f,g)) ○ g h○cons = prod(i,h)○g ⇒ h = prl(h○nil,g) list(f) = prl(nil, cons○prod(f,i))</td></tr><tr><td>infinite lists</td><td>right object inflist(x) with fold is head: inflist → x tail: inflist → inflist end object</td><td>head ○ fold(f,g) = f tail○fold(f,g) = fold(f,g) ○ g tail○h = g○h ⇒ h = fold(head○h,g) inflist(f) = fold(f○head,tail)</td></tr><tr><td>co-nno</td><td>right object conat with copr is pred: conat → coprod(1, conat) end object</td><td>pred ○ copr(f) = coprod(i,copr(f)) ○ f h○pred = f○coprod(i,h) ⇒ h = copr(f)</td></tr><tr><td>automata</td><td>right object dyn&#x27;(i,o) with univ&#x27; is next&#x27;: prod(dyn&#x27;,i) → dyn&#x27; output&#x27;: dyn&#x27; → o end object</td><td>next&#x27;○ prod(univ&#x27;(f,g),i) = univ&#x27;(f,g) ○ f output&#x27;○ univ&#x27;(f,g) = g next&#x27;○ prod(h,i) = h○f ⇒ h = univ&#x27;(f,output&#x27;○h)</td></tr></table>

figure 3.1: cdt objects

let us recall the standard construction of initial  $t$ -algebras (see, for example, [scott 76, lehmann and smyth 81]).

proposition 3.5.1: for a  $\omega$ -cocomplete category  $\mathcal{c}$  (i.e. it has colimit of any  $\omega$ -chain diagram) and an endo-functor  $t\colon \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c}$  which is  $\omega$ -cocontinuous (i.e. it preserves colimit of any  $\omega$ -chain diagram), its initial  $t$ -algebra is given by the colimit of the following  $\omega$ -chain diagram. $^{10}$

$$
0 \xrightarrow {\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} t (0) \xrightarrow {t (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} \mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} t (0)} \xrightarrow {t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} \mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h o l o m e g a . 1 . 2 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 .
\vdots})} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 1 . 5 p t w i d t h 0 . 4 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 1 . 5 p t}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 1 . 5 p t w i d t h 1 . 5 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 1 . 5 p t}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i d t h 1 . 5 p t w i d t h 1 . 5 p t \vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w i | c u r l}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w | c u r l}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w | c u r l}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w | c u r l}} t ^ {2} (\mathbin {\text {\vrule h e i g h t 6 . 0 p t w} | c u r l}))}
$$

as we presented in section 3.1,  $\mathbf{left}[f,g]$  is a generalization of initial  $t$ -algebras, where  $f$  is a functor of  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{e}$  and  $g$  is of  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d}^{-} \to \mathcal{e}$ . we will reduce the existence problem of  $\mathbf{left}$  to that of corresponding  $t$ -algebras. for a  $\mathcal{d}$  object  $a$ , from its definition  $\langle \mathbf{left}[f,g](a), \eta_a \rangle$  is the initial object in the category  $\mathbf{dalg}(f(\cdot, a), g(\cdot, a))$ , so

$$
f (\mathbf {l e f t} [ f, g ] (a), a) \xrightarrow {\eta_ {a}} g (\mathbf {l e f t} [ f, g ] (a), a)
$$

now, if  $g(\cdot, a)$  has a left adjoint functor, say  $h(\cdot, a): \mathcal{e} \to \mathcal{c}$ , this morphism  $\eta_a$  has its one-to-one corresponding morphism

$$
h (f (\mathbf {l e f t} [ f, g ] (a), a), a) \longrightarrow \mathbf {l e f t} [ f, g ] (a).
$$

this means that  $\mathbf{left}[f,g](a)$  is a  $t$ -algebra, where  $t(b) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} h(f(b,a),a)$  and we naturally expect this  $t$ -algebra to be special. it really is the initial  $t$ -algebra, so we can formulate the following theorem.

theorem 3.5.2: let  $f\colon \mathcal{c}\times \mathcal{d}\to \mathcal{e}$  and  $g\colon \mathcal{c}\times \mathcal{d}^{-}\to \mathcal{e}$  be functors. if

1.  $\mathcal{c}$  is  $\omega$ -cocomeplete,  
2. for each  $\mathcal{d}$  object  $a$ ,  $g_a \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} g(\cdot, a)$ :  $\mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{e}$  has a left adjoint  $h_a$ :  $\mathcal{e} \to \mathcal{c}$ , and  
3. for each  $\mathcal{d}$  object  $a$ ,  $f_a \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} f(\cdot, a)$ :  $\mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{e}$  is  $\omega$ -cocontinuous,

then  $\mathbf{left}[f,g](a)$  exists in  $\mathcal{c}$  and

$$
\mathbf {l e f t} [ f, g ] (a) = \operatorname * {c o l i m i t} _ {n} (h _ {a} \circ f _ {a}) ^ {n} (0)
$$

proof: since a left adjoint is cocontinuous,  $t_a \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} h_a \circ f_a$  is  $\omega$ -cocontinuous if  $f_a$  is so. all we have to show is that the initial  $t_a$ -algebra gives the initial object of  $\mathbf{dalg}(f_a, g_a)$ , and the rest follows from proposition 3.5.1.

let the initial  $t_{a}$ -algebra be  $i$  paired with a morphism

$$
h _ {a} (f _ {a} (i)) \xrightarrow {\iota} i.
$$

for an object  $\langle b, f \rangle$  in  $\mathbf{d}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{l}\mathbf{g}(f_a, g_a)$

$$
f _ {a} (b) \xrightarrow {f} g _ {a} (b)
$$

we have to construct the unique morphism from  $i$  to  $b$  which makes a certain diagram commute. to do so, let us name the factorizer of the adjunction  $h_a \vdash g_a\psi$ , that is,  $\psi$  is the natural isomorphism  $\operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{c}}(h_a(c), d) \xrightarrow{\cong} \operatorname{hom}_{\mathcal{e}}(c, g_a(d))$ . then,  $\psi^{-1}(f)$  gives a  $t_a$ -algebra

$$
h _ {a} (f _ {a} (b)) \xrightarrow {\psi^ {- 1} (f)} b
$$

and since  $\langle i, \iota \rangle$  is the initial  $t_a$ -algebra, there exists a unique morphism  $g: i \to b$  such that the following diagram commutes.

$$
\begin{array}{c} h _ {a} (f _ {a} (i)) \xrightarrow {\iota} i \\ h _ {a} (f _ {a} (g)) \biggl \downarrow \quad \bigcirc \biggl \downarrow g \\ h _ {a} (f _ {a} (b)) \xrightarrow [ \psi^ {- 1} (f) ]{} b \end{array} \qquad (*)
$$

the naturality of  $\psi$  converts this diagram to the following commutative diagram.

$$
\begin{array}{c} f _ {a} (i) \xrightarrow {\psi (\iota)} g _ {a} (i) \\ f _ {a} (g) \biggl | \quad \bigcirc \biggr | g _ {a} (g) \\ f _ {a} (b) \xrightarrow {f} g _ {a} (b) \end{array} \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad (* *)
$$

(i.e.  $\psi(g \circ \iota) = g_a(g) \circ \psi(\iota)$  and  $\psi(\psi^{-1}(f) \circ h_a(f_a(g))) = f \circ f_a(g)$ ). that showed the existence of  $g$ . the uniqueness of  $g$  is no more difficult. if  $g: i \to b$  satisfies  $(**)$ , then by applying  $\psi^{-1}$  we get  $(*)$  back again and so there  $g$  should be unique.

example 3.5.3: trivially, if  $\mathcal{e}$  is  $\mathcal{c}$  and  $g\colon \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d}^{-} \to \mathcal{c}$  is the projection functor,  $g_{a}$  is the identity which has the left adjoint  $h_{a}$  which is also the identity functor. in this case, the above theorem is essentially stating the same thing as proposition 3.5.1.

example 3.5.4: another simple case is that  $\mathcal{e}$  is  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}$  and  $g_{a}$  is the diagonal functor. its left adjoint is the binary coproduct functor.  $f_{a} \colon \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}$  can be decomposed into  $f_{a}'$  and  $f_{a}''$  both of which are functors of  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{c}$  such that  $f_{a}(b) = \langle f_{a}'(b), f_{a}''(b) \rangle$ . the theorem states that  $\operatorname{left}[f, g](a)$  is the initial  $t_{a}$ -algebra where  $t_{a}(b) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} f_{a}'(b) + f_{a}''(b)$ . this explains that our natural number object in subsection 3.3.4 is the initial  $t$ -algebra where  $t(b) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} 1 + b$ .

theorem 3.5.2 has its dual form for  $\mathbf{right}[f,g]$

theorem 3.5.5: let  $f \colon \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d} \to \mathcal{e}$  and  $g \colon \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{d}^{-} \to \mathcal{e}$  be functors. if

1.  $\mathcal{c}$  is  $\omega$ -complete,  
2. for each  $\mathcal{d}$  object  $a$ ,  $f_a \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} f(\cdot, a)$ :  $\mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{e}$  has a right adjoint  $k_a$ :  $\mathcal{e} \to \mathcal{c}$ , and  
3. for each  $\mathcal{d}$  object  $a$ ,  $g_a \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} g(\cdot, a)$ :  $\mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{e}$  is  $\omega$ -continuous,

then  $\mathbf{right}[f,g](a)$  exists in  $\mathcal{c}$  and

$$
\operatorname {r i g h t} [ f, g ] (a) = \lim  _ {n} (k _ {a} \circ f _ {a}) ^ {n} (1) \quad \mathbb {i}
$$

example 3.5.6: as an application of this theorem, let us calculate  $\mathrm{dyn}'(i,o)$ . for this, we should take  $\mathcal{d}$  to be  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}^{-}$ ,  $\mathcal{e}$  to be  $\mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}$ ,  $f\colon \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}^{-} \to \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}$  to be  $f(a,i,o) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \langle a \times i, a \rangle$  and  $g\colon \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}^{-} \times \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c} \times \mathcal{c}$  to be  $g(a,i,o) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \langle a,o \rangle$ .  $f_{i,o}$  has a right adjoint  $h_{i,o}(d,e) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \exp(i,d) \times e$ . therefore,  $\mathrm{dyn}'(i,o)$  is the final  $t_{i,o}$ -coalgebra, where  $t_{i,o}(a) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} h_{i,o}(g_{i,o}(a)) = \exp(i,a) \times o$ . now, let us calculate  $\lim_{n} t_{i,o}^{n}(1)$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} t _ {i, o} ^ {0} (1) \cong 1 \\ t _ {i, o} ^ {1} (1) \cong \exp (i, 1) \times o \cong o \cong \exp (1, o) \\ t _ {i, o} ^ {2} (1) \cong t _ {i, o} (o) \cong \exp (i, o) \times o \cong \exp (1 + i, o) \\ t _ {i, o} ^ {3} (1) \cong \exp (i, \exp (1 + i, o)) \times o \\ \cong \exp (i \times (1 + i), o) \times o \\ \cong \exp (1 + i + i ^ {2}, o) \\ \end{array}
$$

.

$$
t _ {i, o} ^ {n} (1) \cong \exp \left(1 + i + i ^ {2} + \dots + i ^ {n - 1}, o\right)
$$

.

therefore,  $\mathrm{dyn}^{\prime}(i,o)\cong \lim_{n}t_{i,o}^{n}(1)\cong \exp (1 + i + i^{2} + \dots ,o)\cong \exp (\mathrm{list}(i),o).[$

# chapter 4

# computation and categorical data types

in chapter 2 section 2.4, we introduced csl expressions  $\mathbf{exp}(\gamma, \delta, \psi)$  which denote polymorphic functions in csl structures. in this chapter, we will see them as programs and see how they can be executed. one of the ways to treat specifications as programs is to regard equations as rewrite rules, but in our case, csl statements are in general conditional equations and, therefore, it is quite difficult to treat them as rewrite rules. furthermore general rewriting cannot be regarded as real computation unless rules are church-rosser, otherwise, rewriting is more close to theorem proving.

there is no other way so long as we are dealing with csl specifications. remember that csl was introduced in order to give semantics to categorical data types we have investigated in chapter 3. in cdt theory, we are not dealing with arbitrary csl specifications, but some special ones, ones which are associated with cdt declarations. therefore, we have much more hope for executing these special csl specifications than arbitrary ones. for example, cartesian closed categories are, as is well-known, connected to lambda calculus which is a model of computation, so we can put some evaluation mechanism into them. [curien 86], for example, has developed such a system. a difference of our approach from his is that we do not restrict ourselves only to cartesian closed categories. one of the main aims of cdt is to study categories formed by programming languages, and we should not presume any structure in the categories without proper reasons. we can only accept the cartesian closed structure in cdt if this is necessary for putting the concept of computation to it. as we will see later in this chapter, our categories are cartesian closed (with some extra structure), and by then we should know why the cartesian closed structure is necessary.

csl expressions in section 2.4 and the cdt declaration mechanism in chapter 3

give us the basis of categorical programming language (cpl) to which we will devote this chapter. cpl tries to extract the computable part of cdt. as we have seen in subsection 3.3.8, cdt in general allows us to define very strange objects which have no concept of being computable. we are going to put restrictions to the form of cdt declarations in cpl.

therefore, in cpl, we can declare data types by cdt (with restrictions). as we have seen in section 3.4, this will determine a csl in which we can have csl expressions introduced in section 2.4. csl expressions are the programs in cpl. there is no difference between programs and data. from the categorical point of view, there is nothing inside objects, that is, there are no data inside data types. csl expressions whose domain is the terminal object are called cpl elements. the execution in cpl is essentially a reduction of a cpl element to a canonical irreducible cpl element.

following the result of this chapter, cpl will not only exist on paper, but also can be implemented. this will be presented in chapter 5.

in section 4.1 we introduce a restriction to objects we can declare in cpl and a set of reduction rules for cpl computation. in section 4.2 we see an example of computation in cpl. in section 4.3 we prove that any computation in cpl terminates (i.e. the reductions are normalizing) by tait's computability method. in section 4.4 we show some properties about objects in cpl and, finally, section 4.5 gives another set of reduction rules for cpl computation which reduces cpl elements into intuitively more canonical elements.

# 4.1 reduction rules for categorical programming language

in this section, we will present some basic definitions together with the reduction rules for cpl. let us assume in the following discussion that we have defined categorical data types by a sequence of cdt declarations,  $d_{1},\ldots ,d_{l}$ , and that we have obtained the corresponding csl,  $\langle \gamma ,\delta ,\psi ,\theta \rangle$  as in definition 3.4.3.

in ordinary programming languages, we distinguish programs and data. we feed data into a program; it processes the data and then outputs the result data. we cannot feed a program into another program and data cannot process other data or programs. however, it is true that data are a special kind of programs, very simple ones. we can write data directly into programs as initialization statements or as assignments. for example, natural numbers like 1, 132, 59, etc. are data as well as constants in programs. in some languages like lisp, there is no difference between data and programs at all. cpl is not as liberated as lisp, but both

data and programs are morphisms and data are just special morphisms having a special domain object.

as we know, category theory deals with the external structure of objects rather than their inner structure so it is not proper to think about data inside objects. however, we do sometimes need something similar to elements in set theory. we say elements in category theory are morphisms whose domain is the terminal object.

$$
1 \xrightarrow {e} a
$$

we say  $e$  is an element of  $a$ . if we think in the category of sets (set), '1' is the one-point set and a morphism from the one-point set to a set corresponds to an element in the set.

$$
\mathrm {h o m} _ {\mathbf {s e t}} (1, a) \cong a
$$

hence, the definition of elements in cpl is:

definition 4.1.1: given a csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ , a cpl element  $e$  is a csl expression with no morphism variables whose domain type is the terminal object.

$$
\rho \vdash e: \lambda \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right). 1 \rightarrow k
$$

(see section 2.4 for typing). in case  $n = 0$  which is very often the case, we may write  $\vdash e: 1 \rightarrow e$  where  $e$  is a functorial expression without variables. we also say that  $e$  is an element of  $e$ .

in order for this definition to be sensible, we need to have the terminal object in our category. for simplicity, we assume that  $d_{1}$  is the cdt declaration of the terminal object as is presented in subsection 3.3.1.

though this definition is a natural way of defining elements in category theory, it introduces non-symmetry in cpl. remember that cdt is meant to be symmetric: e.g. if we have an object of natural numbers, we should have its dual, an object of co-natural numbers, and so on. since we treat '1' as a special object and the elements in cpl are defined in this way, we destroy the beauty of symmetry. we will see the consequence of this shortly.

example 4.1.2: assume that we have defined all the objects in section 3.3. then

$\mathrm{succ}\circ \mathrm{zero}$

pi2  $\circ$  pair(succ  $\circ$  zero, nil),

eval  $\circ$  prod(pr(curry(pi2), curry(succ  $\circ$  eval)), i)  $\circ$  pair(succ  $\circ$  succ  $\circ$  zero, succ  $\circ$  zero)

are all cpl elements.

as we can see from this example, we cannot regard all the elements as data. 'succ o zero' and 'pair(succ o zero, nil)' can be data, but 'pi1 o pair(succ o zero, nil)' cannot be. we call special data-like cpl elements canonical cpl elements. the definition is:

definition 4.1.3: a canonical cpl element is a cpl element which wholly consists of natural transformations introduced by left objects and factorizers by right objects. formally, a canonical cpl element,  $c \in \mathbf{ce}$ , is defined by

$$
c: := \mathbf {i} \mid \alpha_ {l} \circ c \mid \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}) \circ c
$$

where  $\alpha_{l}$  is a natural transformation of a left object and  $\psi_{r}$  is a factorizer of a right object. note we often do not write  $\mathbf{i}$  at the tail of canonical elements.

from this definition, 'pair' and 'curry' can form canonical elements, but 'pi1', 'pi2' or 'eval' cannot. so for the right objects, factorizers are the means of creating canonical elements. on the other hand, 'zero', 'succ' and 'cons' can form canonical elements, but not 'pr' or 'case'. so for the left objects, natural transformations are the means of creating canonical elements.

<table><tr><td></td><td>canonical</td><td>non-canonical</td></tr><tr><td>left object</td><td>natural transformations
e.g. zero, succ, nil, cons</td><td>factorizers
e.g. case, pr, prl</td></tr><tr><td>right object</td><td>factorizers
e.g. pair, curry</td><td>natural transformations
e.g. pi1, pi2, eval</td></tr></table>

definition 4.1.3 is beautifully symmetrical as we hoped from the symmetry of cdt. in addition, if we look at the canonical elements in other programming languages, we note that 'pair' corresponds to making pairs of data, 'curry' corresponds to lambda abstraction and both create canonical things (i.e. we cannot process them any more as themselves), and also we note that 'pr' and 'case' are programming constructs corresponding to primitive recursive definitions and case statements and they can never be data. therefore, definition 4.1.3 fits well with the usual notion of data.

of course, this is not the only definition of canonical elements. it defines quite lazy canonical elements. it does not care what are inside factorizers. for example,

$$
\mathrm {p a i r (p i 1 \circ p a i r (s u c c \circ z e r o , n i l) , z e r o)}
$$

is a canonical element of 'prod(nat, nat)' from this definition. some might not want to call it canonical because it is equal to another canonical element

$$
\operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}, \operatorname {z e r o})
$$

which looks more canonical. however, this is because 'prod' is a rather special object. in general, expressions inside factorizers may not be elements, so we

cannot demand them to be canonical (or we have to treat product-like objects special). one might still accept this easily since canonical elements in martin-löf's type theory are similar to ours, but another queer aspect of our definition is that we accept

$$
\operatorname {p a i r} (\mathrm {p i} 2, \mathrm {p i} 1) \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\mathrm {s u c c}, \mathbf {i}) \circ \operatorname {z e r o}
$$

as a canonical element. it is equal to the following element

$$
\mathrm {p a i r} (\mathrm {p i} 2 \circ \mathrm {p a i r} (\mathrm {s u c c}, \mathbf {i}) \circ \mathrm {z e r o}, \mathrm {p i} 1 \circ \mathrm {p a i r} (\mathrm {s u c c}, \mathbf {i}) \circ \mathrm {z e r o}),
$$

but this is again because of the special property of 'prod'. in general

$$
\psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}) \circ c
$$

is not equal to something in the form of  $\psi_r(e_1', \ldots, e_n')$ . for example, we cannot always find other simpler canonical elements which is equal to 'fold  $(f, g) \circ c$  (where 'fold' is the factorizer of 'inlist').

there are many views of computation, but in cpl computation is the reduction of an element to a canonical element equal to the given element.

$$
e \rightarrow c
$$

since the reduction is not straightforward, we want to do step-by-step reduction. therefore, we modify the above form of reduction to the following one.

$$
\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}
$$

this means that a csl expression  $e$  applied to a cpl canonical element  $c$  is reduced to a cpl canonical element  $c'$ . it is like calculating the result of applying an element  $c$  to a function  $e$ . obviously,  $e \circ c$  should be semantically equal to  $c'$ . since a cpl element is a morphism from the terminal object and  $\mathbf{t}'$  is its canonical element, if we want to reduce an arbitrary cpl element  $e$  we can ask for the following reduction.

$$
\langle e, \mathbf {i} \rangle \rightarrow c
$$

in the following, we assume that the associativity of  $\circ$  (the composition of morphisms) has been taken care by some means so that in our rules we do not consider it. we also assume that there are no functors in cpl elements because we can always replace them with factorizers and natural transformations. for example,

$$
\operatorname {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p r o d} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c}), \operatorname {n i l})
$$

is equivalent to

$$
\operatorname {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c}) \circ \operatorname {p i 1}, \operatorname {n i l} \circ \operatorname {p i 2}).
$$

let us now define rules for the reductions. the simplest one is for identities. since  $\mathbf{i} \circ c$  is equal to  $c$ , we should have the following rule.

$$
\langle \mathbf {i}, c \rangle \rightarrow c \quad (\text {i d e n t})
$$

next, for the composition, we naturally have

$$
\frac {\left\langle e _ {2} , c \right\rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \quad \left\langle e _ {1} , c ^ {\prime \prime} \right\rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\left\langle e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} , c \right\rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}} \tag {comp}
$$

in case that  $e$  is a natural transformation introduced by a left object or a factorizer introduced by a right object, the rule is easy because the composition of  $e$  with a canonical element is canonical by definition 4.1.3.

$$
\langle \alpha_ {l}, c \rangle \rightarrow \alpha_ {l} \circ c \quad (\text {l - n a t})
$$

$$
\langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}), c \rangle \rightarrow \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}) \circ c \quad (\text {r - f a c t})
$$

for example,  $\langle \mathrm{succ},\mathrm{zero}\rangle \rightarrow \mathrm{succ}\circ \mathrm{zero}$  and

$\langle \mathrm{curry(pi2),pair(zero,succ\circ zero)}\rangle \rightarrow \mathrm{curry(pi2)\circ pair(zero,succ\circ zero).}$

difficulty comes in for the other cases, i.e. when  $e$  is a natural transformation of a right object or a factorizer of a left object. let us first consider the case for a factorizer  $\psi_l$  introduced by the following left object.

left object  $l(x)$  with  $\psi_l$  is

$$
\alpha_ {l} \colon e (l, x) \to e ^ {\prime} (l, x)
$$

end object

from the property of this object, we have a csl equation

$$
e ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {l} (e), \mathbf {i}) \circ \alpha_ {l} = e \circ e (\psi_ {l} (e), \mathbf {i}).
$$

an instance of this equation is  $\operatorname{pr}(f, g) \circ \operatorname{succ} = g \circ \operatorname{pr}(f, g)'$ , and in this case we should have a rewrite rule from  $\operatorname{pr}(f, g) \circ \operatorname{succ}'$  to  $g \circ \operatorname{pr}(f, g)'$ . therefore, in general we might have the following rule.

$$
\frac {\langle e \circ e (\psi_ {l} (e) , \mathbf {i}) , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle e ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {l} (e) , \mathbf {i}) , \alpha_ {l} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

however, this rule is not what we wanted. we wanted a rule for  $\langle \psi_l(e), c'' \rangle \rightarrow c''$ . in order that the above rule to be a one we want,  $e'(l, x)$  should be simply  $l$ , and we get

$$
\frac {\langle e \circ e (\psi_ {l} (e) , \mathbf {i}) , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \psi_ {l} (e) , \alpha_ {l} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}. \tag {l-fact}
$$

the restriction that demands  $e^{\prime}(l,x)$  should be  $l$  is the first restriction we put onto objects in order to obtain the cpl computation rules. the left objects introduced in chapter 3, initial object, coproducts, natural number object and lists, all satisfy this restriction except the left adjoint functor of 'list' which we do not expect to be in the world of computation. if  $e^{\prime}(l,x)$  is something other than  $l$ , we are allowing to have the left adjoint  $l^{\prime}$  of  $e^{\prime}(\cdot ,x)$  by

left object  $l^{\prime}(x,y)$  with  $\psi_{l^{\prime}}$  is

$$
\alpha_ {l ^ {\prime}} \colon y \to e ^ {\prime} (l l ^ {\prime}, x)
$$

end object.

in familiar categories (e.g. the category of sets), a functor  $f \colon \mathcal{c} \to \mathcal{c}$  hardly has a left adjoint: e.g. the product functor  $\bullet \times a$  does not have one, nor does the coproduct functor  $\bullet + a$ .

as an example of l-fact, let us write the rules for the factorizer associated with the natural number object. there are two rules for two natural transformations, 'zero' and 'succ'.

$$
\frac {\langle e , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \mathrm {p r} (e , e ^ {\prime}) , \mathrm {z e r o} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}} \qquad \frac {\langle e ^ {\prime} \circ \mathrm {p r} (e , e ^ {\prime}) , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \mathrm {p r} (e , e ^ {\prime}) , \mathrm {s u c c} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

let us now consider the last case, the case for the reduction rule of a natural transformation  $\alpha_{r}$  introduced by the following right object declaration.

right object  $r(x)$  with  $\psi_r$  is

$$
\alpha_ {r} \colon e (r, x) \to e ^ {\prime} (r, x)
$$

end object

from the property of this object, we have a csl equation

$$
\alpha_ {r} \circ e (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}) = e ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}) \circ e.
$$

an instance of this equation is 'pi1  $\circ$  pair  $(f,g) = f$ ' and in this case we should have a rule to rewrite 'pi1  $\circ$  pair  $(f,g)$ ' to '  $f$ '. therefore, in general we have a rule rewriting from the left-hand side to the right-hand side as the following rule.

$$
\frac {\langle e ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {r} (e) , \mathbf {i}) \circ e , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \alpha_ {r} , e (\psi_ {r} (e) , \mathbf {i}) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

however, this rule is not quite right because  $e(\psi_r(e), \mathbf{i}) \circ c$  is not a canonical element. we cannot have functors in canonical elements. therefore, what the rule should really look like is

$$
\frac {c = e (\psi_ {r} (e) , \mathbf {i}) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime} \quad \langle e ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {r} (e) , \mathbf {i}) \circ e , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \alpha_ {r} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}. (+)
$$

we now have a different problem of finding out an expression  $e$  and a canonical element  $c''$  from a given canonical element  $c$  such that

$$
c = e (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}.
$$

since we are dealing with computation, we need a mechanical way of solving this problem. let us introduce another form of reduction rules.

$$
\langle c, e, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \quad (*)
$$

such that  $c = e(\psi_r(e), \mathbf{i}) \circ c''$  where  $e$  is a functorial expression in which  $r$  is a variable. by these rules, we can rewrite the rule (+) to

$$
\frac {\langle c , e (r , x) , r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e) , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \qquad \langle e ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {r} (e) , \mathbf {i}) \circ e , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \alpha_ {r} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}. \qquad \mathrm {(r - n a t)}
$$

we now have to list the rules for  $(\ast)$ . if  $e$  is simply  $r$  itself,  $c$  should be  $e(\psi_r(e), \mathbf{i}) \circ c'' = \psi_r(e) \circ c''$ . therefore, the rule should be

$$
\langle \psi_ {r} (e) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}, r, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle . \quad (\text {r - n a t - v})
$$

next, if  $e(r,x)$  does not depend on  $r$ ,  $c$  is  $e(\psi_r(e),\mathbf{i}) \circ c'' = c''$ , so the rule may be

$$
\langle c, e, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e), c \rangle ,
$$

but where does  $\psi_r(e)$  come from? we cannot determine  $e$ . therefore,  $e(r,x)$  must not be independent from  $r$  (i.e.  $e(r,x)$  should not be free of  $r$ ).

the case left is when  $e(r, x)$  is not a variable but a real functorial expression. let it be

$$
f \left(\hat {e} _ {1} (r, x), \dots , \hat {e} _ {n} (r, x)\right), \tag {1}
$$

where  $f$  is a functor name. in this case, the equation we are solving is

$$
c = f \left(\hat {e} _ {1} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right), \dots , \hat {e} _ {n} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right)\right) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}. \tag {2}
$$

since functors are always represented by their associated factorizers, the equation looks like

$$
c = \psi_ {f} \left(\dots \circ \hat {e} _ {1} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right) \circ \dots \circ \hat {e} _ {n} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right) \circ \dots\right) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}. \tag {3}
$$

as we can see, we might have to pick up the same  $\psi_r(e)$  from more than one place and this would be a trouble. because  $\psi_r(e)$  is a general csl expression and we cannot do theorem proving to show two csl expressions are equal when doing the cpl computation. therefore, we need to restrict that  $\psi_r(e)$  should appear only once in (3). in order for this, we have to first restrict that only one

$\hat{e}_i$  in (2) contains  $r$ . without loss of generality, we can assume it is  $\hat{e}_1(r,x)$ , and since we are only interested in the first argument of  $f$ , we assume that  $f$  is a unary functor and  $e(r,x)$  is  $f(\hat{e}(r,x))$ . now (2) looks like

$$
c = f (\hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}, \tag {2'}
$$

but still (3) might have more than one  $\psi_r(e)$  because when we expand functors by factorizers using (lfeq) or (rfeq) in definition 3.4.1, we might duplicate  $\psi_r(e)$ . before stating the restriction to guarantee the no-duplication of  $\psi_r(e)$ , let us note that  $f$  should be a functor introduced by a right object declaration. this is because, if we look at the equation (3),  $c$  is a canonical element consisting of left natural transformations and right factorizers, so  $\psi_f$  should be a right factorizer. let the following be the declaration of  $f$ .

right object  $f(y)$  with  $\psi_f$  is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \beta_ {1} \colon \tilde {e} _ {1} (f, y) \to \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (f, y) \\ \dots \tag {4} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\beta_ {m} \colon \tilde {e} _ {m} (f, y) \to \tilde {e} _ {m} ^ {\prime} (f, y)
$$

end object

from the equation (rfeq) in definition 3.4.1, (3) really is

$$
c = \psi_ {f} (\tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i}, \hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ \beta_ {1} \circ \tilde {e} _ {1} (\mathbf {i}, \hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})),
$$

$$
\dots \tag {3}
$$

$$
\tilde {e} _ {m} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i}, \hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ \beta_ {m} \circ \tilde {e} _ {m} (\mathbf {i}, \hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}
$$

in order that  $\psi_r(e)$  should appear only once in this equation,  $y$  should appear only once in one of  $\tilde{e}_i$  and  $\tilde{e}_i'$ . in order to show that  $y$  should not be in one of  $\tilde{e}_i'$ , let  $f$  be simply

right object  $f(y)$  with  $\psi_f$  is

$$
\beta : y \rightarrow f \tag {4}
$$

end object.

(3) becomes

$$
c = \psi_ {f} (\beta \circ \hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}.
$$

we may demand that  $c$  should be  $\psi_f(\hat{e})\circ \hat{c}$  and find  $\psi_r(e)$  such that

$$
\hat {e} = \beta \circ \hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}),
$$

but how can we solve this equation? in general, we need theorem proving for this. we should have reduced the problem recursively into

$$
\langle \check {c}, \check {e}, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e), \check {c} \rangle ,
$$

but there is no way to do this if  $y$  appears in one of  $\tilde{e}_i$ , because  $\hat{e}$  should never be an element. the typing rule in definition 3.2.2 gives us

$$
\frac {\hat {e} \colon y \to z}{\psi_ {f} (\hat {e}) \colon z \to f (y)}
$$

and we cannot choose  $y$  to be the terminal object. if  $y$  and  $f$  were the other way round in (4'), we could choose  $z$  to be the terminal object to make  $\hat{e}$  an element. therefore, if  $y$  is in one of  $\tilde{e}_i'$  in (4), we have a possibility of reducing the problem of solving (3') into a smaller problem of the same kind. without losing generality, we can assume that  $y$  only appears in  $\tilde{e}_1'$  in (4). now, (3') is

$$
c = \psi_ {f} \left(\tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i}, \hat {e} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right)) \circ \beta_ {1}, \beta_ {2}, \dots , \beta_ {m}\right) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}. \tag {3''}
$$

we demand  $c$  to be  $\psi_f(\hat{e}_1, \hat{e}_2, \ldots, \hat{e}_m) \circ \hat{c}$ , so  $(3'')$  is further rewritten to

$$
\psi_ {f} (\hat {e} _ {1}, \hat {e} _ {2}, \dots , \hat {e} _ {m}) \circ \hat {c} = \psi_ {f} (\tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i}, \hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ \beta_ {1}, \beta_ {2}, \dots , \beta_ {m}) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}. \qquad (3 ^ {\prime \prime \prime})
$$

here, we cannot jump to the conclusion that  $\hat{e}_1$  is  $\tilde{e}_1'(\mathbf{i},\hat{e} (\psi_r(e),\mathbf{i}))\circ \beta_1,\hat{e}_2$  is  $\beta_{2}$ $\dots$  , and  $\hat{c}$  is  $c''$  , because a part of  $\hat{c}$  may well contribute to form  $e_1^\prime (\mathbf{i},\hat{e} (\psi_r(e),\mathbf{i}))$  what is desirable is that we could rewrite  $\psi_f(\hat{e}_1,\hat{e}_2,\ldots ,\hat{e}_m)\circ \hat{c}$  to  $\psi_f(\hat{e}_1',\hat{e}_2',\ldots ,\hat{e}_m')$  like  $\operatorname {pair}(f,g)\circ h = \operatorname {pair}(f\circ h,g\circ h)$  . this is possible when each  $\tilde{e}_i'$  in (4) does not depend on  $f$

proposition 4.1.4: let  $r$  be a right object defined by

right object  $r(x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n})$  with  $\psi_r$  is

$$
\alpha_ {1} \colon e _ {1} (r, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \rightarrow e _ {1} ^ {\prime} (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {m} \colon e _ {m} (r, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \to e _ {m} ^ {\prime} (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

end object

(note that  $e_{i}^{\prime}$  does not depend on  $r$ ), then

$$
\psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ e ^ {\prime} = \psi_ {r} (e _ {1} \circ e _ {1} (e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \dots , \mathbf {i}), \dots , e _ {m} \circ e _ {m} (e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \dots , \mathbf {i})) (\mathrm {r e q c})
$$

proof: from (rceq) in definition 3.4.1,

$$
\alpha_ {1} \circ e _ {1} (\psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) \circ e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \ldots , \mathbf {i}) = f _ {1} \wedge
$$

.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {m} \circ e _ {m} \left(\psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right) \circ e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \dots , \mathbf {i}\right) = f _ {m} \wedge \\ \rightarrow \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right) \circ e ^ {\prime} = \psi_ {r} \left(f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

using  $(\mathrm{req}_i)$  and the fact that  $e_{i}$  is covariant in  $r$  , we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} e _ {1} \circ e _ {1} \left(e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \dots , \mathbf {i}\right) = f _ {1} \wedge \dots \wedge e _ {m} \circ e _ {m} \left(e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \dots , \mathbf {i}\right) = f _ {m} \\ \rightarrow \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right) \circ e ^ {\prime} = \psi_ {r} \left(f _ {1}, \dots , f _ {m}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore,

$$
\psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) \circ c ^ {\prime} = \psi_ {r} (e _ {1} \circ e _ {1} (e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \ldots , \mathbf {i}), \ldots , e _ {m} \circ e _ {m} (e ^ {\prime}, \mathbf {i}, \ldots , \mathbf {i})). [ ]
$$

we call this kind of right objects unconditioned. the name indicates that the objects are characterized without using conditional csl equations. in fact,  $(\mathrm{req}_i)$ , (reqc), (rfeq) and the following (reqi) characterize the unconditioned right objects. $^1$

$$
\psi_ {r} \left(\alpha_ {1}, \dots , \alpha_ {m}\right) = \mathbf {i} \quad (\text {r e q i})
$$

therefore, we assume that in (4)  $f$  does not appear in any of  $\tilde{e}_i'$ . as we have already assumed that  $y$  appears only in  $\tilde{e}_1'$ , (4) now looks like

right object  $f(y)$  with  $\psi_f$  is

$$
\beta_ {1} \colon \tilde {e} _ {1} (f) \to \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (y)
$$

$$
\beta_ {2} \colon \tilde {e} _ {2} (f) \to \tilde {e} _ {2} ^ {\prime}
$$

.

$$
\beta_ {m} \colon \tilde {e} _ {m} (f) \to \tilde {e} _ {m} ^ {\prime}
$$

end object
