$(\tilde{e}_2', \ldots, \tilde{e}_m')$  do not depend on  $f$  or  $y$  in this case, but in general  $f$  might have parameters other than  $y$  and they can appear in  $(\tilde{e}_2', \ldots, \tilde{e}_m')$ , and (3) is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \psi_ {f} \left(\hat {e} _ {1} \circ \tilde {e} _ {1} (\hat {c}), \hat {e} _ {2} \circ \tilde {e} _ {e} (\hat {c}), \dots , \hat {e} _ {m} \circ \tilde {e} _ {m} (\hat {c})\right) \\ = \psi_ {f} \left(\tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} \left(\hat {e} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right)\right) \circ \beta_ {1} \circ \tilde {e} _ {1} \left(c ^ {\prime \prime}\right), \beta_ {2} \circ \tilde {e} _ {2} \left(c ^ {\prime \prime}\right), \dots , \beta_ {m} \circ \tilde {e} _ {m} \left(c ^ {\prime \prime}\right)\right). \tag {3} \\ \end{array}
$$

if, furthermore, the first argument of  $\psi_f$  is an element, we can reduce the problem of solving (3) into

$$
\hat {e} _ {1} \circ \tilde {e} _ {1} (\hat {c}) = \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} \left(\hat {e} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right)\right) \circ \beta_ {1} \circ \tilde {e} _ {1} \left(c ^ {\prime \prime}\right). \tag {5}
$$

since  $\hat{e}_1\circ \tilde{e}_1(\hat{c})$  is an element, we can ask its canonical element and it becomes almost like the original object (3) except  $e$  is replaced. let us see the typing rule for  $\psi_f$  defined in definition 3.2.2.

$$
\begin{array}{c} f _ {1} \colon \tilde {e} _ {1} (z) \to \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (y) \qquad \ldots \\ \hline \psi_ {f} (f _ {1}, \ldots) \colon z \to f (y) \end{array}
$$

since  $\psi_f$  in (3) is making an element,  $z$  above should be the terminal object 1. in order that  $f_1$  above is also an element,  $\tilde{e}_1(z)$  (or  $\tilde{e}_1(1)$  because  $z$  is 1) should also be the terminal object. because  $\tilde{e}_1(z)$  cannot be independent from  $z$ ,  $\tilde{e}_1(z)$  should be simply  $z$ . therefore, (5) is

$$
\hat {e} _ {1} \circ \hat {c} = \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ \beta_ {1} \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}. \tag {5'}
$$

as  $\hat{e}_1\circ \hat{c}$  is an element, we can ask its canonical element by

$$
\langle \hat {e} _ {1}, \hat {c} \rangle \rightarrow \hat {c} ^ {\prime}, \tag {6}
$$

and we can also ask to solve

$$
\hat {c} ^ {\prime} = \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ \hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime} \tag {7}
$$

by

$$
\langle \hat {c} ^ {\prime}, \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\hat {e} (r, x)), r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e), \hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle .
$$

if we set  $c''$  to be

$$
\psi_ {f} (\hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime}, \hat {e} _ {2} \circ \hat {e} _ {2} (\hat {c}), \dots , \hat {e} _ {m} \circ \hat {e} _ {m} (\hat {c})),
$$

(3) is satisfied from  $(\mathrm{req}_i)$  in definition 3.4.1.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\hat {e} \left(\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i}\right)) \circ \beta_ {1} \circ \tilde {e} _ {1} \left(c ^ {\prime \prime}\right) \\ = \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ \beta_ {1} \circ \tilde {e} _ {1} (\psi_ {f} (\hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime}, \hat {e} _ {2} \circ \hat {e} _ {2} (\hat {c}), \dots , \hat {e} _ {m} \circ \hat {e} _ {m} (\hat {c}))) \\ = \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\hat {e} (\psi_ {r} (e), \mathbf {i})) \circ \beta_ {1} \circ \hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime} \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \left(\text {f r o m} (\operatorname {r e q} _ {i})\right) \\ = \hat {c} ^ {\prime} \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots (from(7)) \\ = \hat {e} _ {1} \circ \hat {c} \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots (from(6)) \\ \beta_ {i} \circ \tilde {e} _ {i} \left(c ^ {\prime \prime}\right) \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots (i = 2, \dots , m) \\ = \beta_ {i} \circ \tilde {e} _ {i} \left(\psi_ {f} \left(\hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime}, \hat {e} _ {2} \circ \hat {e} _ {2} (\hat {c}), \dots , \hat {e} _ {m} \circ \hat {e} _ {m} (\hat {c})\right)\right) \\ = \hat {e} _ {i} \circ \bar {e} _ {i} (\hat {c}) \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \left(\operatorname {r e q} _ {i}\right)) \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, we got the following rule.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\langle \hat {e} _ {1} , \hat {c} \rangle \rightarrow \hat {c} ^ {\prime} \quad \langle \hat {c} ^ {\prime} , \tilde {e} _ {1} ^ {\prime} (\hat {e} (r , x)) , r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e) , \hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle}{\langle \psi_ {f} (\hat {e} _ {1} , \hat {e} _ {2} , \dots , \hat {e} _ {m}) \circ \hat {c} , f (\hat {e} (r , x)) , r \rangle} \tag {r-nat-f} \\ \sim \left\langle \psi_ {r} (e), \psi_ {f} \left(\hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime}, \hat {e} _ {2} \circ \tilde {e} _ {2} (\hat {c}), \dots , \hat {e} _ {m} \circ \hat {e} _ {m} (\hat {c})\right) \right\rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

as an example of the rules (r-fact), (r-nat), (r-nat-v) and (r-nat-f), let us write the rules for the exponentials. the cdt declaration of the exponentials is

right object  $\exp (x,y)$  with curry is eval:prod  $(\exp ,x)\to y$  end object

as we have seen in subsection 3.3.3. the rule (r-fact) is simply

$$
\langle \operatorname {c u r r y} (e), c \rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {c u r r y} (e) \circ c,
$$

and the rule (r-nat) is

$$
\frac {\langle c , \operatorname {p r o d} (\exp , x) , \exp \rangle \sim \langle \operatorname {c u r r y} (e) , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \qquad \langle e , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \operatorname {e v a l} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}.
$$

the rule (r-nat-v) is simply

$$
\langle \operatorname {c u r r y} (e) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}, \exp , \exp \rangle \sim \langle \operatorname {c u r r y} (e), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle ,
$$

and, finally, the rule (r-nat-f) is

$$
\frac {\langle \hat {e} _ {1} , \hat {c} \rangle \rightarrow \hat {c} ^ {\prime} \qquad \langle \hat {c} ^ {\prime} , \exp , \exp \rangle \sim \langle \mathrm {c u r r y} (e) , \hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle}{\langle \mathrm {p a i r} (\hat {e} _ {1} , \hat {e} _ {2}) \circ \hat {c} , \mathrm {p r o d} (\exp , x) , \exp \rangle \sim \langle \mathrm {c u r r y} (e) , \mathrm {p a i r} (\hat {c} ^ {\prime \prime} , \hat {e} _ {2} \circ \hat {c}) \rangle}.
$$

we may simplify the last three rules and have the next one instead.

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {1} , c \rangle \rightarrow \mathrm {c u r r y} (e) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime} \qquad \langle e , \mathrm {p a i r} (c ^ {\prime \prime} , e _ {2} \circ c) \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \mathrm {e v a l} , \mathrm {p a i r} (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

we will see an example using these rules in the next section.

in order to obtain (r-nat-f), we have put several restrictions to the right object declaration. to state the restrictions formally, let us introduce the notion 'productive'.

definition 4.1.5: functorial expressions which are productive in  $x$  are generated by the following two rules.

1.  $x$  itself is a functorial expression productive in  $x$ .  
2. if  $p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{n})$  is a functor which is productive in  $y_{i}$  and  $e$  is a functorial expression productive in  $x$ ,  $p(e_{1},\ldots ,e_{i - 1},e_{i},e_{i + 1},\ldots ,e_{n})$  is productive in  $x$ , where  $e_{1},\ldots ,e_{i - 1},e_{i + 1},\ldots ,e_{n}$  are functorial expressions which do not contain  $x$ .

a functor  $p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{n})$  is called productive in its  $i$ -th argument  $y_{i}$  when  $p$  is declared as a right object and its declaration

right object  $p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{n})$  with  $\psi_p$  is

$$
\alpha_ {p, 1} \colon e _ {p, 1} (p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {n}) \to e _ {p, 1} ^ {\prime} (p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {n})
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {p, m} \colon e _ {p, m} (p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {n}) \rightarrow e _ {p, m} ^ {\prime} (p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {n})
$$

end object

satisfies

1.  $p$  is unconditioned (i.e.  $p$  does not appear in  $e_{p,1}^{\prime},\ldots ,e_{p,m}^{\prime}$ ),  
2.  $y_{i}$  does not appear in  $e_{p,1},\ldots ,e_{p,m}$  
3.  $y_{i}$  appears only one of  $e_{p,1}^{\prime},\ldots ,e_{p,m}^{\prime}$ , so let us assume that it appears in  $e_{p,j}^{\prime}$  only,

4.  $e_{p,j}$  is simply  $p$ , and  
5.  $e_{p,j}^{\prime}$  is a functorial expression productive in  $y_{i}$ .

therefore, the declaration above may look more like

right object  $p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{i},\ldots ,y_{n})$  with  $\psi_p$  is

$$
\alpha_ {p, 1} \colon e _ {p, 1} (p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}) \rightarrow
$$

$$
e _ {p, 1} ^ {\prime} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right)
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {p, j} \colon p \rightarrow e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right) \tag {p}
$$

··

$$
\alpha_ {p, m} \colon e _ {p, m} (p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}) \rightarrow
$$

$$
e _ {p, m} ^ {\prime} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right)
$$

end object

$\alpha_{p,j}$  may be called projection to  $y_{i}$ .

the functor 'prod' is a typical productive functor. it is productive in its both arguments. the functor 'exp' is not productive in any of its arguments. a functorial expression 'prod(prod(x, exp(y, z)), prod(x, u))' is productive in  $u$  but not in  $x$  or  $y$  or  $z$ .

definition 4.1.6: a right object  $r$  is called computable if its declaration

right object  $r(x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n})$  with  $\psi_r$  is

$$
\alpha_ {r, 1} \colon e _ {r, 1} (r, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \rightarrow e _ {r, 1} ^ {\prime} (r, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {r, m} \colon e _ {r, m} (r, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \rightarrow e _ {r, m} ^ {\prime} (r, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

end object

satisfies that  $e_{r,1}, \ldots, e_{r,m}$  are functorial expressions productive in  $r$ . we also call a left object  $l$  computable when its declaration is

left object  $l(x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n})$  with  $\psi_l$  is

$$
\alpha_ {l, 1} \colon e _ {l, 1} (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \rightarrow l
$$

.

(lc)

$$
\alpha_ {l, m} \colon e _ {l, m} (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \to l
$$

end object

#

the reduction rules we have defined in this section work when all the objects we define are computable, and all the objects declared in section 3.3 are computable

except the obscure ones in subsection 3.3.8. obviously, we did not want to have those obscure objects in our datatype system and the computability constraint gets rid of them. in other words, the categories which are defined by declaring computable objects cannot be richer than cartesian closed category with recursive objects. note that we did not make the restriction in the beginning. we had the ability to declare a lot of other objects, but it turned out that in order to be able to discuss the computability in cdt, the categories should be cartesian closed with recursive objects. this signifies the importance of cartesian closed categories in computer science yet again (e.g. the models of typed lambda calculus correspond to cartesian closed categories).

note that not all the productive objects are computable by the definition 4.1.5, but from now on we only treat computable objects, so productive objects mean computable productive objects.

definition 4.1.7: let  $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{l}$  be a sequence of cdt declarations defining only computable objects and let  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi, \theta \rangle$  be the corresponding csl theory defined by definition 3.4.3. then, we can have computation rules for cpl elements over the csl signature  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ . the computation rules are divided into two: those in the form of

$$
\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}
$$

where  $c$  and  $c'$  are canonical elements and  $e$  and  $c$  can be composed (i.e.  $e \circ c$  is an element), and those in the form of

$$
\langle c, e, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}), c ^ {\prime} \rangle
$$

where  $c$  and  $c'$  are canonical elements,  $r$  is a right functor name,  $e$  is a functorial expression productive in  $r$  and, if  $c$  is an element of  $e'$ ,  $e$  should be more general than  $e'$ .

1. ident

$$
\langle \mathbf {i}, c \rangle \rightarrow c
$$

2. comp

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \qquad \langle e _ {1} , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

3. l-nat

$$
\left\langle \alpha_ {l, j}, c \right\rangle \rightarrow \alpha_ {l, j} \circ c
$$

4. r-fact

$$
\left\langle \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right), c \right\rangle \rightarrow \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right) \circ c
$$

5. l-fact

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {j} \circ e _ {l , j} [ \psi_ {l} (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {m}) / l ] , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \psi_ {l} (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {m}) , \alpha_ {l , j} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

6. r-nat

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\langle c, e _ {r, j}, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle} \\ & {\langle e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} [ \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) / r ] \circ e _ {j}, c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}} \\ & \hline & {\langle \alpha_ {r, j}, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}} \end{array}
$$

7. r-nat-v

$$
\langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}, r, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle
$$

8. r-nat-f

$$
\frac {\begin{array}{c}r \in e _ {i} \qquad y _ {i} \in e _ {p , j} ^ {\prime} \qquad \langle \hat {e} _ {j} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}\\\langle c ^ {\prime} , e _ {p , j} ^ {\prime} [ e _ {i} / y _ {i} ] , r \rangle \rightsquigarrow \langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {m ^ {\prime}}) , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle\end{array}}{\langle \psi_ {p} (\hat {e} _ {1} , \ldots , \hat {e} _ {m}) \circ c , p (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {n}) , r \rangle \rightsquigarrow}\\\langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m ^ {\prime}}) , \psi_ {p} (\hat {e} _ {1} \circ e _ {p , 1} [ c / p ] , \ldots ,\\\hat {e} _ {j - 1} \circ e _ {p, j - 1} [ c / p ] , c ^ {\prime \prime} , \hat {e} _ {j + 1} \circ e _ {p, j + 1} [ c / p ] , \ldots , \hat {e} _ {m} \circ e _ {p , m} [ c / p ]) \rangle
$$

where the objects  $l$ ,  $r$  and  $p$  are defined as (lc), (rc) and (p), respectively, and  $r \in e_i$  means that  $r$  appears in a functorial expression  $e_i$ .

we have to show that the rules are well-formed, but we have to show their soundness at the same time. we will do this in section 4.3 as well as showing that every reduction terminates (in other words, every element is normalizable by these rules).

# 4.2 an example of using reduction rules

in this section, we will see an example computation in cpl using the reduction rules defined in the previous section. since computation by hand is very tiresome, we do only one example, but we will see some more examples of cpl computation done by a computer in chapter 5.

let us assume that we have declared the terminal object as in subsection 3.3.1, products as in subsection 3.3.2, exponentials as in subsection 3.3.3 and natural number object as in subsection 3.3.4. we can write down instances of the reduction rules in definition 4.1.7 as follows.

$$
\langle \mathbf {i}, c \rangle \rightarrow c \quad (\mathrm {i d e n t})
$$

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \quad \langle e _ {1} , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}} \tag {comp}
$$

for the terminal object, we have

$$
\langle !, c \rangle \rightarrow ! \circ c. \quad (\mathrm {r} - \mathrm {f a c t} _ {!})
$$

for products, we have

$$
\langle \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1}, e _ {2}), c \rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1}, e _ {2}) \circ c, \quad \left(\mathrm {r - f a c t} _ {\text {p a i r}}\right)
$$

$$
\frac {\langle c , r , r \rangle \sim \langle \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \qquad \langle e _ {1} , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \mathrm {p i} 1 , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}, \qquad \left(\mathrm {r - n a t} _ {\mathrm {p i} 1}\right)
$$

and

$$
\langle \operatorname {p a i r} \left(e _ {1}, e _ {2}\right) \circ c, \operatorname {p r o d}, \operatorname {p r o d} \rangle \sim \langle \operatorname {p a i r} \left(e _ {1}, e _ {2}\right), c \rangle . \quad \left(\mathrm {r} - \mathrm {n a t} - \mathrm {v} _ {\mathrm {p r o d}}\right)
$$

if we combine  $(\mathrm{r - nat_{pi1}})$  and  $(\mathrm{r - nat - v_{prod}})$  together, we get a familiar rule

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {1} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \operatorname {p i 1} , \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}. \quad \left(\mathrm {r} - \mathrm {n a t} _ {\mathrm {p i l}} ^ {\prime}\right)
$$

similarly, for 'pi2', we have

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \mathrm {p i} 2 , \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}. \quad \left(\mathrm {r - n a t} _ {\mathrm {p i} 2} ^ {\prime}\right)
$$

for exponentials, as we have seen in the previous section, we have the following two rules.

$$
\langle \operatorname {c u r r y} (e), c \rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {c u r r y} (e) \circ c \quad (\mathrm {r} - \mathrm {f a c t} _ {\text {c u r r y}})
$$

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {1} , c \rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {c u r r y} (e) \circ c ^ {\prime} \qquad \langle e , \operatorname {p a i r} (c ^ {\prime \prime} , e _ {2} \circ c) \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \operatorname {e v a l} , \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}} \qquad (\mathrm {r - n a t} _ {\mathrm {e v a l}} ^ {\prime})
$$

for natural number object, we have

$$
\langle \text {z e r o}, c \rangle \rightarrow \text {z e r o} \circ c, \quad (\mathrm {l} - \mathrm {n a t} _ {\text {z e r o}})
$$

$$
\langle \operatorname {s u c c}, c \rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {s u c c} \circ c, \quad (\mathrm {l} - \mathrm {n a t} _ {\operatorname {s u c c}})
$$

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {1} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \operatorname {p r} (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) , \operatorname {z e r o} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}, \quad \left(\mathrm {l - f a c t} _ {\mathrm {z e r o}}\right)
$$

and

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {2} \circ \pr (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \pr (e _ {1} , e _ {2}) , \operatorname {s u c c} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}. \quad \left(\mathrm {l - f a c t} _ {\text {s u c c}}\right)
$$

now let us try to calculate  $1 + 1$  in cpl. the addition function is defined in subsection 3.3.4 as

$$
\mathrm {a d d} = \operatorname {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p r o d} (\operatorname {p r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2), \operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {e v a l})), \mathbf {i}).
$$

if we expand 'prod' by 'pair', we get

$$
\mathrm {a d d} = \mathrm {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {p r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2), \operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {e v a l})) \circ \mathrm {p i} 1, \mathrm {p i} 2).
$$

therefore, the calculation  $1 + 1$  corresponds to the following reduction.

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\langle \text {e v a l} \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {p r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {p i} 2), \operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {e v a l})) \circ \operatorname {p i} 1, \operatorname {p i} 2),} \\ & {\qquad \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}, \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}) \rangle \rightarrow c} \end{array} \tag {1}
$$

from (comp),

$$
\begin{array}{c} \langle \text {p a i r (p r (c u r r y (p i 2) , c u r r y (s u c c \circ e v a l)) \circ p i 1 , p i 2)} , \\ \text {p a i r (s u c c \circ z e r o , s u c c \circ z e r o) \rangle \rightarrow c _ {1}} \\ \langle \text {e v a l , c _ {1} \rangle \rightarrow c} \\ \hline \langle \text {e v a l \circ p a i r (p r (c u r r y (p i 2) , c u r r y (s u c c \circ e v a l)) \circ p i 1 , p i 2)} , \\ \text {p a i r (s u c c \circ z e r o , s u c c \circ z e r o) \rangle \rightarrow c} \end{array}
$$

from (r-factpair),  $c_{1}$  is

pair(pr(curry(pi2, curry(succ o eval) o pi1, pi2) o pair(succ o zero, succ o zero),

so we need to calculate

$$
\begin{array}{c} \langle \text {e v a l}, \text {p a i r (p r (c u r r y (p i 2 , c u r r y (s u c c \circ e v a l) \circ p i 1 , p i 2)} \circ} \\ \text {p a i r (s u c c \circ z e r o , s u c c \circ z e r o)} \rangle \rightarrow c. \end{array}

from (comp),

$$
\frac {\langle \mathrm {p i} 1 , \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o} , \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}) \rangle \rightarrow c _ {3}}{\left\langle \operatorname {p r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2) , \operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {e v a l})) , c _ {3} \right\rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {c u r r y} (e _ {1}) \circ c _ {2}} \quad (3)}{\langle \operatorname {p r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2) , \operatorname {c u r r y} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {e v a l})) \circ \mathrm {p i} 1 ,} \quad \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o} , \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}) \rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {c u r r y} (e _ {1}) \circ c _ {2}}

from  $(\mathrm{r - nat}_{\mathrm{pi1}}^{\prime})$

$$
\frac {\langle \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o} , ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {3}}{\langle \operatorname {p i 1} , \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o} , \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}) \rangle \rightarrow c _ {3}},

and from  $(\mathrm{l - nat}_{\mathrm{zero}})$  and  $(\mathrm{l - nat}_{\mathrm{suc}})$ ,  $c_{3}$  is

\frac {\langle \mathrm {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2) , ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {4}}{\langle \mathrm {p r} (\mathrm {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2) , \mathrm {c u r r y} (\mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {e v a l})) , \mathrm {z e r o} \circ ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {4}}

\langle \operatorname {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2) \circ !,! \rangle \rightarrow \operatorname {c u r r y} (e _ {2}) \circ c _ {6}
$$

\langle e _ {2}, \operatorname {p a i r} \left(c _ {6}, \operatorname {p i} 2 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}, \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o})\right) \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}
$$

\langle \mathrm {p i} 2, \operatorname {p a i r} (! \circ !, \mathrm {p i} 2 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}, \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o})) \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}
$$

$$
\frac {\langle \mathrm {p i} 2 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\text {s u c c} \circ \text {z e r o} , \text {s u c c} \circ \text {z e r o}) , ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}}{\langle \mathrm {p i} 2 , \operatorname {p a i r} (! \circ ! , \mathrm {p i} 2 \circ \operatorname {p a i r} (\text {s u c c} \circ \text {z e r o} , \text {s u c c} \circ \text {z e r o})) \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}}
$$

from (comp),

$$
\frac {\langle \mathrm {p a i r (s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o , s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o)} , ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {6}}{\langle \mathrm {p i 2} , c _ {6} \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}}}{\langle \mathrm {p i 2} \circ \mathrm {p a i r (s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o , s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o)} , ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}}
$$

from (r-fact $_{\mathrm{pair}}$ ),  $c_6$  is

$$
\operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}, \operatorname {s u c c} \circ \operatorname {z e r o}) \circ !,
$$

so we have

$$
\langle \mathrm {p i} 2, \mathrm {p a i r (s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o}, \mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o}) \circ ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}
$$

and  $\mathrm{(r - nat_{pi2}^{\prime})}$

$$
\frac {\langle \mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o} , ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}}{\langle \mathrm {p i 2} , \mathrm {p a i r} (\mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o} , \mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o}) \circ ! \rangle \rightarrow c _ {5}}
$$

by using (l-nat<sub>zero</sub>) and (l-nat<sub>succ</sub>),  $c_{5}$  is

$$
\mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o} \circ !.
$$

now, we go back to (5) and we need to calculate

$$

$$

but this is straightforward from  $(\mathrm{l - nat}_{\mathrm{succ}})$  and  $c$  is

$$
\mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o} \circ !.
$$

therefore, the reduction (1) is

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\langle \mathrm {e v a l} \circ \mathrm {p a i r} (\mathrm {p r} (\mathrm {c u r r y} (\mathrm {p i} 2), \mathrm {c u r r y} (\mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {e v a l})) \circ \mathrm {p i} 1, \mathrm {p i} 2),} \\ & {\qquad \mathrm {p a i r} (\mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o}, \mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o}) \rangle \rightarrow \mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {s u c c} \circ \mathrm {z e r o} \circ !} \end{array}
$$

that is, we have shown  $1 + 1$  is  $2$  in cpl.

# 4.3 well-definedness and normalization theorem for reduction rules

in section 4.1, we discussed what is computation in cpl and obtained a set of reduction rules (definition 4.1.7). usual questions to be asked when we get reduction rules are, firstly, whether they are well-defined or not and, secondly, whether they are normalizing or not. in this section, we will answer both questions affirmatively.

let us assume in this section that we are working in a csl theory  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi, \theta \rangle$  which is obtained by a sequence of cdt declarations,  $d_{1}, \ldots, d_{l}$ , each of which is a computable object declaration.

first, we prove the well-definedness of the reduction rules.

theorem 4.3.1: well-definedness theorem: let  $e \circ c$  be an element in  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$  and  $c$  be a canonical element. if from the rules listed in definition 4.1.7 we deduce

$$
\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime},
$$

then  $c'$  is a canonical element and  $e \circ c = c'$  holds in  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi, \theta \rangle$  (or in any csl theory structure of this).

proof: we prove at the same time that for a canonical element  $c$ , a right functor  $r$ , a functorial expression  $e$  productive in  $r$  which is more general than the type of  $c$ , if the rules in definition 4.1.7 deduce

$$
\langle c, e, r \rangle \sim \langle e, c ^ {\prime} \rangle ,
$$

then  $e$  is  $\psi_r(e_1, \ldots, e_m)$  for some  $e_1, \ldots, e_n$ ,  $c'$  is a canonical element and  $c = e[\psi(e_1, \ldots, e_m) / r] \circ c'$  holds in  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi, \theta \rangle$ .

the proof is done by mathematical induction on the length of reduction, so all we have to do is to check each reduction rule.

1. ident

$$
\langle \mathbf {i}, c \rangle \rightarrow c
$$

it is trivial from  $\mathbf{i} \circ c = c$ .

2. comp

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \qquad \langle e _ {1} , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

from the induction hypothesis  $c'$  is canonical and  $c' = e_1 \circ c'' = e_1 \circ e_2 \circ c$ .

3. l-nat

$$
\left\langle \alpha_ {l, j}, c \right\rangle \rightarrow \alpha_ {l, j} \circ c
$$

it is trivial since  $\alpha_{l,j} \circ c$  is a canonical element.

4. r-fact

$$
\langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}), c \rangle \rightarrow \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ c
$$

it is again trivial since  $\psi_r(e_1, \ldots, e_m) \circ c$  is a canonical element.

5. l-fact

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {j} \circ e _ {l , j} [ \psi_ {l} (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {m}) / l ] , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \psi_ {l} (e _ {1} , \ldots , e _ {m}) , \alpha_ {l , j} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

if  $\alpha_{l,j} \circ c$  is canonical,  $c$  is canonical, and from  $(\mathrm{leq}_j)$

$$
e _ {j} \circ e _ {l, j} [ \psi_ {l} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) / l ] \circ c = \psi_ {l} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ \alpha_ {l, j} \circ c.
$$

therefore, the premise of the rule is well-formed, so from the induction hypothesis  $c'$  is canonical, and

$$
c ^ {\prime} = e _ {j} \circ e _ {l, j} [ \psi_ {l} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) / l ] \circ c = \psi_ {l} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ \alpha_ {l, j} \circ c.
$$

6. r-nat

$$
\begin{array}{c} \langle c, e _ {r, j}, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \\ \langle e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} [ \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) / r ] \circ e _ {j}, c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime} \\ \hline \langle \alpha_ {r, j}, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime} \end{array}
$$

since  $\alpha_{r,j}$  can be composed with  $c$  and  $\alpha_{r,j}$  has the type  $e_{r,j} \to e_{r,j}'$ ,  $c$  is an element of a functorial expression not more general than  $e_{r,j}$ . from the induction hypothesis,  $c''$  is a canonical element and

$$
e _ {r, j} [ \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) / r ] \circ c ^ {\prime \prime} = c.
$$

therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {r, j} \circ \mathcal {c} \\ = \alpha_ {r, j} \circ e _ {r, j} \left[ \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right) / r \right] \circ c ^ {\prime \prime} \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \left(\text {f r o m} (\operatorname {r e q} _ {j})\right) \\ = e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} \left[ \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}\right) / r \right] \circ e _ {j} \circ c ^ {\prime \prime} \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \tag {from hypothesis} \\ = c. \quad \text {(f r o m} \\ \end{array}
$$

7. r-nat-v

$$
\langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}, r, r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle
$$

$r$  is a variable, so it is more general than anything, so the rule is well-formed. since  $\psi_r(e_1,\ldots ,e_m)\circ c''$  is a canonical element, so is  $c^{\prime \prime}$

# 8. r-nat-f

$$
r \in e _ {i} \quad y _ {i} \in e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} \quad \langle \hat {e} _ {j}, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}
$$

$$
\langle c ^ {\prime}, e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} [ e _ {i} / y _ {i} ], r \rangle \sim \langle \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m ^ {\prime}}), c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \psi_ {p} (\hat {e} _ {1}, \dots , \hat {e} _ {m}) \circ c, p (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {n}), r \rangle \sim \\ \langle \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m ^ {\prime}}\right), \psi_ {p} \left(\hat {e} _ {1} \circ e _ {p, 1} [ c / p ], \dots , \right. \\ \hat {e} _ {j - 1} \circ e _ {p, j - 1} [ c / p ], c ^ {\prime \prime}, \hat {e} _ {j + 1} \circ e _ {p, j + 1} [ c / p ], \dots , \hat {e} _ {m} \circ e _ {p, m} [ c / p ]) \rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

$\psi_p(\hat{e}_1, \ldots) \circ c = \psi_p(\hat{e}_1 \circ e_{p,1}[c / p], \ldots)$  and from the typing rule of  $\psi_p$ ,  $\hat{e}_j \circ e_{p,j}[c / p] = \hat{e}_j \circ c$  is an element of a functorial expression which is not more general than  $e_{p,j}'[e_1 / y_1, \ldots]$  which is not more general than  $e_{p,j}'[e_i / y_i]$ . therefore, the premises of the rule are well-formed and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {e} _ {j} \circ e _ {p, j} [ c / p ] \\ = \hat {e} _ {j} \circ c \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots (e _ {p, j} \text {i s s i m p l y} p) \\ = c ^ {\prime} \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \left(\text {f r o m} \langle \hat {e} _ {j}, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}\right) \\ = e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} \left[ e _ {i} / y _ {i} \right] \left[ \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots\right) / r \right] \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}. \quad \dots \dots (\text {f r o m} \langle c ^ {\prime}, e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} [ e _ {i} / y _ {i} ], r \rangle \sim \dots) \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} p (e _ {1}, \dots) [ \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots) / r ] \circ \psi_ {p} (\hat {e} _ {1} \circ e _ {p, 1} [ c / p ], \dots , c ^ {\prime \prime}, \dots) \\ = \psi_ {p} \left(\alpha_ {p, 1}, \dots , e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} \left[ e _ {i} / y _ {i} \right] \left[ \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots\right) / r \right], \dots\right) \\ \circ \psi_ {p} (\hat {e} _ {1} \circ e _ {p, 1} [ c / p ], \dots , c ^ {\prime \prime}, \dots) \quad \dots \dots (\text {e x p a n d} p) \\ = \psi_ {p} \left(\hat {e} _ {1} \circ e _ {p, 1} [ c / p ], \dots , e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} \left[ e _ {i} / y _ {i} \right] \left[ \psi_ {r} \left(e _ {1}, \dots\right) / r \right] \circ c ^ {\prime \prime}, \dots\right) \\ \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots (from 4.1.4 and (req _ {k))} \\ = \psi_ {p} \left(\hat {e} _ {1} \circ e _ {p, 1} [ c / p ], \dots , \hat {e} _ {j} \circ e _ {p, j} [ c / p ], \dots\right) \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \text {(f r o m a b o v e)} \\ = \psi_ {p} \left(\hat {e} _ {1}, \dots , \hat {e} _ {m}\right) \circ c. \quad \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots (from 4.1.4) \\ \end{array}
$$

of course,  $\psi_p(\hat{e}_1\circ e_{p,1}[c / p],\ldots ,c^{\prime \prime},\ldots)$  is a canonical element.

we have proved the theorem as well as shown the well-formedness of the reduction rules.

next, we prove the normalization, that is to prove the following theorem.

theorem 4.3.2: normalization theorem: for a canonical element  $c$  and a csl expression  $e$  whose domain is compatible with the codomain of  $c$  (i.e. we can have  $e \circ c$  as an element), there is a canonical element  $c'$  such that

$$
\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}
$$

by the rules listed in definition 4.1.7.

before proving this theorem, we need some preparation. note that the theorem proves the two things together: the existence of  $c'$  and the reducibility of  $\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c'$ . therefore, from the existence part, we will have the following corollary.

corollary 4.3.3: for any element  $e$  in  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi \rangle$ , there is a canonical element  $c$  such that  $e = c$  holds in  $\langle \gamma, \delta, \psi, \theta \rangle$ .

showing the reducibility can be regarded as showing the termination of computation in cpl. so, every program terminates in cpl. this is what we expected because we only use primitive recursions.

first, we show a property of canonical elements.

proposition 4.3.4: a canonical element of  $r(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$  for a right object  $r$  defined by (rc) has the following form.

$$
\psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \ldots , e _ {m}) \circ c
$$

where  $c$  is another canonical element.

on the other hand, a canonical element of  $l(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$  for a left object  $l$  defined by (lc) has the following form.

$$
\alpha_ {l, j} \circ c
$$

where  $c$  is another canonical element.

proof: a canonical element only consists of natural transformations of left objects and factorizers of right objects. therefore, a canonical element should look like either

$$
\alpha_ {l, j} \circ c \qquad \text {o r} \qquad \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ c.
$$

from the typing rules in section 2.4, the first one always gives an element of  $l(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$  and the second one gives an element of  $r(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ .

from this proposition, we can see that whenever we have

$$
\langle e, c \rangle \qquad \text {o r} \qquad \langle c, e, r \rangle
$$

we can always apply exactly one of the rules in definition 4.1.7. in other words, the computation in cpl is never stuck and deterministic. we can always proceed to the next computation step.

we are going to prove the normalization theorem by the computability method due to tait [tait 67]. this method is often used to show normalization of various systems especially that of lambda calculi (see, for example, [stenlund 72] and [lambek and scott 86]) where two kinds of induction are used: induction on types and induction on structures. the method usually goes for lambda calculi as follows:

1. define the notion of computable terms inductively on types.  
2. show that any computable term is normalizable.  
3. show that all the terms are computable by induction on terms.  
4. therefore, any term is normalizable.

the notion of computable terms is stronger than that of normalizable terms, but we need this stronger notion (which is a part of the essence of the tait computability method) to carry out the normalization proof. the computability predicate also divide the two inductions involved in the proof clearly. in our case, the normalization proof goes as follows.

1. we define the notion of computable canonical elements inductively on types. intuitively, a canonical element  $c$  is computable if

(a)  $\alpha_{l} \circ c$  is computable if  $c$  is computable.  
(b)  $\psi_r(e)\circ c$  is computable if there is a reduction  $\langle \alpha_r,\psi_r(e)\circ c\rangle \rightarrow c'$  such that  $c^{\prime}$  is computable, that is all the components of  $\psi_r(e)\circ c$  are computable.

2. we define the notion of calculability for expressions (we could have called it computability as well as is conventional in proofs about lambda calculi, but we distinguish them for clarity). an expression  $e$  is calculable if for any computable canonical element  $c$  there is a reduction  $\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c'$  such that  $c'$  is computable. note that an expression  $e$  is normalizable if for any canonical element  $e$  there is a reduction  $\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c$ , whereas  $e$  is calculable if there is a reduction for any computable canonical element. therefore, it should be easier to prove that an expression is calculable than to prove it is normalizable.  
3. we will prove that all the expressions are calculable by structural induction.  
4. as an easy corollary, we can show that any canonical element is computable.  
5. finally, we prove that all the reductions are normalizing.

first, let us assign for each  $n$ -ary functor  $f$  a function which given sets  $c_1, \ldots, c_n$  of canonical elements of type  $e_1, \ldots, e_n$  gives a set of canonical elements  $c$  of type  $f(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$ . we write  $\tilde{f}$  for the function (i.e.  $c = \tilde{f}(c_1, \ldots, c_n)$ ).  $\tilde{f}$  is monotonic in the  $i$ th argument if  $f$  is covariant in the  $i$ th argument, and  $\tilde{f}$  is anti-monotonic in the  $i$ th argument if  $f$  is contravariant in the  $i$ th argument.

definition 4.3.5: 1. for a left object

left object  $l(x)$  with  $\psi_l$  is

$$
\alpha_ {l} \colon e _ {l} (l, x) \to l
$$

end object,

$\tilde{l}(c)$  is the minimal fixed point of the following monotonic function:

$$
s \longmapsto \left\{\alpha_ {l} \circ c \mid c \in \tilde {e} _ {l} (s, c) \right\}
$$

the minimal fixed point can be calculated as the least upper bound of the following ascending chain:

$$
\tilde {l} _ {0} (c) \subseteq \tilde {l} _ {1} (c) \subseteq \dots \subseteq \tilde {l} _ {n} (c) \subseteq \tilde {l} _ {n + 1} (c) \subseteq \dots \subseteq \tilde {l} _ {\omega} (c) \subseteq \dots
$$

where  $\tilde{l}_0(c)$  is  $\varnothing$  and

$$
\tilde {l} _ {\beta + 1} (c) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \left\{\alpha_ {l} \circ c \mid c \in \tilde {e} _ {l} (\tilde {l} _ {\beta} (c), c) \right\}.
$$

2. for a right object

right object  $r(x)$  with  $\psi_r$  is

$$
\alpha_ {r} \colon r \to e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (r, x)
$$

end object,

$\tilde{r}(c)$  is the maximal fixed point of the following monotonic function:

$$
s \longmapsto \left\{\psi_ {r} (e) \circ c \mid \langle \alpha_ {r}, \psi_ {r} (e) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime} \text {s u c h t h a t} c ^ {\prime} \in \tilde {e} _ {r} ^ {\prime} (s, c) \right\}
$$

the maximal fixed point can be calculated as the greatest lower bound of the following descending chain:

$$
\tilde {r} _ {0} (c) \supseteq \tilde {r} _ {1} (c) \supseteq \dots \supseteq \tilde {r} _ {n} (c) \supseteq \tilde {r} _ {n + 1} (c) \supseteq \dots \supseteq \tilde {r} _ {\omega} (c) \supseteq \dots
$$

where  $\tilde{r}_0(c)$  is the set of all the canonical elements of  $r(e)$  (where  $c$  is a set of canonical elements of type  $e$ ) and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {r} _ {\beta + 1} (c) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \left\{\psi_ {r} (e) \circ c \mid \right. \\ \langle \alpha_ {r}, \psi_ {r} (e) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime} \text {s u c h t h a t} c ^ {\prime} \in \tilde {e} _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\tilde {r} _ {\beta} (c), c) \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

3. for a right object

right object  $r^{\prime}(x)$  with  $\psi_{r^{\prime}}$  is

$$
\alpha_ {r ^ {\prime}} \colon \operatorname {p r o d} \left(r, e _ {r ^ {\prime}} (x)\right)\rightarrow e _ {r ^ {\prime}} ^ {\prime} \left(r ^ {\prime}, x\right)
$$

end object,

$\tilde{r}^{\prime}(c)$  is the maximal fixed point of the following monotonic function:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} s \longmapsto \big \{\psi_ {r ^ {\prime}} (e) \circ c   | & \text {f o r a n y} c ^ {\prime} \in \tilde {e} _ {r ^ {\prime}} (c) \langle \alpha_ {r ^ {\prime}}, \text {p a i r} (\psi_ {r ^ {\prime}} (e) \circ c, c ^ {\prime}) \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \\ & \text {s u c h t h a t} c ^ {\prime \prime} \in \tilde {e} _ {r ^ {\prime}} ^ {\prime} (s, c) \big \} \end{array}
$$

we can similarly define  $\tilde{r}_{\beta}^{\prime}(c)$

well-definedness: we have to show that  $\tilde{f}$  is monotonic or anti-monotonic according to the variance of  $f$ . we prove this by induction on the order of declaration of objects.

1. if  $f$  is the left object  $l$  above and covariant, we show that  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}$  is monotonic by induction on  $\beta$ .  $\tilde{l}_0$  is trivially monotonic.  $\tilde{l}_{\beta + 1}(c)$  is

$$
\{\alpha_ {l} \circ c \mid c \in \tilde {e} _ {l} (\tilde {l} _ {\beta} (c), c) \}.
$$

from the induction hypothesis  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}$  is monotonic. since  $e_{l}(l,x)$  is covariant in both  $l$  and  $x$ , from the other induction hypothesis  $\tilde{e}_l$  is monotonic. therefore,  $\tilde{e}_l(\tilde{l}_\beta (c),c)$  is monotonic in  $c$ , and  $\tilde{l}_{\beta +1}$  is monotonic. hence,  $\tilde{l}$  is monotonic. similarly, we can show that  $\tilde{l}$  is anti-monotonic if  $l$  is contravariant.

2. if  $f$  is the right object  $r$  above and covariant, we show that  $\tilde{r}_{\beta}$  is monotonic by induction on  $\beta$ .  $\tilde{r}_0$  is trivially monotonic.  $\tilde{r}_{\beta +1}(c)$  is

$$
\{\psi_ {r} (e) \circ c | \langle \alpha_ {r}, \psi_ {r} (e) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime} \wedge c ^ {\prime} \in \tilde {e} _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\tilde {r} _ {\beta} (c), c) \}.
$$

from the induction hypothesis,  $\tilde{r}_{\beta}$  is monotonic, and from the other induction hypothesis,  $\tilde{e}_r^\prime$  is monotonic in both arguments. therefore,  $\tilde{r}_{\beta +1}$  is monotonic, and by induction  $\tilde{r}$  is monotonic. we can similarly show that  $\tilde{r}$  is anti-monotonic if  $r$  is contravariant.

3. if  $f$  is the right object  $r'$  above and covariant, we show that  $\tilde{r}_{\beta}'$  is monotonic by induction on  $\beta$ .  $\tilde{r}_0'$  is trivially monotonic.  $\tilde{r}_{\beta + 1}'(c)$  is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\{\psi_ {r ^ {\prime}} (e) \circ c \mid \forall c ^ {\prime} \in \tilde {e} _ {r ^ {\prime}} (c) \langle \alpha_ {r ^ {\prime}}, \operatorname {p a i r} \left(\psi_ {r ^ {\prime}} (e) \circ c, c ^ {\prime}\right) \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \wedge \right. \\ c ^ {\prime \prime} \in \tilde {e} _ {r ^ {\prime}} ^ {\prime} (\tilde {r} _ {\beta} ^ {\prime} (c), c) \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

from the induction hypothesis,  $\tilde{r}_{\beta}^{\prime}$  is monotonic, and from the other induction hypothesis,  $\tilde{e}_{r^{\prime}}$  is anti-monotonic and  $\tilde{e}_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}$  is monotonic in both arguments. therefore,  $\tilde{r}_{\beta +1}^{\prime}$  is monotonic, and by induction  $\tilde{r}^{\prime}$  is monotonic. we can similarly show that  $\tilde{r}^{\prime}$  is anti-monotonic if  $r^{\prime}$  is contravariant.

we now define the notion of computability and calculability.

definition 4.3.6: the set  $\omega_{f(e_1,\ldots ,e_n)}$  of computable canonical elements of type  $f(e_{1},\dots ,e_{n})$  is defined inductively by  $\tilde{f} (\omega_{e_1},\dots ,\omega_{e_n})$

definition 4.3.7: an csl expression  $e$  of type  $e \to e'$  is called calculable with respect to  $c \to c'$  for  $c \subseteq \omega_e$  and  $c' \subseteq \omega_{e'}$  if for any  $c$  in  $c$  there is a reduction  $\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c'$  such that  $c'$  is in  $c'$ . when  $e$  is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e \to \omega_{e'}$ , we simply say that  $e$  is calculable.

example 4.3.8: 1. for the terminal object '1'

right object 1 with!

end object,

since there is no natural transformation, any canonical element is computable. let us use  $\star$  to denote an arbitrary element of  $\tilde{1}$ .

2. for the left object 'nat' of natural numbers

left object nat with pr is

zero:  $1\to \mathrm{nat}$

succ: nat  $\rightarrow$  nat

end object,

$\widetilde{\mathrm{nat}}_0$  is  $\emptyset$ .  $\widetilde{\mathrm{nat}}_1$  is

$$
\{\mathrm {z e r o} \circ c, \mathrm {s u c c} \circ c ^ {\prime} \mid c \in \tilde {1} \wedge c ^ {\prime} \in \widetilde {\mathrm {n a t}} _ {0} \} = \{\mathrm {z e r o} \circ \star \}.
$$

similarly  $\widetilde{\mathrm{nat}}_2$  consists of zero  $\circ \star$  and succ  $\circ$  zero  $\circ \star$ . in general,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{nat}}_n$  is the set of  $n$  elements corresponding to a set of 0, 1, 2, ..., and  $n - 1$ . therefore,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{nat}}$  is the set of all the canonical elements of nat.

3. for the right object 'prod' of products

right object  $\operatorname{prod}(x, y)$  with pair is

pi1: prod  $\rightarrow x$

pi2: prod  $\to y$

end object,

a canonical element pair  $(e_1, e_2) \circ c$  is computable if there are reductions

$$
\langle \operatorname {p i} 1, \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1}, e _ {2}) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c _ {1} \qquad \text {a n d} \qquad \langle \operatorname {p i} 2, \operatorname {p a i r} (e _ {1}, e _ {2}) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c _ {2}
$$

such that  $c_{1}$  and  $c_{2}$  are computable, that is a canonical element of prod is computable if its components are computable. since the reductions above are equivalent to  $\langle e_1, c \rangle \rightarrow c_1$  and  $\langle e_2, c \rangle \rightarrow c_2$ , if  $c$  is computable and  $e_1$  and  $e_2$  are calculable,  $\operatorname{pair}(e_1, e_2) \circ c$  is computable.

4. for the right object 'exp' of exponentials

right object  $\exp (x,y)$  with curry is eval:  $\mathrm{prod}(\exp ,x)\to y$  end object,

a canonical element  $\mathrm{curry}(e) \circ c$  of type  $\exp(e, e')$  is computable, if for any computable canonical element  $c'$  of type  $e'$  there is a reduction

$$
\langle \operatorname {e v a l}, \operatorname {p a i r} (\operatorname {c u r r y} (e) \circ c, c ^ {\prime}) \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime}
$$

and  $c''$  is computable. the reduction is equivalent to  $\langle e, \operatorname{pair}(c, c') \rangle \rightarrow c''$ . since  $\operatorname{pair}(c, c')$  is computable, there is such a reduction if  $e$  is calculable. remember that  $\operatorname{curry}(e)$  corresponds to the closure of  $e$  (or lambda closed term of  $e$ ) so that we can say that a closure is calculable if the application with canonical elements always results in canonical elements. this exactly corresponds to the definition of computability for lambda expressions (see [stenlund 72]).

5. for the right object 'inlist' of infinite lists

right object inflist  $(x)$  with fold is head: inflist  $\rightarrow x$  tail: inflist  $\rightarrow$  inflist   
end object,

let us figure out the computable canonical elements of type inflist(nat).  $\widetilde{\mathrm{inflist}}_0(\omega_{\mathrm{nat}})$  is the set of all the canonical elements of type inflist(nat).  $\widetilde{\mathrm{inflist}}_1(\omega_{\mathrm{nat}})$  is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\{\operatorname {f o l d} \left(e _ {1}, e _ {2}\right) \circ c \mid \langle \text {h e a d , f o l d} \left(e _ {1}, e _ {2}\right) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c _ {1} \wedge c _ {1} \in \omega_ {\mathrm {n a t}} \wedge \right. \\ \langle \text {t a i l}, \operatorname {f o l d} \left(e _ {1}, e _ {2}\right) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c _ {2} \wedge c _ {2} \in \operatorname {i n f l i s t} _ {0} \left(\omega_ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) \rbrace \\ = \left\{\operatorname {f o l d} \left(e _ {1}, e _ {2}\right) \circ c \mid \langle e _ {1}, c \rangle \rightarrow c _ {1} \wedge \langle \operatorname {f o l d} \left(e _ {1}, e _ {2}\right) \circ e _ {2}, c \rangle \rightarrow c _ {2} \right\}. \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, if  $e_1$  and  $e_2$  are calculable and  $c$  is computable,  $\mathrm{fold}(e_1, e_2) \circ c$  is in  $\widetilde{\mathrm{inflist}}_1(\omega_{\mathrm{nat}})$ . we can inductively show that it is in any  $\mathrm{inflist}_{\beta}(\omega_{\mathrm{nat}})$ , and, therefore, it is in  $\widetilde{\mathrm{inflist}}(\omega_{\mathrm{nat}})$ .

6. for the left object of ordinals

left object ord with pro is  
ozero:  $1 \to$  ord  
sup:  $\exp(\text{nat}, \text{ord}) \to \text{ord}$   
end object,

$\widetilde{\mathrm{ord}}_0$  is empty,  $\widetilde{\mathrm{ord}}_1$  is  $\{\mathrm{zero} \circ \star\}$ , and

$$
\widetilde {\mathrm {o r d} _ {2}}
$$

$$
= \{\sup \circ c, \mathrm {o z e r o} \circ \star | c \in \widetilde {\mathrm {e x p}} (\widetilde {\mathrm {n a t}}, \widetilde {\mathrm {o r d}} _ {1}) \}
$$

$$
= \left\{\sup  \circ \operatorname {c u r r y} (e) \circ c, \text {o z e r o} \circ \star \mid \forall c ^ {\prime} \in \widetilde {\operatorname {n a t}} \langle e, \operatorname {p a i r} (c, c ^ {\prime}) \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \wedge \right.
$$

$$
c ^ {\prime \prime} \in \widetilde {\operatorname {o r d} _ {1}} \}
$$

$$
= \{\sup  \circ \mathrm {c u r r y} (e) \circ c, \mathrm {o z e r o} \circ \star \mid \forall c ^ {\prime} \in \omega_ {\mathrm {n a t}} \langle e, \mathrm {p a i r} (c, c ^ {\prime}) \rangle \rightarrow \mathrm {o z e r o} \circ \star \}.
$$

in general,

$$
\widetilde {\operatorname {o r d}} _ {\beta + 1} = \left\{\sup  \circ \operatorname {c u r r y} (e) \circ c, \text {o z e r o} \circ \star \right.
$$

$$
\forall c ^ {\prime} \in \omega_ {\mathrm {n a t}} \langle e, \operatorname {p a i r} (c, c ^ {\prime}) \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \wedge c ^ {\prime \prime} \in \widetilde {\operatorname {o r d}} _ {\beta} \rbrace
$$

therefore, a canonical element  $\operatorname{supocurry}(e) \circ c$  is computable if the following reductions always exist:

$$
\langle e, \operatorname {p a i r} (c, c _ {1}) \rangle \rightarrow \sup  \circ \operatorname {c u r r y} (e _ {1}) \circ c _ {1} ^ {\prime}
$$

$$
\langle e _ {1}, \operatorname {p a i r} \left(c _ {1} ^ {\prime}, c _ {2}\right) \rangle \rightarrow \sup  \circ \operatorname {c u r r y} \left(e _ {2}\right) \circ c _ {2} ^ {\prime}
$$

$$
\langle e _ {2}, \operatorname {p a i r} \left(c _ {2} ^ {\prime}, c _ {3}\right) \rangle \rightarrow \sup  \circ \operatorname {c u r r y} \left(e _ {3}\right) \circ c _ {3} ^ {\prime}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{c c c} \cdot & \cdot & \cdot \\ \cdot & \cdot & \cdot \end{array}
$$

$$
\langle e _ {\beta}, \operatorname {p a i r} \left(c _ {\beta} ^ {\prime}, c _ {\beta + 1}\right) \rangle \rightarrow \text {o z e r o} \circ \star
$$

the next proposition intuitively means that functors preserve the structure of data. for example, when the functor 'list' (or map in ml and mapcar in lisp) is applied to a list, it only changes the components of the list and preserves the length.

proposition 4.3.9: let  $f$  be  $n$ -ary functor, and  $e_1, \ldots, e_n$  be csl expressions calculable with respect to  $c_i \to c_i'$ . then  $f(e_1, \ldots, e_n)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{f}(c_1'', \ldots, c_n'') \to \tilde{f}(c_1'''', \ldots, c_n''')$ , where  $c_i''$  is  $c_i$  and  $c_i'''$  is  $c_i'$  if  $f$  is covariant in the  $i$ th argument and  $c_i''$  is  $c_i'$  and  $c_i'''$  is  $c_i$  if  $f$  is contravariant in the  $i$ th argument.

proof: we prove this by induction on the order of declarations of objects.

1. let  $f$  be a left object declared by

$$
\text {l e f t o b j e c t} l (x) \text {w i t h} \psi_ {l} \text {i s}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {l} \colon e _ {l} (l, x) \to l
$$

$$
\mathrm {e n d o b j e c t}
$$

which is covariant in  $x$ ,  $e$  be a csl expression which is calculable with respect to  $c \to c'$ . we prove that  $l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}(c) \to \tilde{l}_{\beta}(c')$  by induction on  $\beta$ . trivially,  $l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_0(c) \to \tilde{l}_0(c)$  because  $\tilde{l}_0(c)$  is empty. assume we have proved

that  $l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}(c) \to \tilde{l}_{\beta}(c')$ . an element of  $\tilde{l}_{\beta + 1}(c)$  is  $\alpha_{l} \circ c$  such that  $c \in \tilde{e}_{l}(\tilde{l}_{\beta}(c), c)$ . from l-fact we get

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\frac {\langle \alpha_ {l} \circ e _ {l} (\mathbf {i} , e) \circ e _ {l} (l (e) , \mathbf {i}) , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \alpha_ {l} \circ e _ {l} (\mathbf {i} , e) \circ e _ {l} (\psi_ {l} (\alpha_ {l} \circ e _ {l} (\mathbf {i} , c)) , \mathbf {i}) , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}} \\ & {\frac {\langle \psi_ {l} (\alpha_ {l} \circ e _ {l} (\mathbf {i} , e)) , \alpha_ {l} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle l (e) , \alpha_ {l} \circ c \rangle \rightarrow \alpha_ {l} \circ c ^ {\prime}}} \end{array}
$$

since  $e_{l}(l,x)$  consists of functors declared before  $l$ , from the induction hypothesis,  $e_{l}(l(e),\mathbf{i})$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{e}_l(\tilde{l}_\beta (c),c)\to \tilde{e}_l(\tilde{l}_\beta (c'),c)$  and  $e_{l}(\mathbf{i},e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{e}_{l}(\tilde{l}_{\beta}(c^{\prime}),c)\rightarrow \tilde{e}_{l}(\tilde{l}_{\beta}(c^{\prime}),c^{\prime})$ . therefore, there is a reduction

$$
\langle e _ {l} (\mathbf {i}, e) \circ e _ {l} (l (e), \mathbf {i}), c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}
$$

such that  $c'$  is in  $\tilde{e}_l(\tilde{l}_{\beta}(c'), c')$ . from definition 4.3.5,  $\alpha_l \circ c'$  is in  $\tilde{l}_{\beta+1}(c')$ . hence,  $l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta+1}(c) \to \tilde{l}_{\beta+1}(c')$ . by induction,  $l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}(c) \to \tilde{l}_{\beta}(c')$  for any  $\beta$ , and, therefore,  $l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}(c) \to \tilde{l}(c')$ . when  $l(x)$  is contravariant, we can similarly prove that  $l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}(c') \to \tilde{l}(c)$ .

2. let  $f$  be a right object declared by

right object  $r(x)$  with  $\psi_r$  is

$$
\alpha_ {r} \colon r \to e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (r, x)
$$

end object

which is covariant, and  $e$  be a csl expression which is calculable with respect to  $c \to c'$ . we prove that  $r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{r}_{\beta}(c) \to \tilde{r}_{\beta}(c')$  by induction on  $\beta$ . from r-fact, we have

$$
\langle r (e), c \rangle \rightarrow \psi_ {r} (e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i}, e) \circ \alpha_ {r}) \circ c.
$$

trivially,  $r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{r}_0(c) \to \tilde{r}_0(c')$  because  $\tilde{r}_0(c')$  is the set of all the canonical elements of  $r$ . assume we have proved that  $r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{r}_{\beta}(c) \to \tilde{r}_{\beta}(c')$ . an element of  $\tilde{r}_{\beta + 1}(c)$  is  $\psi_r(e') \circ c'$  such that there is a reduction  $\langle \alpha_r, \psi_r(e') \circ c' \rangle \rightarrow c''$  and  $c''$  is in  $\tilde{e}_r'(r(\tilde{r}_\beta(c), c))$ . we will show that the following canonical element is in  $\tilde{r}_{\beta + 1}(c')$ :

$$
\psi_ {r} \left(e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i}, e) \circ \alpha_ {r}\right) \circ \psi_ {r} \left(e ^ {\prime}\right) \circ c ^ {\prime} \tag {*}
$$

from r-nat, we have

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\frac {\langle e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (r (e) , \mathbf {i}) \circ e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i} , e) , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime \prime}}{\langle e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (r (e) , \mathbf {i}) \circ e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i} , e) \circ \alpha_ {r} , \psi_ {r} (e ^ {\prime}) \circ c ^ {\prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime \prime}}} \\ {\hline} & {\frac {\langle e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {r} (e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i} , c) \circ \alpha_ {r}) , \mathbf {i}) , e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i} , e) \circ \alpha_ {r} , \psi_ {r} (e ^ {\prime}) \circ c ^ {\prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime \prime}}{\langle \alpha_ {r} , \psi_ {r} (e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\mathbf {i} , e) \circ \alpha_ {r}) \circ \psi_ {r} (e ^ {\prime}) \circ c ^ {\prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime \prime}}} \end{array}
$$

since  $e_r'(r, x)$  consists of functors declared before  $r$ ,  $e_r'(r(e), \mathbf{i}) \circ e_r'(\mathbf{i}, e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{e}_r'(\tilde{r}_\beta(c), c) \to \tilde{e}_r'(\tilde{r}_\beta(c'), c')$  from the induction hypothesis. therefore,  $c''$  is in  $e_r'(\tilde{r}_\beta(c'), c')$ , and from definition 4.3.5, (*) is in  $\tilde{r}_{\beta+1}(c)$ . therefore, by induction,  $r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{r}_\beta(c) \to \tilde{r}_\beta(c')$  for any  $\beta$ , so it is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{r}(c) \to \tilde{r}(c')$ . when  $r(x)$  is contravariant, we can similarly prove that  $r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{r}(c') \to \tilde{r}(c)$ .

3. if  $f$  be a right object declared by

right object  $r^{\prime}(x)$  with  $\psi_{r^{\prime}}$  is  $\alpha_{r^{\prime}}\colon \mathrm{prod}(r^{\prime},e_{r^{\prime}}(x))\to e_{r}^{\prime}(r,x)$  end object,

we can similarly prove that  $r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{r}(c) \to \tilde{r}(c')$  (or with respect to  $\tilde{r}(c') \to \tilde{r}(c)$  when  $r(x)$  is contravariant).

in the following few lemmas, we are to prove all the expressions are calculable.

lemma 4.3.10: i is calculable.

proof: we have to show that for any computable canonical element  $c$  there is a reduction of  $\langle \mathbf{i}, c \rangle \rightarrow c'$  and that  $c'$  is computable. this is immediate from the reduction rule ident and that  $c'$  is  $c$ .

lemma 4.3.11: if both  $e_1$  and  $e_2$  are calculable, so is  $e_1 \circ e_2$ .

proof: for any computable canonical element  $c'$  we have the following reduction from comp:

$$
\frac {\langle e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \qquad \langle e _ {1} , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle e _ {1} \circ e _ {2} , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}
$$

since  $e_2$  is calculable, there is a reduction for  $\langle e_2, c \rangle \rightarrow c''$  so that  $c''$  is computable. since  $e_1$  is calculable, there is a reduction for  $\langle e_1, c'' \rangle \rightarrow c'$  so that  $c'$  is computable. therefore, there is a reduction for  $\langle e_1 \circ e_2, c \rangle \rightarrow c'$  so that  $c'$  is computable.

lemma 4.3.12: for any natural transformation  $\alpha_{l}$  of a left object  $l$ ,  $\alpha_{l}$  is calculable.

proof: for any computable canonical element  $c$ , we have the following reduction by l-nat:

$$
\left<   \alpha_ {l}, c \right> \rightarrow \alpha_ {l} \circ c
$$

from definition 4.3.6,  $\alpha_{l} \circ c$  is computable. therefore,  $\alpha_{l}$  is calculable.

lemma 4.3.13: for any natural transformation  $\alpha_{r}$  of a right object  $r$ ,  $\alpha_{r}$  is calculable.

proof: let  $r$  be

right object  $r(x)$  with  $\psi_r$  is

$$
\alpha_ {r} \colon r \to e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (r, x)
$$

end object,

and  $c$  be a computable canonical element  $r(e)$ . for any  $\beta$ ,  $c$  is in  $\tilde{r}_{\beta + 1}(\omega_e)$ . from definition 4.3.5, there exists a reduction  $\langle \alpha_r, c \rangle \rightarrow c'$  such that  $c'$  is in  $\tilde{e}_r'(\tilde{r}_\beta(\omega_e), \omega_e)$ . because the result of reductions does not depend on  $\beta$ ,  $c'$  is in

$$
\tilde {e} _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\bigcap_ {\beta} \tilde {r} _ {\beta} (\omega_ {e}), \omega_ {e}) = \tilde {e} _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\tilde {r} (\omega_ {e}), \omega_ {e}) = \omega_ {e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (r (e), e)}.
$$

therefore,  $\alpha_{r}$  is calculable. we can similarly prove that for a right object

right object  $r^{\prime}(x)$  with  $\psi_{r^{\prime}}$  is

$\alpha_{r^{\prime}}\colon \operatorname {prod}(r^{\prime},e_{r^{\prime}}(x))\to e_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}(r^{\prime},x)$

end object,

$\alpha_{r^{\prime}}$  is calculable.

lemma 4.3.14: if  $e$  is calculable, so is  $\psi_l(e)$  where  $l$  is a left object and  $\psi_l$  is its factorizer.

proof: let  $l$  be

left object  $l(x)$  with  $\psi_l$  is

$\alpha_{l}\colon e_{l}(l,x)\to l$

end object,

and  $e\colon e_l(e,e')\to e$  be a calculable csl expression. we will prove that  $\psi_l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})\rightarrow \omega_{e}$  by induction on  $\beta$ . trivially, it is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_0(\omega_{e'})\to \omega_e$  because  $\tilde{l}_0(\omega_{e'})$  is empty. assume we have proved that  $\psi_l(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})\to \omega_{e}$ . an element in  $\tilde{l}_{\beta +1}(\omega_{e'})$  is  $\alpha_{l}\circ c$  for  $c$  which is in  $\tilde{e}_l(\tilde{l}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'}),\omega_{e'})$ . from l-fact, we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle e _ {l} (\psi_ {l} (e), \mathbf {i}), c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \qquad \langle e, c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime} \\ \overline {{\langle e \circ e _ {l} (\psi_ {l} (e) , {\bf i}) , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}} \\ \left\langle \psi_ {l} (e), \alpha_ {l} \circ c \right\rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime} \\ \end{array}
$$

from proposition 4.3.9 and the induction hypothesis,  $e_{l}(\psi_{l}(e),\mathbf{i})$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{e}_l(\tilde{l}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'}),\omega_{e'})\to \tilde{e}_l(\omega_e,\omega_{e'})$ , and there is a reduction  $\langle e_l(\psi_l(e),\mathbf{i}),c\rangle \rightarrow c''$ . since  $e$  is calculable, there is a reduction  $\langle e,c^{\prime \prime}\rangle \rightarrow c^{\prime}$  such that  $c^\prime$  is in  $\omega_{e}$ . therefore,  $\psi_r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta +1}(\omega_{e'})\to \omega_{e}$  and by induction it is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{l}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})\to \omega_{e}$  for any  $\beta$ . because  $\omega_{l(e')} = \tilde{l} (\omega_{e'})$  is  $\bigcup_{\beta}\tilde{l}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})$ , we have proved that  $\psi_l(e)$  is calculable.

lemma 4.3.15: if  $e$  is calculable, so is  $\psi_r(e)$  where  $r$  is a right object and  $\psi_r$  is its factorizer.

proof: let  $r$  be

right object  $r(x)$  with  $\psi_r$  is

$$
\alpha_ {r} \colon r \to e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (r, x)
$$

end object,

and  $e$  be a csl expression of type  $e \to e_r'(e, e')$ . we are to prove that  $\psi_r(e) \colon e \to r(e')$  is calculable. since  $\omega_{r(e')} = \tilde{r}(\omega_{e'})$  is  $\bigcap_{\beta} \tilde{r}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})$ , we prove that  $\psi_r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e \to \tilde{r}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})$  by induction on  $\beta$ . trivially, it is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e \to \tilde{r}_0(\omega_{e'})$  because for any  $c \in \omega_e$  we have  $\langle \psi_r(e), c \rangle \rightarrow \psi_r(e) \circ c$  and  $\tilde{r}_0(\omega_{e'})$  is the set of all the canonical elements of type  $r(e')$ . assume we have proved that  $\psi_r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e \to \tilde{r}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})$ . for any  $c \in \omega_e$  we have  $\langle \psi_r(e), c \rangle \rightarrow \psi_r(e) \circ c$ . from r-nat, we get

$$
\frac {\langle e , c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime \prime} \qquad \langle e _ {r} ^ {\prime} (\psi_ {r} (e) , \mathbf {i}) , c ^ {\prime \prime} \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}{\langle \alpha_ {r} , \psi_ {r} (e) \circ c \rangle \rightarrow c ^ {\prime}}.
$$

since  $e$  is calculable, there is a reduction  $\langle e,c\rangle \rightarrow c''$  and  $c''$  is in  $\tilde{e}_r^{\prime}(\omega_e,\omega_{e'})$ . as we assumed that  $\psi_r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e\rightarrow \tilde{r}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})$ ,  $e_r^\prime (\psi_r(e),\mathbf{i})$  is calculable with respect to  $\tilde{e}_r^\prime (\omega_e,\omega_{e'})\to \tilde{e}_r^\prime (\tilde{r}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'}),\omega_{e'})$  from proposition 4.3.9. therefore, there is a reduction  $\langle e_r'(\psi_r(e),\mathbf{i})c''\rangle \rightarrow c'$  and  $c^\prime$  is in  $\tilde{e}_r^\prime (\tilde{r}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'},\omega_{e'})$ . from definition 4.3.5,  $\psi_r(e)\circ c$  is in  $\tilde{r}_{\beta +1}(\omega_{e'})$ , so  $\psi_r(e)$  is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e\rightarrow \tilde{r}_{\beta +1}(\omega_{e'})$ , and by induction it is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e\rightarrow \tilde{r}_{\beta}(\omega_{e'})$  for any  $\beta$ . therefore, it is calculable with respect to  $\omega_e\rightarrow \tilde{r} (\omega_{e'})$ . we can similarly prove that for a right object

right object  $r^{\prime}(x)$  with  $\psi_{r^{\prime}}$  is

$\alpha_{r^{\prime}}\colon \operatorname {prod}(r^{\prime},e_{r^{\prime}}(x))\to e_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}(r^{\prime},x)$

end object,

$\psi_{r^{\prime}}(e)$  is calculable.

theorem 4.3.16: any csl expression  $e$  is calculable.

proof: this is proved by structural induction and each case follows from the lemmas, 4.3.10, 4.3.11, 4.3.12, 4.3.13, 4.3.14 and 4.3.15.  $\square$

corollary 4.3.17: any canonical element is computable.

proof: as a canonical element  $c$  is a csl expression, and therefore, from theorem 4.3.16 it is calculable. because any canonical element of the terminal object 1 is computable, specially  $\mathbf{i}$  is computable. therefore, there is a reduction  $\langle c, \mathbf{i} \rangle \rightarrow c'$  such that  $c'$  is computable. trivially,  $c'$  is  $c$  (using l-nat, r-fact and comp), so  $c$  is computable.

we now finish this section by proving the normalization theorem.

proof of normalization 4.3.2: from theorem 4.3.16, any expression  $e$  is calculable, and from corollary 4.3.17, any canonical element is computable. therefore, from the definition 4.3.7 of calculable expressions, there is a reduction  $\langle e, c \rangle \rightarrow c'$ .

# 4.4 properties of computable objects

in section 4.1, we saw that we have to restrict ourselves to computable objects (definition 4.1.6) in order to introduce our notion of computability into cdt. let us see in this section some of the properties which these particular objects enjoy.

first, we show that computable left objects are fixed points of some domain equations.

theorem 4.4.1: let  $l$  be a computable left object declared as follows.

$$
\text {l e f t o b j e c t} l \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \text {w i t h} \psi_ {l} \text {i s}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {l, 1} \colon e _ {l, 1} (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \rightarrow l
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {l, m} \colon e _ {l, m} (l, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \to l
$$

end object

then, the following isomorphism holds in any csl structure which has  $l$  and coproducts.

$$
l (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \cong \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {l, j} (l (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}), x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

where  $\sum_{j=1}^{m}$  is the  $m$ -ary coproduct. furthermore, if  $a$  is an object which satisfies

$$
a \cong \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {l, j} (a, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}),
$$

there is a unique morphism  $h$  from  $l(x_1, \ldots, x_n)$  to  $a$  such that the following

diagram commutes.

$$
\begin{array}{c c c} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {l, j} (l (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}), x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \xrightarrow {\cong} l (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \\ | & & | \\ \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {l, j} (h, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) & | & | \\ | & & | \\ \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {l, j} (a, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) & \xrightarrow {} & | \\ & \cong & a \end{array}
$$

proof: for simplicity, we prove the isomorphism in case  $l$  does not have any parameters (i.e.  $n = 0$ ). therefore, the isomorphism we prove is

$$
l \cong \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {l, j} (l).
$$

let  $f$  be a morphism

$$
\left[ \alpha_ {l, 1}, \dots , \alpha_ {l, m} \right]
$$

where  $[,\ldots ,]$  is the factorizer of  $\sum_{j = 1}^{m}$ $f$  is a morphism from  $\sum_{j = 1}^{m}e_{l,j}(l)$  to  $l$ . let  $g$  be a morphism

$$
\psi_ {l} \left(\nu_ {1} \circ e _ {l, 1} (f), \dots , \nu_ {m} \circ e _ {l, m} (f)\right)
$$

where  $\nu_{j}$  is the  $j$ -th injection of  $\sum_{j=1}^{m}$ .  $g$  is a morphism from  $l$  to  $\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{l,j}(l)$ . we show that  $f$  is the inverse of  $g$ . let us first show that  $f\circ g = \mathbf{i}$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \circ g \circ \alpha_ {l, j} \\ = f \circ \nu_ {j} \circ e _ {l, j} (f) \circ e _ {l, j} (g) \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \left(\operatorname {f r o m} \left(\operatorname {l e q} _ {j}\right)\right) \\ = \alpha_ {l, j} \circ e _ {l, j} (f) \circ e _ {l, j} (g) \\ = \alpha_ {l, j} \circ e _ {l, j} (f \circ g) \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \dots \tag {e_{l,j}(l)} \\ \end{array}
$$

from (lceq),

$$
f \circ g = \psi_ {r} (\alpha_ {l, 1}, \dots , \alpha_ {l, m}) = \mathbf {i}.
$$

the second equality holds again from (lceq). next we show that  $g \circ f = \mathbf{i}$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} g \circ f \\ = \left[ g \circ \alpha_ {l, 1}, \dots , g \circ \alpha_ {l, m} \right] \\ = \left[ \nu_ {1} \circ e _ {l, 1} (f \circ g), \dots , \nu_ {m} \circ e _ {l, m} (f \circ g) \right] \\ = \left[ \nu_ {1} \circ e _ {l, 1} (\mathbf {i}), \dots , \nu_ {m} \circ e _ {l, m} (\mathbf {i}) \right] \\ = \left[ \nu_ {1}, \dots , \nu_ {m} \right] \\ = \mathbf {i} \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore,  $l \cong \sum_{j=1}^{m} e_{l,j}(l)$ . for any object  $a$  which satisfies  $a \cong \sum_{j=1}^{m} e_{l,j}(a)$ , let  $i$  be the isomorphism from  $\sum_{j=1}^{m} e_{l,j}(a)$  to  $a$ , then the unique morphism is given by

$$
\psi_ {r} (i \circ \nu_ {1}, \dots , i \circ \nu_ {m}).
$$

it is easy to see the diagram commutes from  $(\mathrm{leq}_j)$  and the uniqueness from (lceq).

if we apply this theorem to the objects we defined in chapter 3, we get the following isomorphisms.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {n a t} \cong 1 + \mathrm {n a t} \\ \operatorname {l i s t} (x) \cong 1 + \operatorname {p r o d} (x, \operatorname {l i s t} (x)) \\ \end{array}
$$

we can see the exact correspondence to domain theory. in domain theory, the domain of natural numbers and that of lists are defined as the minimal domains which satisfies the above isomorphisms.

by duality principle, we have the dual theorem of theorem 4.4.1.

theorem 4.4.2: let  $r$  be a computable right object declared as follows.

$$
\text {r i g h t o b j e c t} r \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \text {w i t h} \psi_ {r} \text {i s}
$$

$$
\alpha_ {r, 1} \colon r \to e _ {r, 1} ^ {\prime} (r, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n})
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {r, m} \colon r \to e _ {r, m} ^ {\prime} (r, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n})
$$

end object

then, the following isomorphism holds in any csl structure which has  $r$  and products.

$$
r (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \cong \prod_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} (r (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}), x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

where  $\prod_{j=1}^{m}$  is the  $m$ -ary product. furthermore, if  $a$  is an object which satisfies

$$
a \cong \prod_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} (a, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}),
$$

there is a unique morphism  $h$  from  $a$  to  $r(x_1, \ldots, x_n)$  such that the following

diagram commutes.

$$
\begin{array}{c c c} a & \xrightarrow {\cong} & \prod_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} (a, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \\ | & & | \\ | & & | \\ h & \subset & \prod_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} (h, x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \\ | & & | \\ \downarrow & & \downarrow \\ r (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) & \xrightarrow {\cong} & \prod_ {j = 1} ^ {m} e _ {r, j} ^ {\prime} (r (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}), x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \end{array}
$$

proof: by duality.  $\square$

we can see that the infinite list defined in subsection 3.3.6 is the maximal fixed point of the following domain equation.

$$
\operatorname {i n f l i s t} (x) \cong x \times \operatorname {i n f l i s t} (x)
$$

the next theorem states that productive objects define products.

theorem 4.4.3: let  $p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{n})$  be a functor which is productive in  $y_{i}$ . then, there is a functor  $f(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{i - 1},y_{i + 1},\ldots ,y_{n})$  such that

$$
p \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right) \cong y _ {i} \times f \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right)
$$

proof: first, note that it is easy to extend the theorem to productive functorial expressions by simply applying the theorem repeatedly. let us prove the theorem by induction on the order of declaration of productive objects. let the declaration of  $p$  to be as follows.

right object  $p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{i},\ldots ,y_{n})$  with  $\psi_p$  is

$$
\alpha_ {p, 1} \colon e _ {p, 1} (p, y _ {1}, \ldots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \ldots , y _ {n}) \to e _ {p, 1} ^ {\prime} (y _ {1}, \ldots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \ldots , y _ {n})
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {p, j} \colon p \to e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} (y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n})
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {p, m} \colon e _ {p, m} (p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}) \rightarrow e _ {p, m} ^ {\prime} (y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n})
$$

end object

by induction hypothesis and from what we note at the beginning of the proof, there is a functor  $f^{\prime}(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{i - 1},y_{i + 1},\ldots ,y_{n})$  such that

$$
e _ {p, j} ^ {\prime} (y _ {1}, \ldots , y _ {n}) \cong y _ {i} \times f ^ {\prime} (y _ {1}, \ldots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \ldots , y _ {n}).
$$

since  $p$  is a computable object as well,  $e_{p,k}(p,y_1,\ldots ,y_{i - 1},y_{i + 1},\ldots ,y_n)$  is productive in  $p$ . therefore, from induction hypothesis there are functors

$$
g _ {k} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right)
$$

such that

$$
e _ {p, k} \left(p, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right) \cong p \times g _ {k} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right).
$$

using exponentials, the above definition of  $p$  is essentially the same as

right object  $p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{i},\ldots ,y_{n})$  with  $\psi_p$  is

$$
\alpha_ {p, 1} \colon p \to \exp \left(g _ {1} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right), \right.
$$

$$
e _ {p, 1} ^ {\prime} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right))
$$

···

$$
\alpha_ {p, j} \colon p \rightarrow y _ {i} \times f ^ {\prime} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right)
$$

中

$$
\alpha_ {p, m} \colon p \to \exp \left(g _ {m} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right), \right.
$$

$$
e _ {p, m} ^ {\prime} \left(y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {i - 1}, y _ {i + 1}, \dots , y _ {n}\right))
$$

end object

from theorem 4.4.2, we have

$$
p(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{n})\cong y_{i}\times f^{\prime}(y_{1},\ldots)\times \prod_{\substack{k = 1\\ k\neq j}}^{m}\exp (g_{k}(y_{1},\ldots),e^{\prime}_{p,k}(y_{1},\ldots)).
$$

$f^{\prime}(y_1,\ldots)\times \prod_{\substack{k = 1\\ k\neq j}}^{m}\exp (g_k(y_1,\ldots),e_{p,k}^{\prime}(y_1,\ldots))$  does not depend on  $y_{i}$ . we have proved the theorem.

from this theorem, we can always make the declaration of computable objects into an equivalent declaration to which we can apply theorem 4.4.2. for example, the declaration of the object for automata in subsection 3.3.7 was

right object  $\mathrm{dyn}'(i,o)$  with  $\mathrm{univ}'$  is

$$
\operatorname {n e x t} ^ {\prime}: \operatorname {p r o d} \left(\operatorname {d y n} ^ {\prime}, i\right)\rightarrow \operatorname {d y n} ^ {\prime}
$$

$$
\mathrm {o u t p u t} ^ {\prime}: \mathrm {d y n} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow o
$$

end object

to which we cannot apply theorem 4.4.2, but the above declaration is equivalent to the following one.

right object  $\mathrm{dyn}^{\prime}(i,o)$  with  $\mathrm{univ}^{\prime}$  is

$$
\operatorname {n e x t} ^ {\prime}: \operatorname {d y n} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow \exp (i, \operatorname {d y n} ^ {\prime})
$$

$$
\mathrm {o u t p u t} ^ {\prime}: \mathrm {d y n} ^ {\prime} \rightarrow o
$$

end object

then, from theorem 4.4.2 we can see  $\mathrm{dyn}'(i,o)$  as the maximal fixed point of the following domain equation.

$$
\mathrm {d y n} ^ {\prime} (i, o) \cong \exp (i, \mathrm {d y n} ^ {\prime} (i, o)) \times o
$$

# 4.5 reduction rules for full evaluation

in section 4.1 we presented a set of reduction rules which can reduce any element to a canonical element. however, the notion of canonical element (definition 4.1.3) was quite weak (or sloppy), and the canonical elements we get out of reductions sometimes not acceptable as 'canonical'. we can define a more refined notion of canonical elements.

definition 4.5.1: a canonical element is called uncondition, if it is generated by the following rule.

$$
p: := \mathbf {i} \mid \alpha_ {l, j} \circ p \mid \psi_ {r} (e _ {1}, \dots , e _ {m}) \circ p \mid \psi_ {c} (\dots , e _ {j}, \dots , p _ {k}, \dots)
$$

where  $r$  is not a unconditioned right object,  $c$  is a unconditioned object

right object  $c(x_1, \ldots, x_n)$  with  $\psi_c$  is

.

$$
\alpha_ {c, j} \colon e _ {c, j} (c, x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) \to e _ {c, j} ^ {\prime} (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

.

$$
\alpha_ {c, k} \colon c \to e _ {c, k} ^ {\prime} (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n})
$$

.

end object

and if  $e_{c,k}(c, x_1, \ldots, x_n)$  is simply  $c$  then the  $k$ th argument of  $\psi_c$  needs to be a unconditioned canonical element.

for example,

$$
